Tenaris · TEN-IT · the new Memo conveyor's card of 25 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Tenaris

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 49,635 words, set out under Richard's eleven ratings in the order of the eleven ratings page: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the eleven ratings page uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces have turned from a headwind (2024 to the first half of 2026: falling US prices, a destocking Middle East, tariff costs, Hormuz) to a moderate tailwind for late 2026 and 2027 (a 20% US price recovery working its way through the income statement, deepwater final investment decisions accelerating, rigs bottoming, Brent above $100). The tailwind is real but of mixed quality. The most durable part, offshore, is about a fifth of revenue. The largest part, US price, is a protection rent that depends on Section 232 staying in place, and part of it is a distributor refill that will end. The model should therefore show a clear 2027 recovery led by price, mix and Hormuz timing, with tonnage growth in the low single digits. 2028 should be treated as the point where outcomes widen: either price normalisation as refill ends and imports respond, or a longer deepwater-led upcycle. The single linkage to keep in view is that the Hormuz closure and the oil price are one variable with offsetting effects on Tenaris.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0252C-0542C-0756C-0952C-3560C-3950
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C529th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Competitive intensity is falling in US seamless and rising in international premium tubulars. The net change is close to flat. What matters for the model is that the rise is in the relative gap between Tenaris and its peers, not in price competition.

  • Easing, in US seamless. The 50% Section 232 tariff, anti-dumping actions (petitions in April 2026 against Austria, Taiwan and the UAE; Enforce and Protect Act findings against Chinese pipe routed through third countries) and the April 2026 move to tariffs on full customs value cut seamless imports 34% year on year. Capacity has also left the market: US Steel closed 1.1mt in 2020 and Vallourec 1.15mt in 2024 (including 685kt in Germany). Bernstein estimates global seamless is about 3% under-supplied, the first deficit since 2013.
  • Rising, in international premium and adjacent product. Vallourec has closed the profitability gap: Tubes EBITDA per tonne of $724 in the first quarter of 2026, a 21.4% EBITDA margin in the second quarter against Tenaris's 21.9%, and margins above 20% for 13 consecutive quarters. Vallourec is also winning offshore (its largest-ever line pipe order, from ExxonMobil Guyana, plus Petrobras Atapu 2 and a new Aramco agreement) and in geothermal (Fervo Energy, $800m over five years). Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund merged JESCO and AMTJ into a state-backed national champion that has taken a leading share of Aramco's supply. Chinese mills (Baosteel, TPCO) are exporting more sophisticated seamless pipe to the Middle East. Welspun is doubling its US capacity to 1.2mt by FY2027 (welded and line pipe) and won an Argentine pipeline tender against Techint. Korean welded imports keep entering the US despite the tariff.
  • The behaviour, though, is rational. Vallourec caps its tube volumes at 1.7mt and pursues "value over volume". It returns 80-100% of cash generation to shareholders, which reduces its means to fight on price. Competition is about who earns the rent, not whether a rent exists.

Over the next 6 months intensity is likely stable. The risk sits in policy: tariff removal would reverse the US easing at once. For the model, the main effect is that Tenaris's margin premium over peers should be assumed to narrow, not widen, and its pricing above the index (34% above the benchmark seamless index) should be held, not extrapolated.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4214th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Input costs are a headwind now, peaking in the second half of 2026, and turn neutral in 2027 as price increases come through. The headwind is concentrated in US welded pipe and in imported steel for the US mills.

  • Costs. US hot-rolled coil is up 20-23% since late 2025 (Midwest highs of $935/t in 2025). That squeezes welded (ERW) margins until prices are passed through. Scrap is up 20-30% over 18 months, but Morgan Stanley finds Tenaris's variable margins actually rose (+15% welded, +20% seamless) because finished prices outran it. Tariff-inflated billets and bars cost $140m to $150m a quarter gross. The mechanism to model: with inventory of about 140 days, costs incurred in the second half of 2025 reached cost of goods sold in the first half of 2026 (Redburn's March 2026 note was titled "Caveat COGS"). This explains why the EBITDA margin fell from 24.8-24.9% in the second half of 2025 to 24.0% and then 22.4% (FactSet basis) in the first half of 2026 even though US prices rose. Freight and re-routing around Hormuz lifted SG&A to $484m in the second quarter.
  • Lags. US land sales reprice about one quarter after costs rise and international contracts about two quarters after. About 43% of sales reprice off PipeLogix within one to two quarters (Redburn); independent estimates put 30-40% on public US index pricing. Steel billet and seamless prices are 80% correlated with about a 12-month lag (Bernstein).
  • Availability and concentration. Tenaris has substantial backward integration (five electric steel shops, scrap yards at Beaver Falls bought for $17.5m, an 81% recycled-content rate, Exiros procurement of more than $13bn a year shared with Ternium, Vale/Samarco/IOC pellets). But it depends on Nucor for US steel bars and coil. The coil contract expires in late 2026 and its terms are confidential, which is a renewal risk; the bar framework began in January 2026. Ternium supplied $183m of flat steel in 2025, up from $149m. JFE (13Cr alloy, extended five years) and Alleima (corrosion-resistant alloys) secure specialist inputs. Energy: two Argentine wind farms (more than $400m, 198.2MW) supply 100% of Campana's power since October 2025. European and US energy exposure remains.

Net effect: a headwind of roughly one to two EBITDA margin points through the fourth quarter of 2026, reversing in 2027 if US prices hold. The Nucor coil renewal is the one dated supply risk.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

A562nd of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The macro force biting hardest is the oil price and its geopolitical driver, the Strait of Hormuz, and it acts most strongly at industry level, on operators' capital budgets. At company level the effect is partly offset internally, because the same closure that defers Tenaris's Gulf shipments keeps Brent above $100, and that funds Tenaris's Americas and offshore customers.

  • Industry level (energy). Brent was $111 on 22 October 2026. The Fitness for Fighting digest says above $100 the energy rows stay supported; below $85 the reason for them goes. Its Energy Industry and Energy services Sector tilts are both Overweight. Offshore spending is insensitive to short-term oil moves because breakevens average about $35/bbl, but US shale budgets respond within quarters (WTI fell from about $100 to $70 in the second quarter of 2026 and returned to about $80, per Morgan Stanley).
  • Sector level (energy services). The scarcity theme (no new capacity being built anywhere) is the leading story, and energy services have run: offshore contractors are up 65% over twelve months. Tenaris is up 69-74% while its tubular peers are up only 8-15%.
  • Company level. Currency is a mild headwind in a weak-dollar world. Tenaris reports in dollars and over 80% of sales are priced in dollars, but labour costs in euros, Mexican pesos and Argentine pesos mean a 5% fall in the dollar cuts operating income by about $70m ($73m drag in FY2024). The FX sensitivity on the balance sheet is small (1% move = $1.2m pre-tax). Peso devaluations cause non-cash deferred tax charges ($346.6m in 2023). Interest rates help: net cash of about $3.5bn earns financial income (a $83m gain in the first half of 2026). Italian sovereign stress affects only the listing, not revenue; the Country IT Underweight tilt should not be read across to Tenaris's earnings ("a country tilt applies to revenue, not to the ticker suffix").
  • Market regime. The regime is risk-off (Momentum tilt Underweight, defensives leading cyclicals), and that is a headwind to the share price rather than to earnings.

The skew comes from the Hormuz-oil link. Reopening returns about $130m a quarter of deferred premium revenue within 70 to 90 days, but could cut Brent sharply and with it 2027 US shale budgets. Prolonged closure keeps oil high but erodes Gulf shipments and inflates freight. The model should run the two Hormuz states as linked scenarios, not as independent inputs.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–52

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Four other external dynamics matter. Their net effect is roughly neutral, with the skew to the upside coming from Hormuz.

  • The two-sided Hormuz effect (positive optionality). Management's second-half guidance assumes the Strait stays closed through 2026. So any reopening is upside not in guidance: deferred shipments to Kuwait, Iraq and Qatar can be invoiced within 70 to 90 days. In the second quarter, revenue fell $133m quarter on quarter and EBITDA fell $86m, which implies the lost revenue carried about 65% EBITDA drop-through including extra logistics cost. That is a measure of the quarterly earnings at stake. The risk is that deferral becomes cancellation. File 07 sets the warning line at cumulative deferrals above $200m by year-end, or third-quarter Middle East revenue failing to rise 15-20% sequentially.
  • Steel-industry consolidation (negative). Nippon Steel completed its $14.9bn acquisition of US Steel, creating a well-funded integrated competitor, although tubulars are only 5-6% of the merged group's revenue. ArcelorMittal sold 10% of Vallourec in May 2026 (keeping 17.3%), removing takeover speculation and leaving Vallourec focused on its own operations.
  • Customer finance (positive). Pemex has been recapitalised and Tenaris's net credit exposure to it was cut to 7% by early 2025. Petrobras suppliers are using non-recourse factoring.
  • Peer read-across into the share price (negative near term). Vallourec's third quarter may be "the last stop" of the Hormuz drag (Bernstein, 12 October), and Vallourec prints before Tenaris. Saipem and Subsea 7's merger decision is due by November 2026 and sets the tone for the services group.

For the model: build Hormuz as an explicit timing variable, with deferred revenue recovered over one to two quarters after reopening.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C+606th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The threat from new entrants is moderate overall and low in Tenaris's core premium OCTG.

  • From adjacent markets (steel majors). Nippon Steel and US Steel, and ArcelorMittal's stake in Vallourec (bought partly for exposure to hydrogen and carbon-capture tubes). Moderate threat: they have the capital, but the premium-connection technology and qualifications take years. Steelmakers rarely move downstream into premium threading (BF digest).
  • Standard entrants. Chinese seamless exporters (copied connection technology; 90% of the Chinese domestic market), Indian mills (Welspun in US welded and line pipe; Maharashtra Seamless and Kirloskar adding premium threading), Southeast Asian capacity (Sunindo), and the Saudi national champion. A moderate threat in commodity and in local-content markets (Argentina, Saudi Arabia, Asia). In Western premium the threat is low, because Asian and CIS suppliers making more than 10mt a year "lack access to premium connections and technical certifications".
  • Orthogonal or innovative entrants. Digital oilfield platforms and service companies. Low threat: they prefer to partner, and Tenaris's own digital services (PipeTracer, WISer, remote monitoring) are ahead.

For the model, apply the entrant threat to pricing in Argentine pipeline tenders, Saudi OCTG share and US welded. Leave premium seamless OCTG share unchanged.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C-5011th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Suppliers are mostly a support because of Tenaris's backward integration and group procurement scale. The US steel supply is a material and growing source of risk.

  • Support. Exiros (50/50 with Ternium) handles more than $13bn of purchases a year across 19 countries, pays invoices in an average of 36 days and has purchasing power. Five electric steel shops, the Beaver Falls scrap yard and an 81% recycled-metal rate give supply security. Pellet supply for Siderca's DRI plant comes under contract from Vale, Samarco and IOC (pellet use fell 25% to 721kt in 2025 as the metal mix was optimised). JFE (13Cr, extended five years) and Alleima (corrosion-resistant alloys) cover speciality alloys. Captive power comes from the Argentine wind farms and the Techgen 197MW contract to 2041. Ternium supplies bars and flat steel.
  • Risk. In the US, Tenaris must buy about 1.14mt of steel externally (Bernstein) and depends on Nucor: a steel-bar framework from January 2026 with confidential pricing formulas and monthly surcharges, and a coil contract expiring in late 2026. Nucor has pricing power in a tariff-protected domestic steel market. Hot-rolled coil is up 20-23%. Related-party purchases (Ternium $183m in 2025, Techgen energy $67m, Tenova furnaces $28m in 2024) are modest. They carry governance rather than cost risk, given only 40% board independence.

For the model: treat US input costs as a separate line from international costs. Assume imported-billet tariff costs fall as Nucor and domestic supply ramp, offset by Nucor's pricing. Flag the late-2026 coil renewal as a dated cost event.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0162C-0357C-0456C-0864C-3260C-3755C-4350
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D6214th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Demand is turning up from a cyclical trough. The change is mostly in value (price and mix), not in global tonnage. Only one part of it, offshore deepwater, has two or more years of visibility. Global OCTG consumption is roughly flat at about 15 to 16 million tonnes (15.5mt in 2023, about 16mt in 2024, "stable" through 2025; management guides about 15mt for 2026). Tenaris's volume gains will therefore come from mix and share, not from a growing market. The parts behave differently and should be modelled separately:

  • Offshore deepwater (about 20% of revenue): a cyclical upswing with structural depth, visible to 2028. Offshore capex rises about 19% from roughly $220bn in 2025 to $260bn in 2029 (Bernstein). Saipem's research shows a 30% step-up in 2027, to $149bn from $115bn, with 71% of it greenfield. Supporting evidence: projects already past final investment decision with two-to-three-year delivery schedules; Petrobras's $78bn exploration and production plan ($43bn of it pre-salt) with Búzios 9 and 11; ExxonMobil's ten-year Stabroek supply contract; TotalEnergies standardising all 36 Suriname wells on Tenaris's Dopeless connections; Sakarya in the Black Sea. This is the only leg that meets the two-years-plus test.
  • US onshore (the largest exposure; North America is 46-49% of sales): a cyclical trough that has passed, with one to two quarters of visibility. The rig count fell about 7% year on year, then rose about 10% this year towards an expected roughly 600 by year-end, with private and independent operators adding about 50 rigs. Pipe per rig rises 3-4% a year, and US footage drilled is forecast up 40% by 2030 (Morgan Stanley). Seamless pricing is up 16% year to date (welded up 21%) from a trough of about $2,110/t in August 2024. The Wisdom Library's "Use, Refill or Price" test applies. The US recovery is mostly price (tariffs cut seamless imports 34%) and refill (distributor stocks below the five-year average). Actual use is growing only modestly. Price lasts as long as the tariffs do, and refill ends once distributor shelves are full, probably during 2027.
  • Middle East: demand is intact but delivery is blocked. Saudi Arabia and the UAE are stable, and ADNOC's $1.9bn contract was extended by two years. Deliveries to Iraq, Kuwait and Qatar are deferred ($130m in the second quarter) because the Strait of Hormuz has been closed since March 2026.
  • Latin American onshore: structurally weak except Argentina. Colombia's rig count fell from 50 in 2022 to 28; the Ecuador threading mill is idle; Latin American rigs fell 15% in 2025. Argentina's Vaca Muerta is the exception: it needs 3-4 times more OCTG per well (about 80kt a year), and Tenaris is supplying the Vaca Muerta Sur and Duplicar Norte pipelines.

Against the last 12 to 18 months the direction has reversed. Then, US prices were falling to the trough, rigs were declining and Aramco was running down stocks. Now prices are about 20% off the trough, rigs have bottomed and offshore is accelerating. Brent at $111 (22 October) funds operator budgets. For US shale, though, capital discipline limits how much higher prices turn into rigs, and Morgan Stanley's 2026 outlook was titled "Navigating an Oil Surplus". The consequence for the model: grow revenue mainly through price and mix, with modest tonnage growth (low single digits a year) from offshore and Argentina. Do not assume a US volume boom.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D5713th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Over the four-year horizon the disruption forces are mostly neutral or favourable for Tenaris. The energy-transition threat to oil-directed drilling is being deferred: Brent is above $100, offshore capex is growing, and deepwater breakevens are about $35/bbl. Three forces matter:

  1. Drilling efficiency, which helps. Fewer rigs drill longer laterals, so more pipe is used per rig (+3-4% a year), and global footage drilled is forecast to grow 40% over five years against 20% for well capex. Efficiency that hurts rig-count-linked service providers is a tailwind for tubulars.
  2. New-energy tubulars, positive but immaterial before 2029 and led by Vallourec. Bernstein estimates geothermal well counts grow 2.6 times by 2030, and puts the 2030 market at about 430kt for carbon capture and about 500kt for geothermal, together roughly 6% of current OCTG tonnage. Tenaris's geothermal revenue is about $145m in FY2027 (about 1% of sales) against Vallourec's projected 6%. Tenaris has a track record of more than 500 geothermal wells without a connection failure, a Green Therma partnership and its THera hydrogen materials, but Vallourec holds the visible anchor contracts (Fervo, XGS Energy for Meta's data centres). This is optionality, not a driver of the model.
  3. Technology copying, a slow negative for the premium-connection moat. Chinese mills replicated premium connections and now hold 90% of their own domestic oil pipe market, and insiders expect them to target Saudi Arabia and Qatar. Separately, the digital services that tie customers in are strengthening Tenaris's position: PipeTracer, the WISer well-integrity platform (with the AllTorque acquisition), and a Houston remote monitoring centre connected to 70 rigs.

The net effect for the next four years is mildly positive, because pipe per well rises regardless of the rig count and the substitution risk is far off. The long-run terminal-value risk is real but outside the model horizon.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C569th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Policy change is large and under way, and on balance it currently helps Tenaris. But it helps in a way that is fragile, and it acts in both directions on the same income statement.

  • US Section 232 at 50% (since June 2025) is both Tenaris's main price support and its main cost penalty. On the price side, imports are down 34%, the seamless index is up about 20% from the trough, and the full-customs-value rule since April 2026 adds support. On the cost side, Tenaris imports about 28% of its North American raw steel billets (Bernstein: it buys 1.14mt of steel externally for its US mills, while Vallourec is 100% self-sufficient at Youngstown). That costs $140m to $150m a quarter. Redburn models the tariffs reducing EBITDA margin by 360 to 480 basis points over 2026-27 and calls it a 5-6% structural drag on processing margins; Bernstein says a 4% price rise neutralises it. Mitigations are under way: a Nucor Memphis steel-bar framework from January 2026, record output at Bay City, the Koppel finishing restart, scrap yards, a Canadian retroactive exemption on imported bars, and the CAD 306m Sault Ste. Marie modernisation backed by CAD 76.2m of federal money.
  • Favourable rulings are releasing cash. On 16 July 2026 the Department of Commerce ruled preliminarily that mechanical pipe is outside the anti-dumping scope, which removes the $49.6m Customs and Border Protection claim. Anti-dumping deposit rates were cut from 78.30% to 6.76% for Argentina and to a preliminary 1.62% for Mexico, so past deposits are recoverable once rulings are final. Canada's 26.2% anti-dumping duty on Mexican OCTG excludes Tenaris's US-made seamless pipe.
  • Elsewhere, policy is mixed. The EU's New Trade Tool (July 2026) cut duty-free quotas by 47% and raised the out-of-quota tariff to 50%. That protects Tenaris's European mills but restricts its exports into Europe. CBAM is live from 2026, and Tenaris's low carbon intensity (1.17t CO2 per tonne of steel) is an advantage under it. In Argentina, the Milei reforms (the RIGI investment regime) help Vaca Muerta activity but strip away domestic protection: Tenaris lost a pipeline tender to India's Welspun, with officials saying its bid was 40% dearer, and currency controls still delay dividends. Mexico is supporting Pemex. Local-content rules favour local producers: Vallourec in Brazil, the Saudi champion in Saudi Arabia.

Bearing on the model: keep the US price premium and the tariff cost together. A trade deal that reverses Section 232 is the single largest policy risk, because the price support would go faster than the cost would come off. The skew is that policy currently pays Tenaris more than it costs it, but this is a political rent, not a moat.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

D6414th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Yes. Three industry drivers are accelerating demand for Tenaris's highest-margin products, and the acceleration is strongest in 2027. They lift value per well more than total tonnage.

  1. Deepwater final investment decisions. Offshore capex steps up about 30% in 2027 (Saipem research). Brazil's pre-salt consumption of premium connections has nearly doubled since 2023. Guyana (ExxonMobil's 35-well campaign), Suriname (GranMorgu, whose service base began receiving casing and tubing in June 2026 and starts Rig Direct services in the second half of 2026) and Nigeria (Shell's Bonga) add to it. Premium tubulars sell for "up to seven times" API-grade product (Bernstein).
  2. Pipe intensity per well. US OCTG intensity has risen about 5% a year since 2015, and 3-4% a year is assumed going forward (Redburn). Global footage drilled is forecast up 40% by 2030, double the growth in well capex (Morgan Stanley).
  3. Vaca Muerta. Argentine shale output grows by about 60,000 barrels a day in 2026 and is expected to exceed 1.6 million barrels of oil equivalent a day by 2035 (Wood Mackenzie). Tenaris adds a third fracturing fleet ($110m) for its Argentine services business and supplies the pipelines.

The acceleration is real but bounded. Offshore is about 20% of revenue, and the US index can move more in one quarter than the structural drivers do in a year. The model should carry offshore and Argentina as the volume growth legs (mid to high single digits a year) and US onshore as a price leg.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D6014th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Customer demand supports a moderate recovery thesis. The support is solid in offshore and NOC contracts, moderate and oil-dependent in US land, and temporarily blocked in the Upper Gulf.

  • Offshore customers. ExxonMobil, TotalEnergies, Petrobras and Shell are committing capital with "impeccable execution opportunities" (Podskubka). Contracts are multi-year (Petrobras conductor and surface casing, ExxonMobil ten-year supply, ADNOC extended two years, using Rig Direct to supply half of Abu Dhabi's needs). Fragility is low.
  • US operators. Private and independent operators are adding rigs, distributor inventories are below average, and buying has accelerated. But this is capital-disciplined shale. A drop in WTI towards $60-65 would cut budgets within quarters. The rule of thumb in file 07 is that 550 rigs for two quarters with WTI below $65 is the point where the onshore thesis breaks. Fragility is medium-high.
  • Middle East. The demand intent is intact: no cancellations reported, Saudi and UAE business as usual. Delivery is blocked for the northern Gulf. Fragility is medium: it is a timing issue with a tail risk of share loss to local or alternative suppliers.
  • Latin American onshore. Weak (Colombia, Ecuador); Argentina is the exception.

Customer concentration is low (the largest customer is about 10% of sales) and government-related customers are about 23% of first-half 2026 sales, down from 26%. Customer behaviour does not add hidden fragility.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C559th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance regulators and politicians are helping Tenaris now, chiefly through US and EU trade protection and Canadian industrial support. The position is fragile, because the biggest help (Section 232) is a political decision that could be bargained away.

  • Help. US tariffs at 50% on full customs value. Anti-dumping actions (including petitions where imports from Austria, Taiwan and the UAE rose 74% from 2023 to 2025, and Enforce and Protect Act findings on transhipped Chinese pipe). Favourable Commerce Department rulings (mechanical pipe out of scope; lower deposit rates for Argentina and Mexico). Canadian co-investment (about CAD 300m with federal and provincial money), a retroactive exemption on imported bars, and a Canadian anti-dumping finding that excluded Tenaris's US-origin seamless. EU quota protection for EU-made product (Dalmine, Artrom). CBAM favours low-carbon producers. Mexico's support for Pemex. Argentina's RIGI regime for Vaca Muerta.
  • Hurt. The tariff cost on imported billets and bars ($140m to $150m a quarter). Argentina's liberalisation exposes the domestic market (Welspun) and FX controls remain. Saudi and Brazilian local content favours rivals (the Saudi champion, Vallourec in Brazil). EU quotas restrict exports into Europe. Canadian producers (Interpro, Welded Tube of Canada) filed dumping complaints against Tenaris. In Brazil, CGU proceedings against Confab carry possible contracting bans.
  • Fragility. High for US tariffs. The Risks report's re-run trigger is unrecovered tariff cost above $150m a quarter. Medium for Argentine politics: Rocca's clashes with Milei came before the lost tender.

For the model: hold tariff-supported US pricing through 2027 in the base case, with a policy-reversal downside scenario that removes price faster than cost.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5021st of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Tenaris already holds about a third of a mature market, so the addressable market allows mid-term growth of only about 2-5% a year in value through the cycle. Within that, a cyclical upswing in price can add a step-change for one or two years. This is not a share-gain or market-expansion growth story.

  • Size. Oil and gas tubes are about $35bn (Bernstein), against Tenaris's revenue of about $12bn. Global OCTG is about 15-16mt a year, roughly flat since 2023.
  • Growth drivers. Pipe per rig +3-4% a year. Offshore capex growth (+19% from 2025 to 2029). Footage drilled +40% over five years. Mix towards premium. New energy by 2030: carbon capture about 430kt and geothermal about 500kt, together about 6% of current OCTG tonnage. Bernstein's longer-range figures are a cumulative $45bn geothermal tube market from 2026 to 2035, and up to $2bn of extra annual sales for Tenaris by 2035 if geothermal capacity reaches 165GW.
  • Limits. Global steel consumption grew only 18% in 15 years. Latin American onshore is structurally weak. Local champions cap share in protected NOC markets.

For the model: revenue growth through the cycle of low-to-mid single digits a year. Consensus FY2025 to FY2028 revenue growth of +3.4% a year is consistent with this. Higher growth in FY2027 is cyclical price recovery, not market expansion.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0663C-1060
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C6310th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: Tenaris is at the end of a trough lasting about seven quarters and at the start of a modest, price-led upturn. The visible turning point is the fourth quarter of 2026.

  • Where it has been. Revenue peaked at $14,869m in FY2023 (EBITDA margin 32.4%) and fell to $12,524m in FY2024 and $11,981m in FY2025. Quarterly revenue has been flat at $2.85bn to $3.10bn since the third quarter of 2024, with a low of $2,845m in the fourth quarter of 2024. Margins compressed from 28.6% (FactSet basis, fourth quarter 2023) to about 24-25% in 2024-25, then to 22.4% in the second quarter of 2026 (21.9% company basis) on tariffs, freight and the Hormuz deferral.
  • Signs of the turn. US seamless prices are up 16% year to date, and those increases reach the income statement with a one-to-two-quarter lag, so in the fourth quarter of 2026 and the first quarter of 2027. Management guides fourth-quarter volume above 1mt, against 946kt in the second quarter. US rigs are bottoming. The offshore backlog accelerates into 2027. Consensus revenue growth is -0.7% for FY2026, +6.1% for FY2027 and +4.8% for FY2028; EBITDA growth is -5.2%, +11.8% and +5.9%.
  • How strong the turn is. Moderate. Brokers' FY2027 EBITDA ranges from $2,865m (Oddo) to $3,556m (Morgan Stanley), and Redburn's figure is about $3.6bn (a 25.6% margin on $14.2bn of revenue). The high cases need US prices to hold and Hormuz to reopen. The low cases assume a short refill-and-tariff upturn that fades by 2028; consensus FY2029 EPS falls 6%, but only one or two brokers cover that year.

For the model: treat FY2026 as the trough year (EBITDA about $2.7bn to $2.8bn), FY2027 as the recovery year, and FY2028 as the year where the debate lies. The third-quarter print on 4 November will be seasonally weak by guidance (European maintenance, mix), so the turn shows in the fourth-quarter guide, not in third-quarter numbers.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C-6010th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: The external picture is a moderate net tailwind, improving from the low point in the first half of 2026. It is strongest in 2027. The tailwinds are mostly cyclical and political rather than structural, so the 2028 picture is less certain than the 2027 one.

Each theme in turn:

  1. "Nobody is building capacity, so owners of existing assets have pricing power" (the Industry and Sector lead theme; Wisdom Library: customers' capital discipline is the asset owners' scarcity). Tailwind, strong. Seamless is about 3% under-supplied, a hot-rolling mill takes years and heavy capital to build, and capacity has left the market (US Steel, Vallourec). The window lasts as long as new mill build times; the Asian welded producers are the release valve.
  2. Trade protection. Tailwind on price and headwind on cost: net positive now, highly fragile. It is the Sector digest's named two-way driver.
  3. The deepwater cycle. Tailwind, strong and visible to 2028.
  4. US shale's trough and recovery. Mild tailwind; depends on oil and on operators' discipline.
  5. The Strait of Hormuz. Headwind now (deferred revenue, freight), with dated optionality on reopening; partly hedged through the oil price.
  6. Input-cost inflation. Headwind, peaking now and fading in 2027.
  7. Competitive convergence (Vallourec). Headwind to Tenaris's relative premium, not to industry pricing.
  8. Energy transition. Neutral over four years.

By level: at value-chain level, suppliers (steel costs, tariffs) are a headwind and customers (offshore spending, NOC contracts) a tailwind. At industry level, structure (an oligopoly, capacity discipline) is a tailwind. At sector level, the scarcity theme is a tailwind for earnings, but the share price has already been re-rated and the regime is risk-off.

Overall: 2027 should be a better external year than 2026, consistent with consensus EBITDA +11.8%. The largest single variable is the persistence of US pricing, which rests on tariffs. The cone of outcomes for 2028 is wide.

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Internally, Tenaris is changing its people and its form of cash return, not its strategy or its economics. The CEO and CFO successions were planned, internal and orderly, and preserve a capable industrial operator with a strong execution record, but they do not create a new catalyst. Strategy has adapted competently to tariffs and to the offshore cycle. The capital-allocation pivot has reduced total distributions and removed share-count support from EPS, and it is unexplained; the most consistent reading is a free-float constraint, alongside the controlling family's preference for cash dividends. Working-capital discipline has slipped. The second set should model internal change as roughly neutral to EPS: no buyback in the base case, a flat share count, North American fixed costs rising slightly, and a dated 2027 option on returning surplus cash. The one internal variable with large cash effect is working capital, about $0.4bn to $0.5bn between the current 143 days and the historical 128 days.

Internal risks

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

F5521st of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Standards are mixed: improving in cost discipline and governance hygiene, deteriorating in working-capital discipline.

  • Improving. Headcount fell by 3,260 in 2024 and by about 1,000 in 2025 (mainly shop floor), while professional resignations fell to a ten-year low of 3.6%. Half of a $200m annual cost-reduction programme was delivered within four quarters. Automation advanced: Bay City is the most automated mill, TuboCaribe has an automated inspection line, and Bernstein estimates AI and automation can cut cycle times by up to 30%. Wind farms remove grid power costs in Argentina. Governance hygiene improved: internal controls were rated effective at end-2025, the auditor was replaced by competitive tender, there is an SEC-aligned clawback, and the audit committee is fully independent with a designated financial expert.
  • Deteriorating. Operating working capital rose to 143 days in June 2026 from 128 days a year earlier, against a historical aim of below 130 days and Vallourec's 86 days. Inventories were $3,716m in mid-2026 and higher stock consumed $124m of cash in the first half. Net working capital rose from 37.3% of trailing revenue (third quarter 2024) to 39.9% (second quarter 2026), about $300m. Part of this is a deliberate buffer against Hormuz and part is the Rig Direct model, but former operations managers interviewed by expert networks describe poor forecasting and slow hand-offs between stages despite the OMP planning software.
  • Execution. Margins held within the corridor through a 17% revenue decline. Bay City hit records. Redburn counts beats on consensus EPS in 18 of the last 21 quarters.

For the model, the working-capital slip is the standard to watch. If days return from 143 towards 128, about $500m of cash is released. If they stay, the FY2026 consensus free cash flow of $1,320m is plausible; if they worsen as volumes rise, it is not.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

B403rd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The negatives are moderate and mostly about capital allocation and governance rather than operations. One operational negative, rising fixed costs from regionalisation, needs modelling.

  1. The buyback pause (see C-19): lower EPS support, a market that feels let down, and an unexplained change in capital policy.
  2. Working-capital deterioration (143 days; $124m of extra inventory cash in the first half of 2026).
  3. Regionalisation raises the fixed-cost base in North America (Sault Ste. Marie, Koppel, extra Bay City shifts). If US rigs fall below about 540-550, these mills are underused. File 07 calls this a possible "structural margin trap".
  4. Incentives. CEO pay is all cash ($3m salary plus a $7m bonus, $10m in total for 2025, up from $9m in 2024), with no equity, no financial targets in the variable pay, and insider holdings of 0.08-0.09%. The new CEO halved a negligible holding in March 2026.
  5. Governance. Four of ten directors are independent, there are no nomination or compensation committees, and several family directors have sat for more than 23 years.
  6. Litigation and regulatory tail. CSN's claim over the 2012 Usiminas purchase: courts have established the indemnity obligation, and a $171m provision was taken in the second quarter of 2024 with a $67m partial reversal in the fourth quarter of 2024. Confab faces BRL 123.9m (Veracel) and BRL 322m civil claims, possible public-contract bans, and CGU proceedings opened in March 2024. Global Tubing is challenging the BlueCoil patents on antitrust grounds.
  7. Labour friction in Argentina (SIAT) and wage indexation risk in Argentina and Brazil; 71% of the workforce is unionised.
  8. Hedging losses in three consecutive years ($13m in 2024, $26m in 2025, $25m in the first half of 2026).
  9. Auditor rotation three times in a few years: PwC, then EY, then Forvis Mazars, the last by competitive tender.

None of these alone changes the earnings model. Items 1, 2 and 3 change EPS, cash and operating leverage and must be modelled.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

B624th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The culture is strongly congruent on the operating side: engineering excellence, customer service, cost discipline, long-term thinking and safety reinforce one another. It is incongruent on the shareholder side, where incentives and ownership do not tie management to share-price returns.

On the operating side, the Techint industrial culture (founded by Agostino Rocca) values engineering, staying power through cycles and margin over volume. That fits the strategy (Rig Direct, premium connections, integration) and the evidence of execution (Toyota's quality award, more than 500 geothermal wells without failure, Bay City records). The CEO holds quarterly town halls with open questions. Survey favourability is 81% on the shop floor against 76% for professionals. Compliance and safety are treated as "non-negotiable". On the shareholder side, CEO pay is entirely cash, and the variable part rests on safety and decarbonisation with no quantified financial targets. Other senior managers are paid on book equity value, not the share price. Insiders hold 0.08-0.09%. The congruence is with the controlling family's long-term industrial goals rather than with minority shareholders' returns. This matters for capital allocation (C-19, C-63) more than for operations.

Group judgement

Risks

C+457th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Tenaris's fundamental risk profile is moderate and well cushioned. There is no financial leverage, a 20% margin floor, low customer concentration and diversified geography. The core growth engine has stopped falling and its inputs are accelerating. Two linked risks dominate: US trade policy, because the most profitable part of the upcycle is a tariff rent, and the oil price, which is tied to the Strait of Hormuz in a way that partly hedges Tenaris's own Gulf deferrals. Legal, Argentine and supply risks are real but one-off or moderate in size. The sharper near-term risk is perception: the stock has re-rated to the top decile of its five-year multiple range, it has decoupled from its cohort, and the regime has turned against high-momentum cyclicals. A fall to fair trough would be about 35-45%. The second set should carry a downside scenario combining a tariff reversal with sub-$65 WTI, rather than treating them separately, and should note that the bear case is already quantified by several brokers, so it is less likely to surprise the market as an unmodelled case would.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C+456th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Seven sudden, high-impact risks exist. Two are large enough to break the model: a US tariff reversal, and an oil-price collapse triggered by a Hormuz reopening together with an oil surplus. Both are low-to-medium probability over 18 months. The others are smaller, or one-off. My estimates of plausibility and size are judgements, with the inputs named.

  1. Section 232 reversal or a trade deal (plausibility low-to-medium; the US midterm elections are on 3 November 2026 and trade policy is negotiable). US prices could fall 15-25% as imports return, set against $560m to $600m a year of tariff cost removed. The net effect is likely negative, because the price falls faster than the cost (inventory lag). EBITDA would fall by roughly $300m to $700m, about 10-25%. This is the primary downside named by several brokers and the Risks report.
  2. Oil collapse below $60 Brent (plausibility low-to-medium; Morgan Stanley's 2026 outlook was "Navigating an Oil Surplus"; Brent is $111 partly because of Hormuz). US rigs could fall 15-25%, below the 540-550 trigger, and US prices towards $2,000/t (Jefferies' downside). EBITDA could fall towards $2.0bn to $2.3bn, or $1.5bn in Morgan Stanley's downcycle case. This is the biggest risk, and it is linked to risk 3.
  3. A Hormuz outcome either way. Prolonged closure risks deferral turning into cancellation and NOCs switching to the Saudi champion or Chinese suppliers (about $0.5bn of revenue a year at risk). Reopening risks the oil price falling (see risk 2).
  4. Legal one-offs (plausibility medium, magnitude moderate). The CSN/Usiminas indemnity, where courts have established the obligation and a $171m charge was taken in 2024. Confab's BRL 322m and BRL 123.9m claims and a possible Brazilian public-contract ban. CGU proceedings. The Global Tubing patent challenge. Charges of $100m to $300m are possible.
  5. An Argentine shock (medium plausibility, moderate magnitude). Devaluation (the peso was 1,482 per dollar in mid-2026; Morgan Stanley expects 1,982 by end-2027) brings deferred-tax charges, repatriation blocks and Vaca Muerta delays. Argentina was about 19% of 2024 sales.
  6. Price war in North America from Nippon Steel and US Steel (low plausibility).
  7. An operational accident or shutdown at Bay City or a key mill (low plausibility, high local impact), and a failed Nucor renewal (medium plausibility, low-to-moderate impact).

A method caution. The Risks report's ACH matrix chose the "FALSE FRIEND" profile because it had the fewest hard inconsistencies (two). The Wisdom Library warns that "the vaguest explanation wins a fewest-contradictions contest". The false-friend claims are weakly supported by the data: dividends are not masking erosion of free cash flow (conversion before dividends was 108% in FY2025 and net cash rose in the first half of 2026), and volume is not decaying in North America. Treat that verdict as a prompt, not a finding.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F3811th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Highly concentrated. Two external drivers, the US seamless price index and the timing of the Strait of Hormuz reopening, generate about 85% of the forecast's step-up from FY2026 to FY2027 and most of its reversal in 2028-29; the policy that underwrites the first of them (Section 232 and the anti-dumping orders) is a third variable that can move both the price and the cost line at once. The diffuse drivers that do exist (offshore volume, Vaca Muerta, pipe intensity, Artrom, cost actions) are steady and well evidenced, but each is small against a price lever that moves EBITDA by about 4% for every 1% on the group's average price. Concentration of this kind cuts both ways for predictability: it makes the forecast fragile to two variables, but both variables are observable monthly and one of them (the index) is visible one to two quarters before it reaches the income statement.

What is different about the forecast years against 2020-25. Revenue in a $12.2-13.5bn band against $5.1-14.9bn; an EBITDA margin band of 22.0-24.9% against 12.0-32.4%; EPS of about $1.85-2.25 against -$0.54 to $3.32; no share-count reduction against 14% in three years; more non-operating income (about 10% of pre-tax profit against 3-6% in 2024-25); and a dividend that absorbs most of free cash flow. Every one of these differences traces to a small number of causes.

Decomposition of the FY2027 step-up (my revised base, EBITDA +$565m from $2,789m to about $3,344m; the second set's bridge of +$462m has the same proportions):

Driver Contribution to the EBITDA change Nature Observable where
US index drop-through (30-43% of sales, +34% premium, one-to-two-quarter lag) about +$360m (about 64%) External; policy and restocking PipeLogix monthly; Census imports monthly; distributor months of supply
Gulf return from Q3 2027 (deferred $120-130m a quarter at 55-65% drop-through, plus freight) about +$110m (about 20%) External; geopolitical timing IMF PortWatch transits; company deferral commentary
Other volume (offshore +8%, Vaca Muerta, US intensity) about +$100m (about 18%) Diffuse; contracted or structural Rig count weekly; offshore FIDs; Petrobras and ExxonMobil schedules
Net tariff mitigation about +$80m (about 14%) Internal execution; policy-dependent Quarterly call commentary on the tariff run-rate
Artrom consolidation about +$15m (about 3%) Internal Closing in Q4 2026
Fixed-cost inflation and regionalisation about -$80m (-14%) Internal, structural Headcount and SG&A per quarter
Input-cost lag (scrap, coil, the Nucor Hickman renewal) about -$20m (-4%) External; cost HRC and scrap indices

The price and Gulf legs together are about 83% of the net step-up. Strip them out and the diffuse drivers add about $100-200m a year, or 1-1.5 margin points, which is the business's organic rate of improvement.

Decomposition of the 2028-29 reversal. The base case's EBITDA falls from about $3.34bn (2027) to about $2.85bn (2029) in my revised path. About 80% of that fall is price (a 5-6% fall in realised US prices as restocking ends and imports and new mills respond, at a decremental margin of about 70%), about 10% is the end of the Gulf catch-up, and about 10% is the fixed-cost ratchet from hiring in the upturn. Again one variable dominates.

Why the concentration is more extreme than the revenue mix suggests. The US index-linked share of sales is 30-43%, but it carries most of the variance because international contract prices are formula-based and "highly stable", offshore pricing is set by two-to-three-year qualifications, and the Gulf is a timing item rather than a price item. The historical record agrees: Redburn attributes Tenaris's earnings dispersion to a single variable, North American land, and the FY2024 price fall produced a 72% decremental margin.

Below the operating line, the drivers are noisy rather than directional. Net financial income and associates were about 15% of pre-tax income in the first half of 2026 (financial result $82.5m, associates $81.3m). A 100 basis-point move in dollar rates is about $0.03 of EPS; Ternium's earnings and dividend are cyclical and its board cut the 2025 payout from $2.70 to $2.20 per ADS in April 2026; the effective tax rate was about 15% in the first half of 2026 against 21% assumed for 2027 because of Argentine inflation adjustments and currency effects, and the half-year report says a sharp peso devaluation would produce a non-cash deferred-tax charge. Brazilian legal items (the CSN indemnity of about BRL 548m, roughly $90m, suspended pending the Supreme Federal Tribunal; Petrobras civil claims of BRL 208.6m; a possible CGU contracting ban) are tail items. Together these can move a year's EPS by $0.08-0.10 either way without any change in the operating story.

Concentration at peers, for calibration. Vallourec faces the same two drivers plus a higher Middle East exposure (41% of its revenue against Tenaris's 12% of EBITDA) and no welded exposure; its dispersion has historically exceeded Tenaris's (negative EBITDA in 2015-16). SBO and Hunting are single-driver US land plays with far steeper swings (SBO's consensus FY2027 EPS growth is +143% against Tenaris's +11%). Tenaris is the least concentrated of its tubular cohort and still highly concentrated in absolute terms.

For the memo. State the forecast as "one price variable, one timing variable, one policy variable". The cone in D-04 follows directly from the sensitivities: 10% on the US index is $360-450m of EBITDA and $0.28-0.35 of EPS over one to two quarters; one quarter of Gulf deferral is about $85m of EBITDA and $0.065 of EPS; a Section 232 relaxation removes price faster than cost because about 140 days of tariffed inventory stay in cost of goods sold for two quarters.

Internal corporate change forces

C-1155C-1255C-1348C-1455C-1550C-1660C-1760C-1858C-1945C-2060C-8745C-2150C-2258
C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F5519th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The leadership change is substantial in form and small in substance. It signals continuity and institutional succession, not a change of direction.

  • Chief executive. Gabriel Podskubka became CEO on 6 May 2026, succeeding Paolo Rocca, who had led for nearly three decades and remains Chairman. Podskubka is the first CEO from outside the Rocca family. He joined Siderca in 1995 (31 years in the group) and was COO from April 2023, so he ran the operational response to the tariffs: regionalising production, Bay City at record output, the Koppel restart.
  • Finance. Carlos Gómez Álzaga became CFO in May 2025 after 20 years inside the group. His predecessor, Alicia Móndolo, became Vice Chair in August 2026 with oversight of sustainability and risk.
  • Board. Reduced from 11 to 10 members; Condorelli retired; Jaime Serra Puche resigned from the board and audit committee in August 2026; Germán Curá stepped down as Vice Chair. Four of the ten directors are independent. The auditor changed by competitive tender to Forvis Mazars, after EY and PwC before it.
  • Controlling holder. Techint sold 23.6m shares under a non-discretionary Accelerated Share Disposal agreement that ended no later than 19 May 2026, conditional on keeping at least 67% beneficial ownership. San Faustin holds 68.34% of the votes and about 64% of the capital. The CEO sold 3,946 shares, half of a negligible personal holding.

What it signals. Rocca's chairmanship keeps strategic and capital-allocation power where it was. The new CEO has given a "constructive multi-year outlook" (Jefferies), guiding fourth-quarter volume above 1mt and pointing to upside in activity, sales and prices. The change lowers key-person risk without creating a change-of-direction catalyst. One caution: the CEO change and the capital-allocation pivot (the buyback pause) happened within months of each other, so it is unclear who owns that pivot.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F5523rd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The strategy is unchanged. What has changed are tactical priorities: regionalising US and Canadian production, extending Rig Direct offshore and into digital services, and switching cash returns from buybacks to dividends. These are adequately resourced but explained only in part.

  • Regionalisation. The response to tariffs is to make more steel and pipe in North America: Bay City running extra shifts, Koppel finishing and heat treatment restarted ($2.1m), the Sault Ste. Marie modernisation (CAD 306m, about US$230m, with CAD 76.2m of federal backing), the Nucor bar framework, and a new TN 95EY sour-service grade produced in Canada. Morgan Stanley notes this reverses the historical model of importing from low-cost mills, and it raises fixed costs in North America (see C-75).
  • Service-led differentiation. Rig Direct has been extended to offshore hubs (the 74,500m² Suriname centre for TotalEnergies; Guyana for ExxonMobil). Digital capability has been added through AllTorque ($4.7m, folded into WISer) and the remote monitoring centre (70 rigs).
  • Europe. The Artrom Steel Tubes acquisition in Romania (€86m, cash-free and debt-free; 450kt of steelmaking and 200kt of seamless capacity; closing in the fourth quarter of 2026) adds low-cost capacity inside the EU's new quota wall.
  • Low-carbon products. Geothermal, carbon capture and THera hydrogen materials; a 30% carbon-intensity cut targeted by 2030 against a restated baseline.
  • Capital returns. The board moved to an all-dividend policy (see C-19). This is the only real change, and the company has not explained it publicly.

Resourcing is ample (net cash about $3.5bn, capex about $600m a year). Bernstein's "Age of Maturity" framing fits: harvest cash, extend the service moat, invest defensively. For the model: no strategic step-change in growth. Fixed costs edge up in North America, the US cost position improves as imported billets are replaced, and the European perimeter grows slightly from the first quarter of 2027.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D4819th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: It has not been raised. The mid-term framework is unchanged, and the near-term guidance was trimmed rather than raised in August.

  • Mid-term. The 2024 capital markets day set an EBITDA margin corridor of 20% to 25% through the cycle, and it is unchanged. FY2025's 24.2% sat near the top. Capex runs at about $600m a year, "in line with historical levels".
  • Near term. Second-half 2026 sales and EBITDA are guided "in line with" the first half, explicitly assuming Hormuz stays closed through year-end. Redburn and the Leadership digest describe this as a trim to second-half expectations. The third quarter is guided lower on seasonality and mix, and the fourth quarter to volume above 1mt with higher prices.
  • Informal ambition. Offshore momentum is said to continue through 2028; the new CEO talks of "impeccable execution opportunities"; but no new targets have been given.

The absence of a raise fits the company's habit of guiding conservatively and beating (see C-70). The guidance itself is mildly above consensus (see C-68). For the model, the 20-25% corridor should be read as a floor-and-ceiling management will defend: a model margin above 25% needs explicit justification, such as Redburn's 25.6% for FY2027.

C-14To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?F5521st of 25full answer under 2a Internal risks
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

D5013th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: There is minimal reduction of the perimeter, and the business does not need much. Tenaris is adding a little (Artrom, Suriname, Canadian grades) and trimming only at the edges.

  • Edge trims. The Machachi threading mill in Ecuador is idle; the Japanese NKKTubes plant was closed earlier and replaced with a long-term JFE supply deal; SIAT Valentín Alsina in Argentina has been on furlough since April 2026, with 150 lay-offs blocked by mandatory mediation; the welded business took a $622m impairment in 2020.
  • No significant divestments, and none needed. The structure is already simple: Tubes is 95% of sales, and the Other segment (sucker rods, coiled tubing, Argentine oilfield services) is 5%. The perimeter question that does matter is the associates: the 11.46% stake in Ternium (market value $980.9m at 30 June 2026), the Usiminas stake and the 22% of Techgen. These carry earnings volatility (Ternium's EBITDA margin fell to 10% in 2025; Usiminas lost $521m in 2025) and legal tail (the CSN litigation). There is no sign of disposal, as these are Techint group cross-holdings.

This is a neutral force for the model: no simplification upside, no disposal proceeds.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C607th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: It is entering new markets at a moderate pace and sensibly. Every move is close to the core business, uses existing customers or capabilities, and needs little capital. By the Wisdom Library's "core versus adjacency" test the moves score well, but none is large enough to change the model before 2028.

  • Geographic. Suriname, a new frontier basin, with a dedicated service centre and Rig Direct for TotalEnergies' GranMorgu, so the customer was in hand before entry. Guyana, a Rig Direct footprint for ExxonMobil's 35-well campaign. Romania (Artrom), low-cost capacity inside the EU quota. Canada, new local grades and capacity.
  • Product. Geothermal: a European closed-loop partnership with Green Therma, more than 500 Enel geothermal wells without a failure, and a casing-drilling service presented at GeoTHERM 2026. Carbon capture: TenarisHydril Blue with Dopeless passed 500 extreme thermal and mechanical cycles for a North Sea project. Hydrogen: THera materials, high-pressure storage vessels in Europe and California, and contributions to DNV's hydrogen pipeline standards. Digital well integrity: WISer and AllTorque.
  • How sensible. Very. These moves follow customers, reuse metallurgy and connections, and avoid large greenfield risk. The weakness is commercial pace against Vallourec in geothermal, where Vallourec holds the $800m Fervo contract, the XGS/Meta partnership and a 10-15% new-energies EBITDA target for 2030; Tenaris has set no target.

For the model: offshore new basins add volume in 2027-28; new energy adds about 1% of revenue by 2027.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B606th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The rises are real in US seamless, nominal in US welded and internationally, and at group level still latent in the income statement. Management and brokers increasingly say the fourth quarter of 2026 is when they show.

  • US seamless: real. The index is up 16% year to date (September 2026) and more than 20% from the second-half 2024 trough; US prices rose 4-5% a month at times. Morgan Stanley finds Tenaris's variable margins up 20% (seamless) and 15% (welded) despite 20-30% higher steel input costs. Tenaris's realised prices exceed the benchmark seamless index by 34% (Bernstein), helped by Rig Direct. The rise exceeds Bernstein's 4% needed to offset the tariffs. It is real, but enabled by tariffs: it is the industry's price power under protection, not Tenaris's alone.
  • US welded: nominal. Prices are up 21%, but hot-rolled coil is up 20-23%, Korean ERW imports keep coming, and the seamless-welded spread is at a record. Welded margins are squeezed (Redburn).
  • International: nominal or flat. Long-term NOC contracts are linked to raw-material formulas. Rocca and Podskubka call international pricing "highly stable". Spot tenders in lower-end products are eroding (Argentina lost to Welspun, Middle East softening per Morgan Stanley).
  • Group: latent. The EBITDA margin fell from 23.7% (first quarter 2026, company basis) to 21.9% (second quarter), so price rises have not yet overtaken cost inflation in the reported numbers. Management says the "lagged drop-through of price increases means margin benefits will not fully realise until the fourth quarter", and that sequential price increases are being implemented across several regions.

For the model: assume US seamless realised prices rise by roughly the index move with a one-to-two-quarter lag (Jefferies sees another 5-10% upside; Jefferies' price target is about $2,850/t by end-2026), hold international prices flat in real terms, and treat welded as cost pass-through only. The key judgement for the second set is the durability of the US real price, because it rests on tariff policy and distributor refill.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C+586th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: It is not stepping up. M&A remains small, strategically sensible and disciplined, with a good integration record. Capacity for a larger deal (about $3.5bn of net cash) is unused.

  • Recent deals. Artrom (€86m for 200kt of seamless and 450kt of steelmaking capacity; closing in the fourth quarter of 2026) is very cheap per tonne of capacity relative to building new. Artrom has, to my knowledge, been owned by Russia's TMK, which would make this consolidation of a sanctioned competitor's European asset; the second set should verify this. Also AllTorque (US$4.7m), the Beaver Falls scrap yard ($17.5m) and Shawcor pipe coating ($183m, end-2023), which is now winning offshore coated line pipe.
  • Discipline. Rocca abandoned Benteler when antitrust conditions threatened the economics (Redburn). Larger deals were timed against the cycle: IPSCO ($1.1bn, 2020) bought US steelmaking at Koppel; Hydril ($1.5bn, 2007) created leadership in premium connections.
  • Integration. Hydril and IPSCO were absorbed into the global industrial system. Shawcor is integrated into Tubes. The two big mistakes were the Usiminas stake (2012), which was impaired and left the CSN indemnity litigation, and the welded impairments of 2020 ($622m).

For the model: no M&A beyond Artrom (small; consolidated from about the first quarter of 2027, financially "non-material"). A large deal is possible in a downturn given the balance sheet and Rocca's counter-cyclical habit, but no evidence of one exists.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D4515th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Total shareholder returns have fallen, not risen. The dividend was raised while buybacks were stopped, so cash returned in 2026 will be roughly half of 2025's. But the balance sheet is building surplus cash at a rate that makes a restart, or a special distribution, likely in 2027.

  • The facts. Buybacks were $1,435m in FY2024 and $1,375m in FY2025, alongside dividends of $758m and $900m: total distributions of about $2.2bn to $2.3bn a year (Bernstein: $2,262m in 2025). In March 2026 the board terminated the second tranche of the third buyback programme early, citing market volatility. Buybacks in the first half of 2026 were only $90m, despite a $1.2bn authorisation renewed in 2025. The 2026 interim dividend was doubled to $0.59 per share. Distributions in 2026 are therefore likely to be about $1.2bn to $1.3bn (the final dividend paid in May plus the November interim). Jefferies had modelled $2.2bn including $1.2bn of buybacks, so the market was disappointed (Redburn, Jefferies).
  • Why it may have happened: three hypotheses, unresolved. (a) Free float: San Faustin's stake rose passively to 68.34% of votes because of the buybacks, and Bernstein warned that continued buybacks could compress the free float enough to force a Milan delisting by 2027. Techint's offsetting share sales under the disposal agreement ended in May 2026, at about the time buybacks stopped. (b) Caution on the cycle and on working capital (Hormuz, a receivables build; Redburn cut FY2026 free cash flow 34%). (c) The controlling holder's liquidity: the Techint group is investing heavily (Ternium's $2.5bn of 2025 capex at Pesquería; $2.4bn of group investment in Vaca Muerta through Tecpetrol in 2026). Dividends give San Faustin cash; buybacks do not. Hypothesis (a) fits the timing best; (c) is a well-founded conjecture; the company has given no explanation.
  • Balance-sheet arithmetic. Consensus free cash flow is $1,320m in FY2026, $1,907m in FY2027 and $1,998m in FY2028 (before dividends). Against dividends of about $1.2bn to $1.5bn, net cash grows by roughly $0.4bn to $0.8bn a year from about $3.5bn. Bernstein still expects $11bn of cumulative distributions through 2028, which implies buybacks or specials resume. Consensus dividend per share is $1.09 for FY2026, $1.50 for FY2027 (an odd figure, possibly a special) and $1.24 for FY2028.

For the model: assume a flat share count (about 1,005m to 1,012m) through FY2026, and model buyback resumption from the 2027 annual meeting as a scenario, not the base. EPS loses the 4-7% a year of support it had from share-count reduction in 2024-25 (shares fell from 1,167.9m to 1,011.8m in two years).

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–60

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Several smaller positive forces exist. Together they could add a few points to EBITDA and a few hundred million dollars of cash over 2027.

  1. Hormuz recovery (see C-09): up to about $130m of revenue a quarter, plus the backlog.
  2. Release of anti-dumping deposits and liabilities. Argentine rates cut to 6.76% and Mexican to a preliminary 1.62% allow past cash deposits to be recovered once final; the $49.6m CBP claim is preliminarily removed.
  3. Tariff mitigation maturing. Nucor domestic bars from January 2026, Koppel, scrap integration and Sault Ste. Marie should cut the imported-billet tariff cost from 2027.
  4. A working-capital release if days fall back from 143 towards 128-130: about $400m to $500m.
  5. Lower energy costs in Argentina (wind farms) and, in time, solar in Romania and Italy.
  6. Restarting buybacks or a special dividend in 2027 on surplus cash.
  7. Contract extensions (ADNOC $1.9bn, Ecopetrol, Petrobras call-offs) and offshore wins (Sakarya phase 3, with $47.8m of Voestalpine plate committed).
  8. Artrom synergies and EU quota protection for EU-made product.
  9. Digital services revenue (remote monitoring, WISer).

Most are timing or cash items rather than structural growth. Hormuz and tariff mitigation are the two with model-level size.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4522nd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: They have indicated very little, apart from the change in how cash is returned, which the controlling holder appears at least to accept and may favour.

  • San Faustin and Techint. No activist is present; the San Faustin stake sits behind the Dutch foundation RP STAK; Techint's share sales were non-discretionary, ended in May 2026 and kept a 67% floor. Rocca as Chairman preserves the industrial strategy, including the long-held preference for focus on the core over diversification.
  • Political positioning. Rocca's public clashes with President Milei preceded the loss of the pipeline tender to Welspun. Rocca has nonetheless said "we are positive on Argentina", and the group is investing heavily there through Tecpetrol.
  • The one real change is capital-return form (dividends instead of buybacks), discussed in C-19. Two readings are consistent with it: protecting the Milan free float, and supporting the controlling holder's cash needs. Neither indicates a strategy change. Both mean minority shareholders should not expect buybacks to be the first use of surplus cash in future.

For the model: assume no strategic change from the controller. Capital returns favour dividends, with buybacks used only as far as free float allows.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Weak to moderate in strength, narrow in breadth, and very well resourced but under-used. Tenaris is not a "corporate change" story. The positives under way are continuity of leadership, adaptation to tariffs, extension of Rig Direct offshore and into digital, small cheap tuck-ins and a higher dividend. Set against them are a cut in total distributions, slipping working-capital discipline and incentives that do not align management with minority shareholders. The resources (about $3.5bn of net cash, capex about 5% of sales, R&D of $64m) are far larger than the changes being made with them. The pattern fits Bernstein's "Age of Maturity": a mature cash harvester. For the model this means internal forces add little to EPS growth beyond operational recovery. The upside option is a 2027 decision to return surplus cash; the downside is fixed-cost creep in North America.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

C-5810th of 25

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The staircase is moderate in height and well founded, but low in novelty. Most steps are recoveries the market already expects (price drop-through, Hormuz, offshore ramp). The novel steps are a return of buybacks or specials in 2027, and evidence that Tenaris can earn at the top of its corridor while tariffs persist.

Next 6 months (to April 2027):

  • 4 November 2026, third-quarter results. Seasonal low, guided flat on the second quarter. The novelty is the fourth-quarter outlook (volume above 1mt; first visible price drop-through). Well founded, low novelty.
  • November 2026. Payment of the doubled interim dividend ($0.59). Saipem and Subsea 7 merger decision (sector read-across). US midterms on 3 November (tariff policy, a two-sided risk).
  • Fourth quarter 2026. Artrom closing (small). Nucor coil contract renewal (a cost event). Suriname Rig Direct deliveries starting (the GranMorgu ramp). Possible final Commerce Department rulings on mechanical pipe scope and lower Mexican and Argentine anti-dumping rates (cash release). Final decisions on the anti-dumping petitions against Austria, Taiwan and the UAE (timing approximate).
  • February 2027, full-year results. First-half 2027 guidance, the final dividend and any buyback or special decision. Novelty medium to high if buybacks restart, given surplus cash of about $0.4bn to $0.8bn a year on top of about $3.5bn.
  • Undated. Hormuz reopening: about $130m a quarter of deferred premium revenue invoiced within 70-90 days. High impact, well founded as a mechanism, undated.

Months 6 to 18 (April 2027 to April 2028):

  • FY2027 margin recovery towards 24-25% as US price increases flow through and tariff mitigation matures (Nucor bars, Koppel, Sault Ste. Marie modernisation). Well founded, if tariffs persist.
  • Offshore deliveries scaling (Guyana, Suriname, Búzios, Sakarya), with offshore spending up about 30% in 2027. Well founded.
  • May 2027 annual meeting: capital-return policy, buyback authorisation and free-float questions. Medium novelty.
  • Working-capital normalisation as a free cash flow surprise against the low FY2026 base. Moderately founded.
  • Geothermal commercial scaling (industrialisation expected by late 2027) and carbon-capture awards. Novel but small.

Months 18 to 36 (2028 to 2029):

  • The deepwater cycle peaks around 2028; CBAM in full effect, favouring low-carbon steel; possible counter-cyclical M&A with the balance sheet. Weakly founded today.
  • Section 232 review risk and the US policy cycle (the 2028 presidential election). A two-sided risk.

The market can look forward mainly to earnings recovery and cash return. The narrative is "a quality cyclical harvesting cash in a protected upcycle", not a transformation. The novel catalysts are capital return and evidence of margins at the top of the corridor. The well-founded ones are the price drop-through and the offshore ramp.
