Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

B655th of 25

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

B655th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry is structurally attractive where Tenaris makes its money. Premium seamless and connections form a Tier 1 oligopoly (top three 84% of premium seamless) with high barriers, disciplined competitors, capacity leaving rather than entering, and long-term NOC contracts. Tenaris is the clear number one by scale, share and service model (Rig Direct). Two developments shape the next four years. First, the industry's US pricing support is a policy rent from Section 232 and anti-dumping orders, which makes the most profitable part of the current upcycle the most fragile. Second, Tenaris's relative advantage has narrowed: Vallourec now earns similar margins at a much lower valuation, and national champions and Asian exporters are taking share in Saudi Arabia, Argentina and commodity pipe. The model should assume stable premium share, margin parity with Vallourec, a US price premium that lasts while tariffs do, and a supply side (US steel) that is Tenaris's structural weak link. On historical patterns, the share price is in the mid-cycle phase where returns depend on delivering earnings rather than on further re-rating.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3260C-3372C-3455C-3755C-3855C-3950
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D6014th of 25full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

A722nd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The route to market is a clear help and a source of advantage. Rig Direct, which bypasses distributors, accounts for about 70% of global sales and 90% of US sales, against Vallourec's 100% through distributors. Its fragility is low.

How it helps:

  1. Switching costs. Once an operator outsources pipe procurement and inventory to Tenaris, leaving means rebuilding a purchasing team. The lock-in is organisational, not just contractual, and Bernstein notes customers "face high costs if switching back to distributors". It served 531 rigs at end-2024 and 512 active rigs in late 2025.
  2. Price capture. Realised prices are 34% above the benchmark seamless index.
  3. Insulation from destocking. Bypassing distributors spares Tenaris most of the distributor inventory cycle. This matters now, because the Risks report cites distributor indices weakening (pricing index from 100 to 67; 35% of distributors expecting oversupply). That data conflicts with Morgan Stanley's view that inventories are below average, so the second set should verify which is current.
  4. Volume outperformance. North American volumes decoupled from rig counts in 2023, and Redburn expects Rig Direct to keep Tenaris ahead of the rig count.

The cost. Tenaris carries the inventory: working-capital days are 137-143 against Vallourec's 86, absolute operating working capital is about $4.6bn to $4.8bn, and inventory is at risk of write-downs if US drilling contracts. Fragility arises if large NOCs build in-house procurement, but NOCs are moving the other way (ADNOC uses Rig Direct). For the model: keep the volume outperformance against the rig count (pipe intensity +3-4% a year plus modest share gains), and keep working capital at about 38-40% of revenue.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C-5511th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Competitors are behaving rationally on price, which helps. They are competing harder on capability and contracts, which hurts at the margin. Tenaris's position is robust in absolute terms and gently eroding in relative terms.

  • Vallourec is the most important. Margins are now level. Jefferies says Vallourec closed its EBITDA-per-tonne gap with Tenaris in late 2025, at €621 a tonne. Vallourec's Tubes EBITDA per tonne was $724 in the first quarter of 2026, and by its own account it was higher than Tenaris's in early 2026. It won the largest line pipe orders in its history from ExxonMobil Guyana (Longtail and Hammerhead, about 40kt) and Petrobras's Atapu 2 (19kt), signed an Aramco agreement to thread premium OCTG locally, is the only local seamless producer in Brazil (under a Petrobras frame agreement), leads in geothermal (Fervo), and is cheaper (8.1x P/E against Tenaris's 13.0x). It is pure seamless, so welded weakness does not touch it, and it makes all its US steel locally.
  • Nippon Steel and US Steel are a latent threat in North America.
  • The Saudi champion is taking Aramco share. Chinese mills are entering the Middle East.
  • What helps. All Tier 1 peers are capacity-disciplined. Vallourec has no Rig Direct equivalent. Tenaris and Vallourec cooperate on trade remedies (joint anti-dumping petitions and Enforce and Protect Act filings).

For the model: margin parity with Vallourec is the new normal. Tenaris's premium is now in scale, cash and service, not in margin per tonne. Its position is not fragile, but it should not expect to win share in offshore line pipe or in Saudi OCTG.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?C559th of 25full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C559th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The disruption forces act on every link of the chain, but net they leave Tenaris's position only moderately exposed. Most act on its competitors or customers in ways Tenaris can absorb.

  • Demand (operators). Drilling efficiency means fewer rigs and more pipe per rig: positive. The energy transition is deferred over four years. NOCs push local content: negative for imports, positive for Tenaris's local mills (Saudi, Mexico, Argentina, Brazil).
  • Competition. Chinese technology replication is a slow negative in the Middle East and Asia. Vallourec's restructuring is a relative negative. Capacity exits are positive.
  • Regulators and politicians. Trade protection is a positive with high fragility. Argentina's liberalisation is mixed. CBAM is positive.
  • Suppliers. Steel consolidation (Nippon and US Steel; Nucor's strength in US bars and coil) and tariffs make US steel inputs dearer. Negative and structural until Tenaris's own US steel supply increases.
  • Logistics. The Hormuz closure is a negative now and temporary in base case.
  • Customers' procurement. Digital procurement and Rig Direct deepen dependence on Tenaris: positive.

The weakest link is supply, not demand: Tenaris's US mills depend on external, tariffed or Nucor-supplied steel while Vallourec is self-sufficient. This leaves margin, not volume, exposed.

Industry structure

Judgement65C-2485C-2578C-2672C-2772C-2862C-2962C-3168C-3560C-3670
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)B655th of 25full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

A852nd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Tenaris is number one by a wide margin in every premium measure, and number one in total OCTG. It is roughly twice the size of the number two by share and about three times by revenue.

  • Global OCTG (seamless plus welded). Tenaris 31%, TMK 21%, Vallourec 15%, Nippon Steel 13%, US Steel 7% (Rystad, via Morgan Stanley). Redburn puts Tenaris at 27% and Bernstein at 35% of a $35bn oil and gas tubes market. The sources differ on what the market includes, not on the ranking.
  • Premium seamless. Tenaris 40%, Vallourec 26%, Nippon Steel 18%. Premium connections. Tenaris 37% (TenarisHydril), Vallourec about 26-31%.
  • Scale. 2025 revenue was $11,981m against Vallourec's €3,809m (about $4.3bn) and Hunting's $1,019m. Capacity is 7.8mt (4.7mt seamless, 3.1mt welded) against Vallourec's under 2mt, capped at 1.7mt of shipments. Tenaris has 12 seamless and 25 welded mills in about 20 countries.
  • Regional. In Brazil's pre-salt, Tenaris and Vallourec together hold more than 70% of OCTG. In the US, Rig Direct supplies 90% of Tenaris's own US sales. Tenaris gained about $900m of US sales when TMK's roughly 9% US share was lost to sanctions.

The ranking is secure. The relevant change is not rank but relative profitability: Vallourec, at about half Tenaris's premium share, now earns a similar margin.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

B+784th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The market is highly concentrated, and steeply so, in the segment that earns the profits (premium seamless and connections). It is fragmented in the commodity segment (welded, API-grade pipe in Asia).

  • Top five in total OCTG: 87% (Rystad, a market definition that appears to exclude Chinese domestic consumption). On the broadest definition (global demand of about 15mt including China and welded), the top three hold 34-35% (Jefferies).
  • Premium seamless: the top three hold 84%. Below them are scattered suppliers (JFE, Hunting, Tubacex, Indian mills such as Maharashtra Seamless, which holds 55% of India's domestic seamless market, and Kirloskar), each in the low single digits globally. The top five therefore hold far more than five times the share of numbers six to ten.
  • Welded. Chinese and Indian mills hold about 40% each of commoditised welded pipe (Bernstein).

The model consequence: price behaviour in Tenaris's premium business (about 75% of OCTG revenue comes from proprietary products) follows oligopoly logic. Welded and API-grade pipe follow commodity logic, with prices set at the margin by imports.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B+723rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Premium shares have been stable over five years, with one large redistribution: TMK's US share moved to Tenaris and Vallourec after sanctions. New entrants have taken little in premium, perhaps under 5% globally, though they are gaining in commodity and state-protected niches.

  • Redistribution. TMK's roughly 9% US share was absorbed by Western producers after sanctions: Tenaris added about $900m of US sales, Vallourec about $300m. Russian peer TMK is still the global number two by volume, but is shut out of Western premium markets.
  • Entrants. Saudi Arabia's state-backed JESCO/AMTJ champion took a leading share of Aramco's supply, a local-content gain at foreign suppliers' expense. Chinese seamless exporters (Baosteel, TPCO) are entering the Middle East. Welspun took an Argentine pipeline tender and is building 1.2mt of US capacity (welded and line pipe). Southeast Asian (Sunindo) and Indian (Kirloskar, about 188.7kt of seamless; Maharashtra Seamless through Japanese connection partners) producers are adding capacity. Korean ERW keeps entering the US despite tariffs.
  • Exits. US Steel took out 1.1mt in 2020 and Vallourec 1.15mt in 2024.

Entry net of exit has been negative in the West, which is why seamless is under-supplied. Entry is concentrated in national-champion and lower-end segments. For the model, Tenaris's premium volume share can be held roughly constant. Some loss should be assumed in Saudi Arabia and in Argentine line pipe, and gains in US OCTG through Rig Direct.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

B+723rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Over two decades share has moved towards the largest and most advantaged producers, and Tenaris has been the main consolidator: Siderca, Dalmine and Tamsa formed Tenaris; then Maverick, Hydril, IPSCO and Shawcor; now Artrom. Capacity exits by the weaker players reinforce this (Vallourec's German closures, US Steel's idling, TMK's sanctions). Tenaris is among the most advantaged. The nuance is that the most advantaged on margin is now arguably Vallourec, smaller and purely seamless, with 100% local steel in the US and a lean cost base after restructuring. The consolidation dynamic still favours Tenaris on scale, service network (Rig Direct; Vallourec sells 100% through distributors) and balance sheet, which allows counter-cyclical buying (Artrom bought cheaply from a sanctioned owner, IPSCO in 2020). The structural force continues, but it no longer widens Tenaris's margin lead.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C629th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin first or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: Among the Tier 1 premium producers (Tenaris, Vallourec, Nippon Steel), margin and returns come first, and behaviour is benign. On the commodity and state-backed fringe (Chinese, Indian and Korean welded; the Saudi champion; historically TMK), volume comes first. Prices do fall when volume falls in the US spot-linked market, but not much in international contracts.

  • Tier 1 discipline. Vallourec's "value over volume" plan caps shipments at 1.7mt, has cut capacity from over 3mt to under 2mt, and targets returning 80-100% of cash generation. Tenaris has prioritised "margin preservation over chasing unprofitable, low-end commodity market share" (Jefferies). Industry players broadly prioritise value, supporting margins above 20%.
  • Price and volume. The US seamless index swings widely: troughs in late 2015 and 2020, and about $2,110/t in August 2024, against about $2,538/t in the first half of 2026 and $2,850/t forecast by end-2026. International NOC pricing is formula-based and "highly stable", but spot tenders in lower-end products erode. The Hunting digest says 40-45% of OCTG volumes are now on multi-year contracts, up from under 30% three years ago, at a 5-10% premium to spot. This dampens the price-volume link.
  • The fringe. Welders and state champions price to fill capacity. The Argentine Welspun bid was said to be 40% cheaper.

For the model, apply the price-volume link to the US index-linked share of sales (about 30-43%) and a damped version to South America. Keep international contract pricing stable, with formula pass-through of raw materials.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B+622nd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The Tier 1 attitude has become more disciplined over three years and looks stable. The shark-attack risk comes from outside the oligopoly, and it is policy-dependent rather than behaviour-dependent.

  • More stable than before. Vallourec under Guillemot moved from survival (2022) to "From Good to Great". Its CEO has about €5m of personal shareholding and it is investment-grade, focused on shareholder returns rather than share. ArcelorMittal's partial exit removes a strategic owner that might have pushed for volume. Vallourec now sells through distributors only, so it is not fighting Rig Direct on service.
  • Possible shark attacks. (a) A Nippon Steel and US Steel combination using new capital to win share in North America (Bernstein flags it as a potential headwind), though tubulars are only 5-6% of the merged group. (b) Chinese and Korean exporters, if US or EU protection is loosened. (c) The Saudi national champion pricing for local share. None of these shows O'Leary-style unpredictability today, but (b) would be instant if tariffs fell.
  • What Tenaris's own record shows. It has not used price aggressively against peers. It defends share through service (Rig Direct) and holds margin.

Over the last 18 months there is no sign of destructive pricing. The 81% month-on-month jump in US welded imports in July 2026 (Jefferies' threshold for discipline breaking) is a welded-segment warning, not a premium one.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B685th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The structure is strongly supportive in premium and internationally, and robust there. It is supportive but fragile in the US commodity-linked segment, where support rests on tariffs.

The robust parts are high capital and qualification barriers (a hot-rolling seamless mill plus multi-year NOC qualification), a Tier 1 oligopoly focused on returns, capacity discipline, local-content positions in NOC markets, and long-term contracts. Together they explain a 21% average EBITDA margin for Tenaris Tubes over a decade (Jefferies), against 4% for Vallourec over the same decade before its restructuring.

The fragile parts are US pricing, which depends on Section 232 and anti-dumping orders, and welded pricing, which follows hot-rolled coil and imports. Imports are 34% lower, but a policy reversal would let low-cost seamless and welded pipe back in. Redburn and others name this as the primary downside, compressing margins below 25%.

Fragility ranking for the model: US price (high fragility, about 30-43% of sales), international premium (low), offshore (low). The supportive structure underwrites the 20% margin floor. It does not underwrite margins above 25%.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?C+606th of 25full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

A701st of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The threat from substitutes is low over four years and broadly unchanged.

  • Well construction. There is no practical substitute for steel casing and tubing in oil, gas or geothermal wells. The real substitution is within steel: welded instead of seamless when hot-rolled coil is cheap (Tenaris owns both, with under-used welded capacity), and seamless instead of welded under high coil prices or tariffs. Tenaris can switch production between the two, which hedges it.
  • Line pipe. Welded pipe and imports compete; composite and non-metallic pipe appear only in low-pressure niches.
  • Energy demand. Renewables substituting for oil and gas is the long-run threat. Over four years it is offset by deepwater growth and by new tubular demand from geothermal, carbon capture and hydrogen, which Tenaris supplies.

The Risks report notes that substituting welded for premium seamless to avoid tariffs is "unquantified". That is a small risk to the US seamless premium if operators accept welded casing in some wells.

Business quality

Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

B666th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Tenaris's competitive advantage is wide, multi-layered and mostly mechanical. Rig Direct's organisational switching costs, the leadership in premium connections, global scale and local presence, backward integration and a fortress balance sheet together explain a decade of about 21% Tubes EBITDA margins and a roughly 20% trough floor. Customer and service advantages are widening as Rig Direct moves offshore and into digital services. Production-cost advantage is narrowing, chiefly against Vallourec in the US. Pricing power is real but in large part an industry-wide protection rent at present. The second set should model the moat as protecting share, contract renewals and a margin floor of about 20% in a downturn, but not a margin premium over Vallourec. It should treat Tenaris's realised price premium as stable rather than expanding.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A752nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Tenaris has a wide moat built from five reinforcing advantages. In aggregate it is stable. Within it, the customer-side and service advantages are widening and the production-cost advantage over Vallourec is narrowing. The moat protects a 20% margin floor and premium share. It no longer guarantees a margin lead.

  1. Customer-related: strong and widening. Rig Direct creates organisational switching costs: the customer dismantles its own procurement and inventory function. By the Wisdom Library's five-type test, the costs are procedural, relational and risk-based, and the risk is downhole failure. It covers 70% of global sales and 90% of US sales, 512-531 rigs, and is now offshore in Suriname and Guyana. Multi-year NOC qualification and contracts add to it (ADNOC $1.9bn extended, ExxonMobil ten-year, Petrobras, Aramco local-content agreements). Digital traceability and monitoring deepen it (PipeTracer, WISer, the remote monitoring centre).
  2. Knowledge and technology: strong and stable, slowly threatened. TenarisHydril holds 37% of premium connections. About 75% of OCTG revenue comes from proprietary products. Dopeless and RunReady (used on Petrobras pre-salt and all 36 Suriname wells) and Blue carbon-capture connections stand out. R&D was $64m in 2025 ($294m over five years) against Hunting's $10.5m. The threats are Chinese copies, Vallourec's CLEANWELL and VAM, and the Global Tubing patent challenge.
  3. Scale: strong and stable. 7.8mt of capacity in about 20 countries, a global system that can shift production between mills (as the tariff response showed), Exiros purchasing scale, and the ability to serve NOCs anywhere.
  4. Production cost and integration: good but narrowing. Five electric steel shops, scrap yards, captive wind power in Argentina, and the lowest carbon intensity (1.17t CO2 per tonne of steel, an advantage under CBAM). The narrowing is in the US, where Tenaris imports 28% of its billets under tariff while Vallourec is self-sufficient, and in Vallourec's lower post-restructuring cost base.
  5. Regulatory and political position: mixed. Local mills inside each protected market (US, Canada, Mexico, Argentina, Saudi Arabia, Brazil through Confab, Romania) let Tenaris benefit from protection. But this exposes it to local politics (Argentina) and to tariffs on its own cross-border flows.
  6. Balance sheet as a weapon. Net cash lets Tenaris invest against the cycle (IPSCO in 2020, Artrom now) and carry Rig Direct inventory that rivals cannot.

The best evidence of the moat is the decade-average Tubes EBITDA margin of 21%, against Vallourec's 4% before its restructuring, and a trough margin of about 20% in late 2024 when sector margins typically compress 400-600 basis points. The best evidence of narrowing is margin parity with Vallourec since late 2025.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B725th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Tenaris is about three times Vallourec by revenue and about four times by capacity, and the largest OCTG supplier outside China. Scale confers a cost of service and capabilities nobody else can match (global Rig Direct, NOC coverage, R&D, procurement). It does not confer lower unit production cost than a focused rival.

  • Relative size. Revenue $11,981m (2025) against Vallourec about $4.3bn and Hunting $1.0bn. Capacity 7.8mt against under 2mt for Vallourec. Operating cash flow of $35bn from 2000 to 2024. Net cash of about $3.5bn against Vallourec's $67m net cash in the first quarter of 2026.
  • What it confers. (a) Procurement leverage through Exiros (more than $13bn a year). (b) A global inventory and service network (Rig Direct hubs, the 74,500m² Suriname base). (c) Being able to bid for every NOC programme, with local mills in most of them. (d) Spreading R&D and digital platform costs. (e) Flexibility to move production between mills when trade barriers or logistics shift. (f) The financial staying power to absorb a 2020-type trough (EBITDA fell to $618m, a 12% margin, while Tenaris still generated positive free cash flow) and buy assets at the bottom.
  • What it does not confer. Lower margin per tonne. By the Wisdom Library's "scale needs a bounded market" test, scale is a real advantage in services and network (where a rival must reproduce the assets) but not in milling, where Vallourec's narrower footprint has caught up.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

B704th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: In premium OCTG and deepwater, Tenaris's surplus customer utility is demonstrably not inferior and probably leading. In commodity line pipe and price-led tenders it is inferior on price.

  • Evidence of leading utility. Customers pay 34% above the benchmark seamless index through Rig Direct, and premium tubulars command up to seven times API-grade prices. The cost of failure dominates the buying decision: operators prioritise performance "to avoid expensive downhole failures" (expert calls). Tenaris has more than 500 geothermal wells without a connection failure. TotalEnergies chose Dopeless for all 36 Suriname wells, and its Senior Vice-President praised the Suriname centre as "a key step in execution". ADNOC extended a $1.9bn contract that uses Rig Direct for half of Abu Dhabi's needs. Cenovus approached Tenaris to test connections jointly. Toyota recognised the Campana mill for quality. US purchasers rate domestic producers as comparable or superior to importers on availability (USTR).
  • Evidence of inferiority. Argentine officials said Techint's pipeline bid was 40% dearer than Indian imports, and it lost to Welspun. Spot tenders in lower-end applications are eroding. Vallourec wins offshore line pipe (Guyana, Atapu 2).

Inferiority in premium is disproved by renewals, extensions and price premiums held through the 2024 trough. Inferiority in commodity line pipe is confirmed.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

B+654th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Tenaris has demonstrable pricing power and uses it: realised prices sit 34% above the index, and variable margins rose despite 20-30% input inflation. But much of the current power belongs to the protected industry, not uniquely to Tenaris. Pricing power at the company level is best shown by margin resilience in troughs, not by price rises in upturns.

  • Applied, with evidence. (a) The premium to the benchmark index (Bernstein). (b) Variable margins up 15-20% while steel inputs rose 20-30% (Morgan Stanley). (c) The EBITDA margin held at 20% or above through 2024's 17% revenue decline. (d) Sequential price increases in several regions announced at second-quarter 2026 results. (e) Formula pass-through in international contracts that protects variable margins from sudden scrap inflation. (f) Average tube price held at $2,930/t in late 2025 despite regional weakness.
  • Limits. Gross margin fell from 41.5% (FY2023) to 34.2% (FY2025) and 32.5% (second quarter 2026) as the US index normalised. Tenaris prices off the market in about 30-43% of sales. There is a one-to-two-quarter lag. Welded pricing follows hot-rolled coil and imports. In Argentina, lower import barriers have exposed a premium that was a protection rent.
  • Erosion of customer utility. Little visible: no loss of Rig Direct customers is reported, and North American market share rose (North American sales were $3,050m in the first half of 2026 against $2,725m in the first half of 2025).

For the model, price power should appear as: (1) realised prices that move with the US index, with a premium and a lag; (2) stable international prices, raw materials passed through; (3) a margin floor of about 20% in a downturn.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

B556th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Partly, and in a particular way. Tenaris shares the savings from scale in logistics and inventory with customers through Rig Direct: customers avoid holding stock, running purchasing teams and paying distributor margins. It does not share them through lower prices; it keeps a premium to the index.

The Rig Direct model is "open-book" and aligns production with customers' drilling schedules (BF digest). Customers gain lower total cost of ownership, fewer failures and less non-productive rig time; Tenaris gains share, stickiness and the distributor's margin. This is a "win-win" that deepens lock-in rather than a Costco-style lowest-price policy. There is no stated value of sharing scale economies in pricing. The capital-allocation culture is oriented to returns ("maximise the potential of the company"). The model consequence is that Tenaris will not cut prices to gain share in a downturn. It will hold price and let volume fall back to the index-linked customers, so downturns show as volume and margin pressure, not price wars initiated by Tenaris.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C+628th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Moderately. Several mechanical forces strengthen Tenaris each year without heroic effort. One mechanical force (peers' restructured cost bases and self-sufficient US steel) works against its relative margin.

Working for Tenaris:

  1. The Rig Direct installed base compounds. Each new customer, rig and offshore hub (Suriname, Guyana) adds switching costs and data (PipeTracer, remote monitoring). This is a reinforcing loop between share and lock-in.
  2. Pipe intensity per well rises about 3-4% a year, so the premium share of OCTG rises with well complexity and deepwater mix. This favours the leader in premium connections.
  3. Capacity scarcity persists while no one builds a seamless mill (years and heavy capital to replicate), and each year without new mills extends the pricing window.
  4. Carbon regulation (CBAM, customers' Scope 3 targets) rewards the lowest-intensity producer more each year.
  5. Cash accumulation of about $0.4bn to $0.8bn a year at consensus increases optionality.

Working against it: Vallourec's self-sufficient US steel and lower cost base. Tariffs on Tenaris's own imported steel, until US supply is localised. National champions under local-content rules.

Net effect: a slow gain in customer lock-in and premium mix, and a slow loss in production-cost advantage. This is a business whose franchise strengthens mechanically while its margin lead does not.

4b

Management quality

C609th of 2512 answers

Group judgement

Company - Management

C609th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a strong, experienced industrial operator with an outstanding record of execution through cycles. Rocca's record as builder is excellent; Podskubka is a well-prepared internal successor; the bench is deep and stable; and the business is lavishly resourced for resilience. The strategy is smart and adaptive, but not growth-oriented. The weaknesses are in shareholder alignment and capital-allocation communication: incentives are cash-only and not tied to financial targets, independent oversight is limited, the controller dominates, and an unexplained pivot cut total distributions. For the model, management quality supports high weight on guidance and execution (beats in 18 of 21 quarters; the margin corridor defended). It gives low confidence that surplus cash will be returned through buybacks rather than held or paid as dividends to suit the controlling family.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

D5217th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Ambition is moderate and focused on the core. Tenaris aims to dominate and maximise its existing business, not to grow into new areas, and in 2026 it is visibly less ambitious than Vallourec.

Rocca's stated philosophy is "ours is to maximise the potential" of the company. He rejects diversification by sector. Ambition shows in pushing service intensity (Rig Direct everywhere, now offshore), in counter-cyclical investment (expanding the US footprint in downturns while peers shrank), and in industrial standards (the lowest carbon intensity; nine consecutive years as a worldsteel Sustainability Champion). Against that, Tenaris has set no new-energy target. Vallourec targets 10-15% of group EBITDA from new energies by 2030 and runs a "From Good to Great" plan aiming to become the premium seamless partner of choice. Bernstein calls Tenaris's phase the "Age of Maturity", meaning harvest. The new CEO's language is constructive ("impeccable execution opportunities", offshore to 2028) but sets no new goals. For the model this means no step-up in growth investment or in revenue ambition, and steady-state behaviour.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C-6211th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The strategy is smart and robust across environments, and it widens the customer-side moat. It is not bold on growth. Its greatest strength is adaptability: a global, integrated industrial system that can move production between countries when trade, logistics or demand shift.

  • Smart and dynamic. (a) Rig Direct turned Tenaris from product maker into service provider. It has run ten years and covers 70% of sales, and it is being extended offshore and into digital services. (b) Within about a year of Section 232 rising to 50%, production was regionalised: Bay City at record output, Koppel restarted, Canadian investment with state support, domestic bar supply. (c) The balance sheet is kept able to act in troughs (IPSCO in 2020). (d) Supply security: scrap yards, captive power, Exiros, a long-term JFE alloy deal after NKKTubes closed.
  • Less smart, or untested. Regionalisation raises fixed costs and depends on tariffs staying. It is the right answer to today's policy and the wrong one if policy reverses. New energy is moving more slowly than at Vallourec. The capital-return policy is unexplained.
  • Across environments. In an oil downturn, net cash, a margin floor and counter-cyclical M&A favour Tenaris. In a tariff reversal, it can switch back to importing from its global mills, but loses the US price rent. In a deepwater boom, it has the premium-connection leadership and offshore Rig Direct.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

D5817th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: On actions, the outgoing CEO and current Chairman, Paolo Rocca, is a great industrial builder. The new CEO, Gabriel Podskubka, has too short a record as CEO (five months) to judge, but his record as COO is good. Power is still shared with Rocca, so for the model "the CEO" means the Rocca-Podskubka partnership.

  • Rocca's actions. He created the unified Tenaris brand in 2001 and listed it in 2002. Revenue grew from $2.8bn (2003) to $12.0bn (2025), and the company generated $35bn of operating cash flow from 2000 to 2024. He bought Hydril ($1.5bn, 2007) and IPSCO ($1.1bn, 2020, counter-cyclical). He cut fixed costs 25% in 2020. He held EBITDA margins mostly within 20-25% through downturns, with a decade-average Tubes margin of 21%. He walked away from Benteler on antitrust grounds. Bernstein called him "the wisest man in the sector". Against him: the Usiminas stake, the welded impairments of 2020, Ternium's falling margins (10% in 2025) and Usiminas's losses under his parallel chairmanships, the SEC settlement ($78m in 2022, without admission), and the political clash with Milei.
  • Podskubka's actions. He joined in 1995. As COO from 2023 he led the operational response to tariffs and the Hormuz closure, kept customer delivery uninterrupted during the succession, and as CEO has guided fourth-quarter volume above 1mt. Jefferies praised his constructive multi-year outlook in May 2026.

The test for Podskubka over the next 18 months is the fourth-quarter 2026 delivery, working-capital normalisation and the 2027 capital-return decision.

C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

C-5810th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Hiring is internal, through the Techint system rather than the CEO's own recruitment. That system produces a deep, experienced and stable team, with the risk of being an echo chamber.

The bench: the CFO, Carlos Gómez Álzaga, has 20 years in the group, including as South America regional CFO. The former CFO, Alicia Móndolo, is Vice Chair for sustainability and risk, after 41 years in Techint. Retention is strong: professional resignations are at a ten-year low of 3.6%. Engagement is 73% (Morgan Stanley) to 85% belonging among shop-floor staff. The weakness is that there are no external hires. Vallourec, by contrast, hired its CFO from Verallia and created a global operations function. Promoting only from within may slow fresh thinking on cost structure (file 06 §7). For the model this means continuity of execution with low risk of disruption, and little likelihood of a radically new playbook.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

A782nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Very well. Tenaris is a textbook "ant colony", built in advance for resilience. Evidence: net cash of about $3.5bn; capex about equal to depreciation every year (about $600m), with more than $700m on decarbonisation over four years; R&D of $64m a year ($294m over five years); inventory buffers that carry Rig Direct and protected customers during the Hormuz closure; captive power and scrap supply; global mill redundancy that allowed production to move between countries; local service centres (Suriname); and a digital stack (PipeTracer, WISer, the remote monitoring centre, OMP planning, AI in hot rolling and heat treatment). The cost of this resilience is lower capital efficiency: 143 working-capital days and a large cash pile. For the model this underpins the 20% margin floor and resilient cash generation in a downturn.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

B+723rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, whatever the environment?

Judgement: The record is strong over both the recent and the mid term, including in tougher environments.

  • Recent. Production was regionalised within about a year of the 50% tariff. A new frontier-basin service centre opened in Suriname in June 2026. Middle East volumes were protected through alternative routes and by holding inventory. The CEO succession went smoothly with no disruption to deliveries. Small acquisitions were closed (AllTorque, Beaver Falls) and Artrom signed. Canadian state co-investment was secured. The Commerce Department scope ruling was won. Second-quarter 2026 EBITDA met consensus despite $130m of deferrals.
  • Mid term. Redburn counts consensus EPS beats in 18 of 21 quarters. The guided 20-25% margin corridor was met through a 17% revenue fall (2023-24). The $1.2bn buyback programme was completed ahead of schedule (Morgan Stanley). Half of a $200m cost programme was delivered in four quarters. The 2020 crisis response was a 25% fixed-cost cut and $220m of annual savings. Rig Direct was built from launch to 70% of sales in ten years.
  • In tougher environments. In the 2024-25 downturn Tenaris outperformed the sector, keeping margins near 24% while sector norms compressed 400-600 basis points, and gained North American share (North American volumes decoupled from rig counts in 2023).

The misses: working capital (above the sub-130-day aim), and earnings hits from non-recurring legal provisions (Usiminas). For the model, this record supports giving management guidance a high weight and assuming execution at least as good as guided.

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

B+653rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Historically Tenaris held outlier standards and out-executed: a decade-average Tubes EBITDA margin of 21% against Vallourec's 4%, ROE averaging 22% in 2004-13, a top-5% balance sheet in its sector, and a top-3% global market share. Since 2025 its lead in out-execution has narrowed to parity on margin. Standards are still outlier in service integration (Rig Direct; Vallourec has no equivalent), quality and reliability (Toyota award, no geothermal failures), carbon intensity (1.17t CO2 per tonne, the lowest) and automation (Bay City). They are not outlier in working-capital efficiency (143 days against Vallourec's 86) or in speed to commercialise new energy (Vallourec is ahead). So Tenaris is outlier in resilience and service, now average-to-good in capital efficiency, and the "higher, faster" edge is no longer visible in the numbers against its closest peer.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

D5213th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Tenaris puts the company and its controlling shareholder first. Minority shareholders benefit as passengers, because their economic interest broadly matches the controller's, but they have little influence. Governance is prudent on controls and finance, weak on independence and incentives. The evidence supports "aligned by ownership, not by governance."

  • Evidence of shareholder-friendly behaviour. Large and consistent cash returns: dividends every year, buybacks of $1.4bn a year in 2024-25, more than $11bn expected by Bernstein through 2028. A conservative balance sheet. No dilutive equity issues. No empire-building diversification. Rocca declining value-destroying deals (Benteler). Related-party transactions on a modest scale (Ternium $183m of flat steel; Techgen $67m of energy; Tenova $28m of furnaces) and disclosed. San Faustin owns about 64% of the capital, so it receives 64% of every dollar of value, which aligns it with value creation.
  • Evidence against. San Faustin holds 68.34% of the votes through RP STAK. Only four of ten directors are independent (a 40% ratio). There are no nomination or compensation committees. Family directors have sat for more than 23 years. CEO pay is all cash (about $10m) with no equity, no quantified financial targets and no long-term incentive plan, while senior managers are paid on book equity. Directors and officers own 0.08-0.09%. The CEO sold half of a negligible holding. The parallel Ternium and Usiminas chairmanships carry agency risk (Usiminas's 2012 purchase led to the CSN litigation). There is legal history (the SEC FCPA settlement of $78m in 2022; the Argentine Notebooks case, dismissed and acquitted, followed by a $9.5m US class-action settlement). The 2026 buyback pause fits the controller's interests (free float, its group's cash needs) as much as minorities'.
  • The scaffolding that exists. A fully independent audit committee with a financial expert, effective internal controls, an SEC and NYSE clawback, an auditor chosen by competitive tender, and transparent disclosure.

The reason for this pattern is that a 64%-capital holder has little reason to extract value from a company it mostly owns, so the agency risk lies in the form and timing of returns and in group priorities, not in theft. For the later sets: a governance discount is justified, and capital returns should be expected to follow the controller's preferences.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

B625th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: Tenaris is a good capital allocator with a mixed record on large acquisitions, and it was, until 2026, a well-timed repurchaser. It has stopped being a dynamic repurchaser just as surplus cash is building again, so "enlightened" is now unproven.

  • Good. Hydril (2007, $1.5bn: leadership in premium connections, high long-run return). IPSCO (2020, $1.1bn, bought in the trough: US steelmaking at Koppel, the platform for regionalisation). Shawcor (2023, $183m, coated line pipe now winning offshore). Artrom (€86m for 200kt seamless plus steelmaking: cheap). Capex held to about 5% of sales. The buybacks of 2023-25 were well timed: about $2.8bn in 2024-25 bought shares when the price was roughly €15-18, about 30-40% below today's €25.93. The share count fell 14% in three years.
  • Poor. The Usiminas stake (2012: impaired, plus the CSN indemnity litigation, an earnings drag of $171m in the second quarter of 2024 alone). The welded impairments of $622m in 2020. Historical acquisitions at peak cycle (Maverick in 2006 is a public fact outside these files; verify).
  • Now. The buyback was paused at a higher share price. This can be read as discipline, not buying back stock at a 13x forward P/E in an upcycle, or as the free-float and controller constraint. Surplus cash of about $3.5bn plus $0.4bn to $0.8bn a year will force a decision in 2027.

For the model: capital deployed at high marginal returns only in small tuck-ins. No buyback in the base case. A dated option to restart from 2027.

4c

Financial profile

C608th of 258 answers
Judgement60C-4950C-5045C-5162C-5255C-5370C-5466C-7682
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

C608th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Tenaris is economically strong but cyclical. Its strengths: the highest margins in its cohort (FY2025 EBITDA margin 24%, with a 20% floor through the cycle), a net-cash balance sheet with no financial gearing, cash conversion around 100% of net income through the cycle before dividends, and post-tax ROIC of about 12-16% at mid-cycle. Its weaknesses for a model are structural rather than about quality. First, operating leverage is about 2x on volume and about 4x on price, so EBITDA swings ±20-25% on plausible revenue paths. Second, working capital of about 38-40% of revenue absorbs cash in upturns. Third, reinvestment opportunities are limited, so EPS growth (about 5% a year on consensus from FY2025 to FY2028) now depends on price, mix and Hormuz timing, with no share-count support unless buybacks resume. Fourth, consensus assumes more non-operating income than history supports. The second set should build the model on regional revenue by pricing mechanism, a roughly $3bn fixed-cost base rising slightly with North American regionalisation, an explicit and declining tariff cost line, Hormuz as a timing variable, and working capital at about 40% of revenue.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

D5018th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: EPS is barely growing now and is expected to grow only modestly: about 5% a year from FY2025 to FY2028 on consensus, on 3-4% a year revenue growth. Durability is low to moderate, because the growth rests on US pricing and offshore mix, and no longer on share-count reduction.

  • History. On the FactSet adjusted, diluted basis, EPS was $0.65 (FY2019), -$0.18 (FY2020), $0.97 (FY2021), $2.23 (FY2022), $3.32 (FY2023), $1.89 (FY2024) and $1.86 (FY2025). On the brokers' basis FY2023 to FY2025 it was $3.33, $1.80 and $1.83. In FY2025 net income fell about 5% but EPS rose about 1%, because the share count fell about 7% through buybacks. That support has now gone.
  • Consensus. EPS $1.86 for FY2026 (+1.9%), $2.06 for FY2027 (+10.7%), $2.11 for FY2028 (+2.2%), and $1.98 for FY2029 (-6.1%, one broker). Revenue: $11,892m (-0.7%), $12,620m (+6.1%), $13,227m (+4.8%). From FY2025 to FY2028 that is EPS growth of about 4.9% a year, revenue about 3.4% and EBITDA about 3.9%.
  • The range is wide. For FY2027 EPS, brokers range from $1.81 (Zephirin) to $2.50 (Morgan Stanley). Redburn expects EPS to rise from $1.96 to $2.70 by 2028, about 28% above consensus for FY2028, on realised tube prices of about $3,277/t in 2028. Morgan Stanley models $3,410/t.
  • Durability. EPS is a function of realised price on a largely fixed cost base (C-75). Holding EPS growth depends on the US price staying up after the distributor refill ends, and on tariffs staying. The step-change from FY2026 to FY2027 is likely. FY2028 and beyond is a coin-flip between normalisation (the shape of consensus) and an extended upcycle (Redburn's view).

The model should carry two EPS paths beyond FY2027, and should not assume buyback accretion in the base case.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4520th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Scope is limited. Tenaris generates far more cash than it can put into high-return growth inside its core. This makes it a cash harvester whose fair multiple is set by its cash yield and cyclicality, not by reinvestment.

  • Organic. Capex of about $534m to $602m a year (consensus) is about equal to depreciation of about $610m, so mostly maintenance, modernisation and decarbonisation (about 30% of capex; $239m in one year; more than $700m over four years). Capex is about 26% of operating cash flow (Bernstein). The high-return projects are small: the Suriname base, Rig Direct hubs, the third Argentine fracturing fleet ($110m), Sault Ste. Marie (subsidised by CAD 76.2m of federal money), Koppel's restart ($2.1m) and digital services. Tariff-driven localisation earns its return by avoiding the tariff, not by growth.
  • Inorganic. Small, cheap tuck-ins (Artrom €86m). A large deal is constrained by antitrust in an oligopoly (Benteler was abandoned on antitrust grounds).
  • Idle cash. About $3.5bn, roughly 12% of market cap, earns financial income but dilutes return on equity (FY2025 ROE about 12% against a pre-tax operating return of about 19%; see C-51).

For the fair multiple: Bernstein's "Age of Maturity, harvesting free cash flow" frames it well. A fair multiple should be anchored to through-cycle free cash flow and dividend yield (consensus FY2027 free cash flow yield about 6.5% at about $29.4bn market cap), with a premium for balance-sheet safety and a discount for cyclicality and minority-governance risk. Mid-term potential rises only if surplus cash is returned (buybacks or specials) or deployed in a downturn acquisition.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

C-6211th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns are good but not exceptional through the cycle (post-tax ROIC of about 12-16% at mid-cycle), very high at peaks (about 28% in FY2023) and near zero in deep troughs (FY2020). Returns on new capital are currently high, because the earnings recovery needs little new capital. Returns on equity are depressed by idle cash.

The figures are my approximations from the block (the inputs are named so the second set can rebuild them):

  • FY2025. Adjusted EBIT $2,295m, tax at 20.7%: NOPAT about $1,820m. Operating invested capital (equity of about $16.5bn plus minorities, less net cash including investments of $3.3bn, less associates of $1.6bn) is about $11.8bn. Post-tax ROIC is about 15%. Pre-tax return on operating capital employed is about 19%. Return on equity is about 12% (net income $1,933m on about $16.5bn). Return on tangible capital is higher by a few points; goodwill and intangibles from Hydril, Maverick and IPSCO are on the balance sheet, but no figure is in the files. The Business Foundations grid gives a starred 14% ROIC estimate.
  • Through the cycle. Post-tax ROIC was about 5% in FY2019, negative in FY2020, about 5% in FY2021, about 18% in FY2022, about 28% in FY2023 and about 16% in FY2024, an average near 12%. Bernstein records a 22% average ROE in the 2004-2013 upcycle. Vallourec targets 15% ROIC.
  • Return on new capital. Consensus FY2027 EBIT rises about $340m on extra working capital of about $290m (40% of revenue growth) and capex at depreciation. The incremental return is very high but cyclical, not structural. Recent acquisitions (Artrom, Shawcor) were bought cheaply, at high prospective returns. The historical record includes value destruction (Usiminas, the 2020 welded impairments).

For the model, keep ROIC at about 15-18% in FY2027-28 at consensus margins. A 20%+ ROIC needs Redburn-type pricing.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

C-5511th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Conversion is high through the cycle (about 100% of net income in FY2023-25 before dividends) but strongly counter-cyclical. Tenaris carries working capital of about 38-40% of revenue, so it releases cash in downturns and absorbs it in upturns. FY2026 conversion falls to about 70% on a working-capital build, and FY2027 recovers to about 90% on consensus.

  • Before dividends (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex; see reading note 2). FY2019 $1,243m (167% of net income), FY2020 $1,353m (net loss year), FY2021 -$19m, FY2022 $887m (35%), FY2023 $3,893m (99%), FY2024 $2,304m (113%), FY2025 $2,094m (108%). The 2022 figure is the precedent for an upturn: revenue +80%, net working capital built heavily, conversion 35%.
  • Working capital. Net working capital (receivables plus inventories less payables) was $5,343m at the end of 2023, fell to $4,528m in the second quarter of 2025, and rose to $4,808m by the second quarter of 2026, or 39.9% of trailing revenue against 37.3% in the third quarter of 2024. Operating working capital days were 143 in June 2026 against 128 a year earlier and 137 for 2025 (Vallourec 86; peer median about 90). Inventories were $3,716m. Drivers: the Rig Direct model structurally, plus buffers built during the Hormuz closure (shipments waiting) and receivables. Redburn cut FY2026 free cash flow 34% to $1,321m on receivables.
  • 2026 so far. First-half free cash flow was $899m (operating cash flow $1,136m less capex $236m). Consensus FY2026 free cash flow of $1,320m implies only about $420m in the second half. That is consistent with a receivables build as fourth-quarter volume passes 1mt and deferred Gulf shipments are invoiced, but it is low, and it is upside if working capital normalises.
  • Other items. Lease liabilities are small ($143m). There are Argentine dividend-repatriation frictions (MULC approvals; securities transactions at implicit rates). Customer finance risk: Pemex net exposure is 7%.

For the model: hold net working capital at about 38-40% of revenue, so each extra $1bn of revenue absorbs about $0.4bn of cash. Model the possible release (about $0.4bn to $0.5bn) as a scenario if days return to 128-130.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

B+704th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Margins are the highest in the cohort and sit mid-to-upper in Tenaris's own corridor. They fell to a cyclical low in the second quarter of 2026 and should recover through 2027, although on consensus not back above 25% EBITDA.

  • Annual (FactSet basis). Gross margin: 29.8% (FY2019), 18.9%, 28.4%, 39.4%, 41.5% (FY2023 peak), 34.8%, 34.2% (FY2025). EBITDA margin: 19.1%, 12.0%, 19.9%, 30.2%, 32.4%, 25.0%, 24.3%; company basis FY2025 24.2%. Adjusted EBIT margin: 11.7%, -1.2%, 10.8%, 25.1%, 28.7%, 19.9%, 19.2%.
  • Quarterly trend (FactSet EBITDA margin). 23.6% (first quarter 2025), 23.9%, 24.9%, 24.8%, 24.0% (first quarter 2026), 22.4% (second quarter 2026). Company basis for the second quarter of 2026: 21.9%. Gross margin fell from 34.0% to 32.5% over the same period. The causes: tariff cost reaching cost of goods sold through inventory, hot-rolled coil, Hormuz freight and the loss of high-margin Gulf mix, and lower fixed-cost absorption.
  • Consensus. EBITDA margin (brokers' basis) 23.0% (FY2026), 24.2%, 24.4%, 23.7% (FY2029, thin), against 24.0% actual for FY2025 on the same basis. Adjusted EBIT margin about 17.9%, 19.5% and 19.8%. Redburn has 25.6% for FY2027.
  • Peers (brokers' EBITDA margin, FY2025). Vallourec 21%, SBO 16%, Hunting 13%, Saipem 11%. Consensus has Vallourec at 23-24% by FY2027-28, level with Tenaris.

For the model: an EBITDA margin trough of about 22-23% in the second half of 2026, recovering to about 24-25% in FY2027 if US prices hold. The 25% ceiling of management's corridor needs Hormuz to reopen and tariff mitigation to work. Incremental EBITDA margins have historically been about 40% on revenue changes (FY2022 +43%, FY2023 +41%; decremental 36-40% in FY2020 and FY2025), and about 70% when the change is all price (FY2024, -72%).

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

A662nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The earnings base is clean at the operating level. The adjusted-to-reported gap is small in normal years, and I found no evidence of aggressive capitalisation. Three items below operating profit need attention, because consensus appears to assume more non-operating income than history supports.

  • Adjusted against reported. FY2025 EPS was $1.83 reported, $1.86 FactSet adjusted, $1.83 brokers' basis. FY2024 was $1.81 against $1.89; the gap was the $171m Usiminas/CSN provision in the second quarter of 2024 and a $67m partial reversal in the fourth quarter. FY2020 was -$0.54 against -$0.18 ($622m welded impairment). The Viewforth definition sits close to reported ($1,950m against $1,933m net income in FY2025).
  • Items to watch. 1. Non-operating income in consensus. Consensus pre-tax profit exceeds EBIT by about $288m (FY2026), $263m (FY2027) and $345m (FY2028), against $121m in FY2025, $51m in FY2024 and $270m in FY2023. The sources are financial income on about $3.5bn of cash (first half 2026: $83m), associates (Ternium at 11.46%, whose margin fell to 10% in 2025; Usiminas, which lost $521m in 2025), and FX results. Lower interest rates, cash distributions or associate losses would erode this. It is worth about $0.05 to $0.15 of EPS. 2. Tax rate. Reported 14.9-20.7% (FY2023-25). Consensus implies about 22-26% for tax plus minorities. Argentine and Mexican deferred-tax swings from peso devaluation are non-cash but cause volatility (the $346.6m charge in 2023). 3. Inventory accounting. Under FIFO in IFRS, cost changes reach margins later than in management's replacement-cost view. Rising costs flatter margins first and dent them later (the tariff effect in 2025-26). Bill-and-hold revenue recognition is permitted under conditions. There have been three years of derivative losses ($13m, $26m, $25m in the first half of 2026).
  • Leakage. Minorities are small ($256m). Related-party purchases are modest and disclosed. Dividends to the controlling holder are pro-rata, not leakage.

The second-quarter 2026 net income beat (+9-10%) came partly from financial results, a lower-quality beat than the in-line EBITDA.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

A822nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: There is none, and the balance sheet is a source of safety, not amplification. Net cash is about $3.56bn on the company basis (FactSet $2.34bn; about -0.8x EBITDA), lease liabilities are $143m, and net interest is income. EPS is not amplified by debt. The cost is that roughly 12% of market cap sits in cash earning about 4% pre-tax, which dilutes ROE, and future EPS is mildly exposed to falling interest rates. Economic leverage
