Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

–not yet rated

Memo topic 5, : the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1760C-4565P-0458
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?B606th of 25full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?B+654th of 25full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

B585th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Price is the swing factor in the forecasts and contributes most of the 2027 recovery: about +4% on the tube average selling price, about +$460m of revenue and about +$275m of EBITDA, which is roughly 60% of the 2027 EBITDA increase and about +1.5 margin points. It then contributes roughly nothing in 2028 (-0.5%), subtracts in 2029 (-2%, about -$255m of revenue and -$190m of EBITDA), and is flat in 2030. From 2025 to 2030 the nominal average selling price rises only about 3%, a real decline. The reason is that most of the 2025-26 US price gain is an industry rent from Section 232 and distributor restocking, not Tenaris's own pricing power. Tenaris's own pricing power is real but shows elsewhere: in its stable premium to the index (realised prices about 34% above it), in international contracts that hold through cycles, and in a margin floor of about 20%. It does not show in an ability to keep raising prices.

The evidence, by pricing mechanism.

Mechanism Share of sales Behaviour and lag My path
US index-linked OCTG (PipeLogix) 30-43% (Morgan Stanley 30-40%; Redburn about 43%) Realised prices follow the index with a lag of one to two quarters (Redburn) or six to 12 months (Bernstein). Index trough about $2,110/t (August 2024), about $2,574/t mid-2026, +16% year to date by September 2026, with Jefferies looking for about $2,850/t by end-2026 Realised +8-10% in 2027 on 2026; flat in 2028 with a fade in the second half; -5% to -6% in 2029
International NOC and IOC contracts About 35-40% Formula pricing linked to raw materials, lag of two to three quarters (Q1 2026 call); "highly stable"; spot tenders softer (Middle East and North Sea -2% month on month in late 2025) Flat nominal
Offshore premium (Brazil, Guyana, Suriname, Black Sea) About 20% (overlaps the above) Two-to-three-year qualification barriers; firm; Vallourec competes hard (Sepia 2, Guyana line pipe) +1-2% a year to 2028 through mix
Argentina and local tenders About 15-19% Premium eroding under liberalisation: Welspun won the SESA pipeline at $203m against Tenaris's $296m; Rocca later said Tenaris cut 24% from a bid -2% to -3% on line pipe; OCTG steady
Welded (US and Canada) Minor Cost pass-through only: hot-rolled coil +20-23%; Korean and Taiwanese imports largely undeterred; welded imports +81% month on month in July 2026 Pass-through, no margin gain

Why the US gain is mostly rent, and why that matters for 2028-29.

  1. Vallourec, which sells only through distributors and has no Rig Direct, got the same index lift.
  2. The gain coincides with imports falling 34-40% after the tariff doubled to 50% (June 2025) and moved to full customs value (6 April 2026).
  3. The distributors' PipeLogix price-outlook index jumped from 29% to 100% between February and March 2026, the classic restocking signal, with inventories at the bottom of the five-to-six-month range (Morgan Stanley).
  4. Bernstein estimates that relaxing the tariff would cut Tenaris's US EBITDA by about 17%.

Restocking is self-limiting: once distributors hold five to six months of supply, the reinforcing loop ends. In the base case that happens during 2027, so the realised price peaks in late 2027 or early 2028. That is why my 2028 price contribution is about zero and 2029 negative. This is a cyclical fade, not a collapse.

How much price Tenaris keeps in a downturn or after tariff removal.

  • Precedent: 2024. Average selling price fell 15% in the first nine months of 2024 as distributors destocked. The EBITDA margin held at about 24-25% (FactSet), because the 34% premium and international contracts held.
  • Tariff removal: a 15-25% fall in the US index on 30-43% of sales cuts group revenue by about 5% to 10%, partly offset after about two quarters by $560m-600m a year of tariff cost removed. The net EBITDA effect ranges from about -$250m (Bernstein's -17% of US EBITDA) to about -$600m (first set). The first two quarters are the worst, because price falls before the tariffed inventory clears.
  • Jefferies' downside, US seamless at $2,000/t by early 2027 with international prices down 15%, would put group EBITDA towards $2.3bn to $2.5bn.

Contribution to margin. Price adds about +1.5 points to the 2027 margin. It subtracts about -0.3 point in 2028 (lag effect as costs keep rising while realised price plateaus) and about -1.2 points in 2029. The margin effect of price is larger than its revenue effect, because a price change carries only partly offsetting input-cost changes, and those come with a 6-12 month lag.

Where I differ from the first set and from consensus. The first set held international prices flat in real terms. I hold them flat in nominal terms, a mild real decline, because Middle East spot pricing softened, the Saudi national champion and Chinese mills are taking share, and Morgan Stanley's October preview is titled "Saudi Slows". I do not include Jefferies' further 5-10% upside beyond end-2026 levels, because it rests on restocking continuing. Consensus North America revenue rises 9% in FY2027 ($5,878m to $6,433m), close to my view. Consensus has FY2028 Tubes revenue flat, which implicitly agrees with my plateau. The bull cases (Redburn's $3,277/t realised in 2028; Morgan Stanley $3,410/t) need the US price leg to persist to 2028, and the evidence on restocking and capacity response does not support that as a base case.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7555P-0158
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

C559th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage is material. Fixed cash costs are about $3bn (about 25% of revenue). A 1% change in volume moves EBITDA by about 2%, and a 1% change in average price (all else equal) moves EBITDA by about 4%. Over FY2026 to FY2028 this creates an EBITDA range of roughly ±20-25% around consensus for plausible ±10% revenue outcomes.

  • Structure. Bernstein puts variable contribution at about 49% of the average selling price. On FY2025 revenue of $11,981m and EBITDA of $2,899m, that implies contribution of about $5.9bn and fixed cash costs of about $3.0bn, including SG&A of about $1.9bn to $2.0bn (second quarter 2026: $484m). Volume operating leverage (contribution divided by EBITDA) is about 2.0x. Price leverage (revenue divided by EBITDA) is about 4x. Because only about 30-43% of sales reprice off the US index, a 10% index move changes group revenue by about 3-4% and EBITDA by about 12-17%, after one to two quarters, before input-cost offsets.
  • History confirms it. FY2020: revenue -29%, EBITDA -56% (36% decremental, cushioned by cutting fixed costs by 25%, about $220m a year). FY2022: revenue +80%, EBITDA +174%. FY2024: revenue -16%, EBITDA -35% (72% decremental, as price fell). FY2025: revenue -4%, EBITDA -7%. The second quarter of 2026 against the first: revenue -$133m, EBITDA -$86m (about 65%, Gulf mix and logistics).
  • Changes now. Regionalising North American production (Sault Ste. Marie, Koppel, more Bay City shifts) raises fixed costs and therefore leverage. This helps in a US upturn and hurts if rigs fall below about 540-550 (the "margin trap" scenario).
  • The consensus incremental margin. About 44% on FY2027 revenue growth ($728m of revenue, $321m of EBITDA) and about 30% for FY2028. Redburn's FY2027 view has a similar incremental margin (about 44%) on much larger revenue growth (+$1.8bn). So the debate is about revenue (price and volume), not about incremental margin.

The range is wide but asymmetric in timing: upside arrives with a lag through the index, downside through volume can arrive within a quarter.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C587th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Operating leverage is material but smaller, and more "semi-fixed", than a static calculation suggests. It will make EBITDA rise about twice as fast as revenue in 2027 (+8.5% revenue, +17% EBITDA), hold EBITDA flat in 2028 as price stops rising, and make EBITDA fall about three times as fast as revenue in 2029 (-3% revenue, -9% EBITDA). Against 2020-25, when revenue swung between -29% and +80% and EBITDA between -56% and +174%, the next four years are a narrow band, because the drivers are a mid-cycle price recovery and offshore volume, not a boom or a collapse. The base case keeps every year inside management's 20-25% EBITDA margin corridor (22.5% to 24.5%), against 12% to 32% over 2019-25.

The cost structure, refined. I disagree in degree with the first set's figure of about $3bn of fixed cash costs and about 2.0x volume leverage, which is the top of the plausible range.

  • Bernstein (22 July 2025) puts the fixed cost base at $2,649m in 2024: $1,729m labour, $632m depreciation and $288m other SG&A. Excluding depreciation, fixed cash costs were about $2.0bn.
  • The first set derived about $3.0bn from Bernstein's other figure, a variable contribution of about 49% of the average selling price. The two Bernstein figures are not consistent with each other on group revenue, probably because the 49% is a per-tonne tube margin that excludes services, freight and SG&A.
  • My resolution: fixed cash costs are $2.0bn to $3.0bn. Volume leverage (contribution divided by EBITDA) is therefore 1.7x to 2.0x, and the incremental EBITDA margin on extra volume is about 41% to 49%. I use 45%.
  • Price leverage before input costs is about 4x (revenue divided by EBITDA). Only 30-43% of sales reprice off the US index, though, and input costs follow with a lag: Bernstein finds an 80% correlation between billet and seamless prices with about a 12-month lag, and scrap and coil are up 20-30%. So I assume 55-65% of a price rise reaches EBITDA in the first year, rising towards 80% only if input costs stall.

Why "semi-fixed" matters more than the static ratio. Tenaris's fixed costs ratchet with the cycle. They rose 55% from 2021 to 2024 while revenue rose 92%, with headcount up 14% and more than 4,000 shop-floor hires (Bernstein). In 2020 management cut them 25%, about $230m a year, within about three quarters, at a severance cost of $81m. The consequence for the model is asymmetry:

  • Upturn incremental margins are about 40-45%, not the 49%+ the static ratio implies, because costs come back with volume. FY2022 was +43% and FY2023 +41%.
  • Downturn decremental margins are about 36-40% when the fall is in volume and cushioned by cuts (FY2020). They are about 70% when the fall is in price (FY2024: revenue -$2.3bn, EBITDA -$1.7bn).
  • The next downturn will be mainly a price downturn (the US protection-and-restocking leg), so the 2029 decremental should be closer to the price case. I use about 73%.

What is new: regionalisation raises the breakeven. The Koppel heat-treatment and finishing restart (January 2026, more than 80 hires), extra Bay City shifts, more than 1,300 employees in Canada, the $90m Pennsylvania programme (completing in the second half of 2027) and the CAD 306m Sault Ste. Marie modernisation all add fixed cost and depreciation. The files do not quantify the total. My estimate is $50m to $100m a year of fixed cash cost plus $20m to $30m of depreciation. That is why depreciation rises from $610m to $650m in my inputs. These investments earn their return by avoiding the billet tariff, not by growth. They raise leverage in both directions: they help while the US is busy and protected, and hurt if US rigs fall below about 540-550 for two quarters, the "margin trap" in the first set.

How it shapes the line items.

Year Revenue change EBITDA change Implied incremental margin Main mechanism
FY2020 (actual) -29% -56% 36% Volume collapse, $230m cost cut
FY2022 (actual) +80% +174% 43% Price and volume boom
FY2024 (actual) -16% -35% 72% Price fall
FY2025 (actual) -4% -7% about 40% Mixed price and volume
FY2026 +1.7% -4% negative Tariff cost through inventory, Hormuz freight and lost Gulf mix on flat revenue
FY2027 +8.5% +17% 45% Price drop-through, volume, partial Gulf return
FY2028 +2.5% +0.4% about 4% Volume and Gulf offset by the US price beginning to fade
FY2029 -3.0% -9% 73% Price-led US normalisation
FY2030 +2.0% +4% 48% Volume, cost actions

The 2027 EBITDA bridge, so the Consumer can test each piece (+$462m in total):

  • Price (tube average selling price +4%, about +$460m revenue at 60% drop-through): +$275m.
  • Middle East return (about +$200m revenue at 55% including logistics savings): +$110m.
  • Other volume (US, offshore, Argentina and the Other segment, about +$226m revenue at 45%): +$100m.
  • Artrom consolidation (about $150m revenue at about 10%): +$15m.
  • Net tariff mitigation: +$80m.
  • Fixed-cost inflation and regionalisation: -$80m.
  • Input-cost catch-up (scrap and coil lag, welded squeeze, Nucor coil renewal): -$40m.

Sensitivities on the 2027 base.

  • 1% on group average selling price: about $125m revenue, $95m EBITDA, $0.075 EPS.
  • 1% on volume: about $130m revenue, $60m EBITDA, $0.045 EPS.
  • 10% on the US seamless index: $450m to $560m of revenue and $360m to $450m of EBITDA at full drop-through, $0.28 to $0.35 of EPS. It arrives over one to two quarters (Bernstein: six to 12 months).
  • One quarter of Gulf deferral: about $130m revenue, $85m EBITDA, $0.065 EPS.

Cone and skew. On these sensitivities the plausible FY2027 EBITDA range is about $2.6bn to $3.55bn around my $3.24bn. Upside arrives with a one-to-two-quarter lag through the index. Downside through volume can arrive within a quarter. Downside through a policy-driven price fall arrives faster than the tariff-cost relief, because inventory of about 140 days keeps the tariffed cost in cost of goods sold for two quarters. Read against history, the band is narrow: the forecast years sit between the FY2025 and FY2024 margin outcomes, not between the FY2020 and FY2023 extremes.

5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7682P-0255
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?A822nd of 25full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

D5514th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: There is no financial leverage, and the balance sheet will become more over-capitalised, not less. In the base case, which returns cash only through dividends, company-basis net cash rises from $3.3bn at end-2025 to about $4.05bn by end-2027 and about $5.2bn by end-2030, about 1.3x to 1.7x EBITDA. EPS is not amplified by debt. Instead it gains a modest and rate-sensitive stream of interest income, about $125m to $155m a year before tax, and loses the share-count support that buybacks gave in 2024-25. The real financial-leverage question for the forecasts is therefore not gearing but capital return. A decision to return the surplus would add about 1% a year to EPS per $500m of annual buybacks. Not making one dilutes return on equity towards 10.5-12%.

The analysis behind it.

  • Debt and cost of debt. Total debt is about $450m (FactSet, end-June 2026), mostly short-term local facilities. Lease liabilities are $143m. There is no refinancing, covenant or rating risk. Net interest expense on the FactSet line was $47m in FY2025, more than covered by interest income.
  • Cash composition matters for the model. The company's net cash ($3,564m at June 2026) includes about $1.2bn of non-current investments, mostly bonds. FactSet's figure is $2,336m. The Argentine subsidiaries hold $204m of dollar sovereign bonds that are not freely remittable. For EPS, the whole portfolio earns income. For the enterprise-value bridge, the Consumer must state which definition is used, because it changes FY2027 EV/EBITDA by about 0.4x.
  • Interest income and rate sensitivity. First-half 2026 finance income was $117.7m and the net financial result a gain of $83m. On a portfolio rising from about $4bn to $5bn, a 100 basis-point move in dollar rates changes pre-tax income by about $40m to $50m, or about $0.03 of EPS (1.5%). The company's own disclosure (100 basis points = $3.5m) covers only the borrowing side, so it understates the sensitivity. Falling dollar rates are a mild EPS headwind I have built in: the financial result, excluding associates, edges from about $150m in 2026 to $125m in 2027 before the growing cash balance lifts it back to about $155m by 2030.
  • Balance-sheet path. Equity rises from about $16.5bn (end-2025) to about $17.9bn (2027) and $19.4bn (2030) on a dividend payout of about 70-76% of free cash flow after leases. Return on equity is about 12.4% in 2027, 12.0% in 2028 and 10.5-10.8% in 2029-30, against about 12% in FY2025 and 22% average in the 2004-13 upcycle. Net cash grows to about 27% of equity.
  • What would change the balance sheet. (a) Buybacks: each $500m a year at about $29 a share retires about 17m shares (1.7%), less about $14m of after-tax interest forgone, so EPS gains about 1% a year net. That is far less than the 4-7% a year the 2024-25 programme delivered, because that programme ran at three times the pace. (b) A large counter-cyclical acquisition in a downturn, which Rocca has done before (IPSCO, 2020). There is no evidence of one, and an antitrust ceiling exists in an oligopoly (Benteler abandoned). (c) Legal one-offs: the CSN/Usiminas indemnity, where Confab's maximum is about BRL 676m ($130m) at June 2026 and part is already provided, and the Petrobras and prosecutors' claim of about $40m. Neither threatens the balance sheet.
  • Free-float arithmetic. I add this to the first set's hypothesis (a). The Milan rule requires at least 25% free float. With the controlling holder at about 64-68% of the capital, the share count can fall by roughly 9% to 15% (about 90m to 150m shares, $2.6bn to $4.3bn at today's price) before the holder reaches 75%. Bernstein's projection of 75% in 2027 and 79% in 2028 assumes buybacks continue at the 2024-25 pace of about $1.4bn a year. So the constraint limits the pace of buybacks; it does not prevent them. The pause is therefore more likely a preference of the controlling holder (cash dividends fund the Techint group's heavy investment in Vaca Muerta and Pesquería) and caution about the cycle than a legal necessity. This matters for the fourth set: a restart is possible without a delisting debate, but its timing depends on the controller's wishes, which are not disclosed.

For the model. Net financial items positive and slightly rate-sensitive; no buybacks in the base case; net cash accumulating. The upside scenario is a buyback of about $0.5bn a year from the May 2027 annual meeting (EPS +1% a year) or a special dividend. The cost of the conservative balance sheet is a return on equity drifting towards 11%, which caps the multiple the market will pay for a cash harvester.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7755P-0352
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C-5510th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: In a downturn about $200m to $300m of fixed cost can realistically be cut (7-10% of fixed cash costs), as in 2020. In the current upturn the realistic reductions are tariff mitigation and the end of Hormuz logistics costs, which together are the largest addressable item (up to several hundred million dollars a year). Labour and plant fixed costs are only partly cuttable because of unionisation and local-content commitments.

By cost line (FY2025 cash costs about $9.1bn):

  • Raw materials (scrap, pig iron, HBI, DRI: 20% of pipe costs; bought-in billets, coil and plate: 14%). These are variable. They can be reduced through the metal mix (Siderca's pellet use fell 25% in 2025), scrap integration and procurement scale (Exiros), but are set by markets.
  • Tariff cost on imported billets and bars ($140m to $150m a quarter gross). Reducible by switching to domestic bars (Nucor), more Koppel and Bay City steel, the Canadian exemption and Sault Ste. Marie. Perhaps a third to a half over 2026-27; the files do not quantify this and it needs verifying.
  • Logistics and freight (SG&A up to $484m in the second quarter of 2026, with Hormuz re-routing). Transitory, and reversible on reopening.
  • Labour. Unionisation is 71%; there are restrictions on Argentine lay-offs (SIAT mediation) and wage indexation in Argentina and Brazil. Precedent: headcount cut by 3,260 in 2024 and about 1,000 in 2025; a 25% fixed-cost cut in 2020; the $200m programme with half delivered in four quarters.
  • Energy. Argentina is largely fixed and cheap now (wind farms). Europe and the US remain exposed.
  • Plant fixed costs. Idling is possible (Machachi, 2020 plant idlings). Automation and AI are cutting cycle times by up to 30% (Bernstein).

For the model: no cost programme in the base case. Tariff mitigation should be modelled as an explicit declining cost line, and the end of Hormuz logistics costs as linked to the reopening scenario.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C529th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: No cost programme is under way that would lift margins structurally, and the $200m programme of 2024-25 has already been delivered and sits in the base. What will reach the line items is a set of specific, mostly external reductions: tariff mitigation, the end of Hormuz re-routing costs, and in time automation. These reduce costs by about $140m in 2027 and about $270m by 2028 against the 2026 run-rate, before cost inflation. Against them runs underlying fixed-cost inflation of about $60m to $80m a year: wages in Argentina and Brazil indexed to inflation, more North American staff, and Bernstein's finding that fixed costs "capture inflation over time". So the net gain reaching EBITDA is about +$70m in 2027 and about +$130m in 2028, worth about 0.5 to 1.0 margin point. In a downturn (2029 in the base case), the 2020 playbook would add a further $100m to $230m of cuts within three to four quarters, but at a severance cost.

By item, with timing.

Item Gross annual size Net reaching EBITDA (my estimate) When Confidence
Section 232 tariff on imported billets and bars $560m-600m gross cost a year ($140m-150m a quarter, Redburn 9 March 2026) About +$80m in 2027, +$150m in 2028, +$200m from 2029 Nucor Memphis bars since January 2026 (one-year framework); Koppel melt-shop upgrade in the second half of 2027; Sault Ste. Marie over 2027-28; Canadian retroactive exemption since December 2025 Medium-low
Hormuz re-routing and freight SG&A $484m in Q2 2026 against $453m in Q4 2025, so about $30m a quarter About +$60m in 2027, about +$120m a year from 2028 (inside the Middle East drop-through in P-01) One to two quarters after reopening Medium
Labour and automation Labour cost $2,081m (FY2025). Bernstein sees the workforce falling from 25,874 (2024) to 23,330 (2028) and labour cost 10-15% lower over five years About zero net to 2028, because indexation and regionalisation hiring offset it; a modest gain after 2028 Gradual Low
Fixed-cost inflation About 2-3% of fixed cash costs a year -$60m to -$80m a year Continuous Medium

Why tariff mitigation saves only about a third of the gross cost. Replacing imported bars with domestic steel removes the 50% duty but pays a domestic price that is itself inflated by protection: US hot-rolled coil about $1,045/t and Nucor spot about $1,301/t in September 2026, and Deutsche Bank's US steel spreads at $843/t in October 2026. Koppel's own steel avoids both, but it needs the $90m upgrade, which runs into the second half of 2027, and full utilisation to absorb its fixed cost. Redburn already expects the $140m-150m a quarter to persist through 2026. The files do not quantify how much of Tenaris's bar requirement Nucor covers. This is the least evidenced number in my model, and the Consumer should treat the mitigation path as a range of +$40m to +$150m in 2027.

What is not cuttable. About two-thirds of the workforce is unionised. Argentina's provincial ministry forced the reversal of 150 SIAT lay-offs for mediation in July 2026. Wages in Argentina and Brazil are indexed. North America is flexible: in April 2020 Tenaris shut Koppel, Ambridge and Baytown temporarily, and Canada has variable work arrangements. Raw materials (scrap and metallics about 20% of pipe cost; bought-in billets, coil and plate about 14%) are variable and set by markets.

Peer precedents and what they say about Tenaris. Vallourec's €230m EBITDA gain from closing German mills and its Brazil programme (over €150/t) were self-help turnarounds from a broken base, and that is why Vallourec has caught up. Tenaris has no equivalent pool of loss-making capacity to close. Its cost story is defensive. This supports my view that Tenaris's margin advantage over Vallourec will not reopen through cost.

For the model. No programme line. Net cost tailwind of about +0.5 margin point in 2027 and a further +0.5 in 2028, most of it from tariff mitigation, which is already embedded in the margin inputs. If Section 232 were relaxed, the tariff cost would disappear within about two quarters (inventory lag), but the price would fall faster (see P-04).

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4762P-0552
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C+628th of 25full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C-5210th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Reinforcing loops dominate until about the end of 2027, and balancing loops gain the upper hand during 2028. That timing gives the forecasts their shape: margin recovery in 2027, a plateau in 2028, a dip in 2029. The fast loops (US price, restocking, tariffs) are the ones that turn. The slow loops (Rig Direct lock-in, the global seamless deficit, industry discipline led by Vallourec) keep reinforcing through the horizon, and they are why the dip in 2029 is modest (margin 22.5%, not 20%). One loop, the Strait of Hormuz linked to oil, works as a self-hedge and narrows the cone in both directions. One loop, cash accumulation, creates pressure for a capital-return decision that is not in the base case.

The loops, their state and their effect on the line items.

Loop Type State now (dated evidence) Expected turn Effect on line items
1. US tariff, imports, price and distributor restocking Reinforcing now, turning balancing Imports -34% to -40%; PipeLogix outlook 100% (March 2026); inventories at the bottom of the five-to-six-month range; seamless +16% year to date (September 2026); seamless-welded spread above $500/t During 2027, as distributors refill and welded imports respond (+81% month on month in July 2026). Balancing capacity arrives: Welspun's US high-frequency welded mill (done March 2026) and longitudinal-weld mill (December 2026); Tenaris's Koppel upgrade (second half of 2027); U.S. Steel's $475m Fairfield quench-and-temper line (second quarter of 2029); Nippon Steel's $11bn US programme to 2028 Price +8-10% on US index-linked sales in 2027, flat in 2028, -5% to -6% in 2029. The main driver of the margin path
2. Rig Direct installed base, switching costs and share Reinforcing, slow 90% of US sales; offshore extension to Suriname (June 2026 base, 47kt GranMorgu casing) and Guyana (ten-year contract); 70 rigs on remote monitoring Continues; risk of multi-sourcing by majors (Chevron) Volume 1-2 points a year above the rig count; price premium about 34% held; underpins the 20% margin floor
3. Capacity exits, seamless deficit and new capacity Reinforcing to 2028, balancing after Global seamless capacity flat at 21.2mt since 2018; 3% deficit, the first since 2013 (Bernstein); Vallourec closed 685kt, U.S. Steel 1.1mt Jindal Saw's 300kt Abu Dhabi mill (fiscal years 2028-29); Saudi national champion; Indian additions too small to matter; customer qualification takes two to three years International prices stable to 2028; some share loss in Saudi Arabia; supports the case that 2029 is a dip, not a slump
4. Hormuz, oil price and operator budgets Balancing (self-hedging) Strait largely closed (31 transits a day against 138, late September 2026); Brent $105-111; operators' 2027 budgets only about 1% above consensus (Morgan Stanley, 14 October 2026), so high oil is not producing more activity Reopening returns about $130m a quarter of Gulf revenue but may lower oil and trim 2027-28 US shale budgets Narrows the cone: neither outcome is all good or all bad. Base: reopening mid-2027, no change to US activity
5. Upcycle working capital, cash and capital return Balancing on cash; reinforcing on EPS only if cash is returned 2022: working capital absorbed $2.1bn and operating cash flow fell to $1.17bn. Now days are 143 against 128, partly from Hormuz stock This upturn is price-led and modest, so absorption is small (about $0.2bn in 2027) and the Hormuz stock releases on reopening Free cash flow about $1.9bn to $2.1bn a year; net cash builds; pressure for a buyback or special in 2027-28
6. Vallourec's margin catch-up and industry discipline Reinforcing for industry discipline Vallourec at margin parity; "value over volume"; volumes capped at 1.7mt; €650m returned in 2026; 80-100% of cash returned; ArcelorMittal cut its stake to 17.3%, removing takeover speculation Stable unless demand falls sharply or Nippon Steel and U.S. Steel push volume Supports price discipline in premium products; removes the prospect of Tenaris regaining a margin lead
7. Upcycle hiring, fixed-cost ratchet and downturn decrementals Balancing over the cycle Fixed costs +55% (2021-24); regionalisation adding staff in 2026 Bites in the next price downturn Raises the 2029 decremental margin to about 73%

How the loops combine on the line items.

  • Revenue: loops 1, 2 and 4 add about 8.5% in 2027. Loop 1's turn takes 2028 growth down to about 2.5% and 2029 to about -3%.
  • EBITDA margin: loops 1 and 3 lift it to 24.5% in 2027. Loops 1 and 7 together take it to 22.5% in 2029. Loops 2, 3 and 6 keep it above the 20% floor.
  • Cash: loop 5 keeps free cash flow at about $1.9bn to $2.1bn a year with little working-capital drag, and builds cash.

Leading indicators that would show loop 1 turning from reinforcing to balancing earlier than my base case.

  • US OCTG imports up more than 20-25% over three months (monthly Census data).
  • Distributor months of supply above six.
  • Two consecutive monthly falls in the PipeLogix seamless index.
  • US rigs below about 550 for two quarters, with WTI below $65.
  • Hot-rolled coil prices falling, which triggers destocking.
  • Any Section 232 relaxation, exemption or trade deal (watch the period after the 3 November 2026 midterms).

Indicators that would extend the reinforcing phase into 2028:

  • Hormuz staying closed with Brent above $100 and US rigs rising towards 600 or more.
  • Continued anti-dumping actions (Austria, Taiwan, the UAE; Enforce and Protect Act findings).
  • Delays to the new US mills.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set treated 2028 as "a coin-flip between normalisation and an extended upcycle". My reading of the loops favours normalisation starting in 2028 and showing in 2029, about 60:40. Restocking ends by construction, three named US capacity additions are dated, and operators' budgets are not responding to $100+ oil.

Guidance conservatism

Guidance

Layer 1 · Guidance

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Guidance is conservative, moderately specific and reliable. FY2026 guidance (second half in line with the first, about $12.1bn of revenue and $2.77bn of EBITDA) is slightly above the FactSet median consensus and matches most named brokers. It deliberately excludes any Hormuz reopening, and it gives only qualitative credit for the US price increases now reaching the income statement. Management upgrades through results, not targets, and its informal commentary is more positive than its formal guidance. The second set should treat guided FY2026 numbers as the base and the 20-25% margin corridor as the operating range. It should expect any guidance surprise in February 2027 (the first-half 2027 outlook) to be on the upside if US prices hold. It should also note that capital-return guidance is the most uncertain piece of investor relations.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C588th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: For FY2026, guidance sits slightly above the FactSet median consensus (about 1-2% on revenue and EBITDA) and in line with most of the named brokers. For FY2027 there is no formal guidance. The qualitative guidance (a 20-25% margin corridor, offshore momentum to 2028, a fourth-quarter volume step-up and rising prices) is consistent with, or above, consensus.

  • FY2026 arithmetic. In the first half of 2026 revenue was $6,067m and EBITDA $1,384m (company basis: $3,100m and $735m in the first quarter, $2,967m and $649m in the second). "Second half in line with first half" implies about $12.1bn of revenue and about $2.77bn of EBITDA for the year. The FactSet median is $11,892m and $2,732m (mean $11,971m and $2,746m), which implies a second half about 4% below the first. The brokers FactSet names mostly sit at $12.06bn to $12.41bn of revenue and $2.75bn to $2.83bn of EBITDA, which matches guidance. Unnamed or stale estimates pull the median down.
  • Why consensus is below. Haircuts for third-quarter seasonality and mix, a possibly longer Hormuz closure, hot-rolled coil costs and receivables, and the August Jefferies view that guidance carried a "3% risk to FY2026 consensus EBITDA of $2.86bn" (consensus has since fallen to that level).
  • FY2027. Consensus revenue is $12.6bn (+6.1%) and EBITDA $3.05bn (24.2% margin), within the corridor. Management's commentary of a Q4 above 1mt, price increases dropping through, offshore through 2028 and "upside in activity, sales and prices" implies upside, but no number.

For the model: FY2026 EBITDA of $2.75bn to $2.80bn is the guided base. The third-quarter print on 4 November should be about $650m of EBITDA (Jefferies: broadly in line with the second quarter), with the fourth quarter carrying the step-up.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

C628th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Current guidance is conservative once the change in input forces is taken into account.

  • Then and now. In 2024-25 Tenaris guided to, and delivered, margins of about 24% while input forces were negative (the US index falling to its trough, rigs falling, Aramco destocking). Now the input forces are positive or turning (the US index +16-20%, rigs bottoming, offshore spending up, Brent above $100). Yet near-term guidance is flat half on half, with the margin trough expected in the second half of 2026. It assumes Hormuz stays closed and gives only qualitative credit for price drop-through.
  • The corridor. 20-25% EBITDA margins against history of 12% (2020) to 32% (2023). The corridor is a through-cycle band, not a forecast, and the top of it (25%) is what Redburn expects for FY2027.
  • What is not conservative. Guidance does not discount further hot-rolled coil and scrap inflation, the Nucor coil renewal, or a policy reversal. These are the risks of an adverse input change.

Net: if input forces stay as they are, actual FY2026 and FY2027 results are more likely to be above guidance and the current consensus than below.

C-72

How specific is the guidance: built from stated building blocks, or vague about how results come about?

D5217th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: The guidance is moderately specific. It names the building blocks (volume direction, price direction, the Hormuz assumption, seasonality, capex) but does not quantify them by region or product, so the model must estimate the transmission itself.

What is specific: sales and EBITDA for the half against the prior half; fourth-quarter volume above 1mt; third-quarter seasonality (European maintenance) and mix; capex at about $600m a year; the margin corridor; the Hormuz assumption; deferred shipments to be invoiced within 70-90 days of navigability; about 50 US rigs to be added by year-end (management's expectation). What is vague: regional price and volume split; international contract pricing; tariff cost after mitigation; working-capital targets; capital-return policy after the pause. The second set will need to build price-volume-mix by region from external indices (PipeLogix, rig counts, offshore schedules) rather than from company building blocks.

C-85

To what extent is informal/qualitative company commentary above or below formal guidance and sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

C+627th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Informal commentary is above both formal guidance and consensus, especially for FY2027.

  • Informal statements. The new CEO expects "upside in activity, sales and prices" in North American onshore. Offshore operators are proceeding, giving "impeccable execution opportunities" and a strong backlog through 2028. Offshore deepwater spending is projected to reach about $120bn by 2028. The Middle East deferrals are "postponed rather than lost". International pricing is "highly stable". Fourth-quarter volumes will be above 1mt "on higher pricing". Management told analysts that US rigs had reached a cyclical bottom.
  • Formal. Second half flat on first half, Hormuz assumed closed.
  • Why the gap. It reflects the company's conservative guidance culture (C-70) and the uncertainty around Hormuz and US activity. The informal tone reflects what management sees in order books: offshore project schedules, Rig Direct demand, price increases being implemented.

For the model: the informal commentary supports a FY2027 consensus revenue figure (+6%) as a cautious base, with upside towards the Redburn and Morgan Stanley cases if Hormuz reopens and US pricing holds.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

D5013th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Five features are material for modelling and for reading consensus.

  1. Half-yearly guidance, quarterly results. Each guidance step lands at the full-year results (February) and the half-year results (August). Quarterly calls add direction. The third-quarter results on 4 November 2026 and the full-year results in February 2027 are the next guidance events. The February results are also when the final dividend, and any buyback, will be set.
  2. Multiple listings and units (Milan shares, NYSE ADS equal to two shares, Mexican listing). Broker targets in dollars are often per ADS. Consensus per-share data are per share.
  3. Alternative performance measures. The company's net cash includes non-current investments, and its EBITDA definition differs slightly from FactSet's (FY2025: company $2,899m, FactSet operating $2,911m, brokers' basis $2,881m).
  4. Opaque international pricing, which limits visibility (only 30-40% of revenue on transparent public pricing).
  5. The unexplained capital-allocation change, communicated through a dividend announcement rather than a policy statement. Investor relations has not set out a buyback framework since the pause.

Uncommon positives: transparent currency-sensitivity disclosure, and a conservative-guidance culture that has made consensus a floor.

C-74

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

Judgement: Estimates were cut deeply in 2024 and have since been rebuilt, so they are not at a kitchen-sink base now. Recent revisions are positive over six to twelve months and flat to slightly negative over one to three months. Guidance has been trimmed for Hormuz but not rebased. It is sandbagged in the usual way rather than kitchen-sinked.

  • The revision path (FactSet mean, weekly). FY2026 EPS: +8.7% over 12 months, +4.1% over 6, -0.1% over 3, -1.0% over 1. FY2027: +17.7%, +9.2%, +1.7%, +1.3%. The series low for FY2027 was $1.52, so EPS is up about 40% from the trough. FY2028: +16.9%, +6.0%, -1.2%, +0.5%. The dashboard's FY2027 percentages (basis caveat in reading note 9) agree: EPS +4% over one month, +3% over three, +14% over six and +23% over twelve; EBITDA +4%, +5%, +11%, +17%; sales +1%, +2%, +7%, +9%. Target price -11% over one month (direction uncertain; possibly a currency artefact), +10% over three, +19% over six. Buy share is up 8 points over three months to 14%.
  • Breadth and direction. Positive over the medium term across EPS, EBITDA and sales. Momentum has flattened over three months, with FY2026 slipping on Hormuz and receivables, and FY2027 still edging up. Per the Wisdom Library's "composite signal masks component deterioration", the profit lines should be read separately from the headline score: the FY2026 profit lines are softening while FY2027 rises. FactSet does not supply counts of analysts revising up or down.
  • Guidance. Second-half 2026 guidance assumes Hormuz closed and is flat on the first half. That is conservative, not a reset.

For the later sets: there is no low base to beat dramatically. The upside surprise would come from price drop-through and a Hormuz reopening, not from an over-cut consensus.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

C559th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The product perimeter is narrow (Tubes 95% of sales, all energy-linked) and the geographic perimeter is wide (mills in about 20 countries, sales in more than 30). It is widening slightly (Romania, Suriname, Canada). This mix gives low diversification by end market (oil and gas drilling dominates) and high exposure to many political and currency regimes. Predictability is therefore moderate: good on structure and on offshore and NOC contracts, poor on the US price index, Hormuz, Argentine policy and tariffs.

Revenue weights: North America 46-49% (the US-heavy, index-linked part), South America 18% (Argentina about 19% of 2024 sales), and the Middle East, Europe and Asia for the rest. The major revenue countries are the US, Argentina, Canada, Saudi Arabia, Mexico and the UAE. Sales are over 80% priced in dollars, which helps. Only 30-40% of revenue is tied to transparent public pricing, which hurts forecasting precision. International contract margins (ADNOC, Petrobras) are undisclosed. For the model: build revenue by region and by pricing mechanism (US index-linked, international contract, Argentine local). This will be more predictive than a group-level approach.

Group judgement

Risks

D537th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable in their first half and not in their second, and the memo should say so in those words. To mid-2027 the case is unusually trackable for a cyclical: the two drivers that generate 85% of the FY2027 step-up (the US seamless index, now above $3,100/t and 9% ahead of what the inputs assumed, and the timing of a Hormuz reopening, which guidance assumes does not happen) are published monthly, lead the income statement by one to two quarters, and are both skewed favourably against guidance and the consensus median. Tenaris's demonstrated strengths (index capture with a stable 34% premium, Rig Direct retention, a 20% margin floor shown in 2016, 2020 and 2024, cost flexibility, 18 beats in 21 quarters) make the floor of the forecast credible and the near-term beat likely; the strengths it still has to acquire (US bar self-sufficiency, working-capital normalisation) move the margin by about half a point and the cash by $0.4-0.5bn, not the direction. From the second half of 2027 predictability falls sharply: the price leg is a policy and restocking rent whose end is dated by imports already rising in seamless, by named capacity additions and by a trade-policy decision point after 3 November; the Hormuz reopening that helps the Gulf line is the same event that lowers Brent and the 2028 US budget round; and operating leverage of about 4x on price with a 70% decremental turns a 5-6% price fade into a 10% EBITDA fall. The answers in the earlier sets are congruent on all of this, and the one place they jarred, the form and size of capital return, resolves into a larger ordinary dividend, which I have put into the inputs. The share price is 60% idiosyncratic and 40% energy-services beta, and its multiple is at the top decile of five years: delivery will be rewarded, but the model's return at the guideline multiple leans on a re-rating the evidence does not support. Net: a predictable 2027 beat of a stale consensus, inside a four-year path whose 2028-29 leg is a cyclical fade of uncertain depth; positive for an 18-month investment case, with the exit timing being the hard part.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C+664th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The expected change is plausible, and about four-fifths of it rests on strengths Tenaris has demonstrated through more than one cycle. The expected change is modest in historical terms: an EBITDA margin from 22.8% (first half of 2026) to about 24.5-25% in 2027 and EPS from $1.83 to about $2.15-2.25, within a 20-25% corridor the company has defended since 2024. The one-fifth that depends on strengths still to be acquired (self-sufficiency in US steel bars, working-capital normalisation) affects the margin's level by about half a point and its timing by two to four quarters, not the direction. The important qualification is a different one: plausibility rests on capability, but the level of the 2027 outcome rests on two external variables (the US index and Hormuz) that no capability controls. Delivery makes the floor plausible; it does not make the ceiling predictable.

Demonstrated, with dated evidence (T-D1; the first and second sets).

  • Capturing a rising index with a lag, and holding a premium to it. Realised prices about 34% above the PipeLogix seamless index (Bernstein, 22 July 2025). In 2018, after the first Section 232 tariff, Tenaris passed input inflation through fully (Morgan Stanley, 16 March 2018): gross margin rose from 30.3% to 31.1% and operating margin from 6.3% to 11.4% while it paid 25% duties on Mexican billets of about $50m a quarter (Jefferies, 2018). The mechanism the 2027 recovery relies on, index up, realised price up one to two quarters later, margin up after input costs lag, has worked in 2017-18 and 2021-22.
  • Customer retention through Rig Direct. 70% of US sales by the first quarter of 2025 and about 90% by late 2025; 530 rigs from 44 warehouses (September 2024). North American volumes decoupled from the rig count in 2023; North American sales rose 12% in the first half of 2026 while rigs were flat to down. No loss of a Rig Direct customer is reported.
  • Margin resilience in downturns. 13.9% reported EBITDA margin in 2016 on a 38% fall in sales; 12% reported and 14% adjusted in 2020 with $1.1bn of working capital released; 24.4% in 2024 against 32.7% in 2023 while sector margins compressed 400-600 basis points. The 20% floor the model uses in 2029 has three precedents behind it.
  • Cost flexibility. $230m of annualised fixed-cost savings within about three quarters in 2020 (18% headcount cut, 40% lower selling and administrative costs); more than half of a $200m programme delivered within four quarters by May 2025. The model's $50m of cuts in 2029 is conservative against this record.
  • Contract stability and NOC behaviour. No Gulf cancellations reported by Tenaris or Vallourec eight months into the closure; an Eastern Hemisphere backlog above $3bn in 2021 after the pandemic; ADNOC's $1.9bn contract extended; Saudi Steel Pipe won a SAR 177m Aramco order on 15 October 2026 during the blockade. The precedent that deferred NOC volume returns rather than disappears is good.
  • Beating conservative guidance. EPS beats in 18 of 21 quarters; first-quarter 2026 EBITDA +4% against consensus; second quarter in line on EBITDA, +9-10% on net income.

Needed, not yet demonstrated.

  • US steel bar self-sufficiency. Koppel makes about 430kt against a US requirement of about 1.57mt; about 1.14mt is bought externally, half of it historically imported; Vallourec makes 100% of its US steel at Youngstown (750kt). The $90m Koppel and Ambridge programme runs into the second half of 2027; Sault Ste. Marie (CAD 306m) over 2027-28. Progress is real (the tariff run-rate fell from about $150m to $110-120m a quarter by May 2026), but the remaining saving is capped because domestic bars are priced in a protected market (Nucor's 20-plus spot increases). The precedent (U.S. Steel's Fairfield EAF, 1.6m short tons for $215m) says domestic replacement works but takes years and capital. The model's net mitigation of +$80m in 2027 and +$150m in 2028 is consistent with this. If it slips a year, the 2027 margin is about 0.5 point lower.
  • Working-capital normalisation. 143 days against 128 a year earlier; a former ConocoPhillips supply-chain manager expects re-routing to take months, so days stay elevated through the first half of 2027. The model keeps 38.5% of revenue in 2027 and does not bank the $0.4-0.5bn release. This is a cash item, not an EPS item.
  • Geothermal and carbon capture. About 1% of 2027 revenue (Bernstein); zero failures across 500 Enel wells; a 175kt Aramco CCS order; but Vallourec holds the anchor contracts. Not in the model and not needed for it.

What the precedents say about speed and completeness. Recoveries from troughs have been price-led and fast once the index turns (2017-18: operating margin nearly doubled in a year; 2021-22: revenue +80%, EBITDA +174%), and they have faded when North American activity slowed (2019: operating income fell to $832m as sales slipped 5%). Completeness has depended on how long the policy and activity supports lasted, not on execution. That is exactly the shape of the base case: a fast 2027, a 2028 plateau, a 2029 fade.

Peers as a check. Vallourec's catch-up to margin parity came from closing German mills and a Brazil programme, which is self-help from a broken base; Tenaris has no such pool to harvest, so its improvement is cyclical and external, as the sets say. Hunting (asset-light, no steelmaking) cut its 2026 EBITDA guidance 7% on a Kuwait tender delay; SBO is a pure US land play. Tenaris is the most integrated and diversified of the group, which is why its forecast is the most plausible on capability and the least dependent on any one customer.

Conclusion. The thesis is built on what Tenaris does well: capture a protected index with a lag, keep its customers, hold a floor, flex costs. It does not need Tenaris to become something it is not. What it needs from outside is that the index stays high for about four more quarters and that the Strait reopens in 2027. Those are the questions for D-03 and D-04.

D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

D479th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The cone is narrow for the next two to three quarters and wide thereafter, and its skew turns over inside the 18 months. To mid-2027 the skew is upward: the October index is already above what the base assumes, realised prices follow it with a one-to-two-quarter lag, guidance excludes any Gulf return and gives only qualitative credit to price, and the company beats conservative guidance by habit. From the second half of 2027 the skew turns downward in size while staying roughly balanced in probability: the biggest monthly price gain since July 2008 is, by the industry's own history, a late-cycle signal; imports have already begun to respond in seamless; the trade-policy and oil-price tails are larger than the Gulf-timing upside. The result for FY2027, my revised base of about $3.34bn of EBITDA and $2.23 of EPS after minorities, is a plausible range of about $2.65bn to $3.65bn (-21% to +9%) and $1.70 to $2.45 of EPS, with a probability-weighted centre of about $3.27bn and $2.17 that sits almost exactly on the consensus mean. Predictability is therefore good for the first half of the 18 months, poor for the second half, and moderate overall.

The cone by period.

Period Plausible EBITDA range around my revised base Skew Why
Q3 2026 (reported 4 November) $630-670m against $642m (consensus $656m) Neutral Seasonal; Sakarya mix; HRC; largely known
Q4 2026 and Q1 2027 ±4% Up Index +26% year to date reaches invoicing; volume above 1mt guided; inventories lean; Hormuz still excluded from guidance
Q2-Q3 2027 -10% to +8% Up on price, balanced overall Peak realised price; Gulf return assumed from Q3 (timing could slip or come early); HRC pass-through; Nucor Hickman renewal
Q4 2027 to Q2 2028 -25% to +10% Down Restocking ends; imports and new capacity respond; operators' 2028 budgets set after any oil fall; post-midterm trade policy; fixed-cost ratchet

Scenarios for FY2027, revised from P-10 for the October data.

Scenario Probability What happens Revenue EBITDA (margin) EPS after minorities
Bull 30% Index holds above $3,000/t through 2027; inventories stay below average; Hormuz reopens in Q1 2027 and the catch-up lands in Q2-Q3; Section 232 intact about 14,000 about 3,650 (26.0%) about 2.45
Base (revised) 50% Index averages about $3,000/t, fading in H2 2027 as imports respond; Hormuz reopens mid-2027; mitigation on plan; HRC squeeze persists in welded about 13,430 about 3,344 (24.9%) about 2.23
Bear 15% Either Section 232 relaxed or exemptions widened after the midterms, or WTI below $65 from mid-2027 on a reopening with surplus; US index towards $2,400-2,600/t by late 2027 about 12,100 about 2,650 (21.9%) about 1.70
Severe (within bear) 5% Both together; Jefferies' $2,000/t; international -15% about 11,300 about 2,150 (19.0%) about 1.30

The probability-weighted FY2027 EBITDA is about $3,270m and EPS about $2.17. Consensus is $3,053m median and $3,158m mean on EBITDA, $2.06 median and $2.12 mean on EPS (about $2.10 and $2.16 on the true share count). My base is 6% above the mean and 9.5% above the median; the expected value is on the mean. The Consumer should read this as: the likeliest single path is above consensus, the median is stale (several brokers last updated in August with an index of $2,500-2,600/t), and the downside tails are fat enough that the expected value is not far above where the better brokers already sit.

Why the skew turns. Three pieces of evidence, each dated. First, the October move itself: the largest monthly dollar gain since July 2008, when prices peaked weeks before the rig count halved; Redburn's own marker for cyclical peaks is a sharp fall in the distributor price-outlook index and a rise in expected oversupply, neither of which has yet appeared. Second, imports: seamless imports rose 48% month on month in August even as welded fell 44%; Jefferies' 2018 lesson is that when the US premium over world prices is wide enough the arbitrage pays the tariff (US HRC at $1,380/t against a tariff-inclusive import parity of $900-920/t is such a gap); anti-dumping petitions against Austria, Taiwan and the UAE (margins alleged at 44-126%) will close some routes in 2027, but China-linked transhipment through 40 named countries remains. Third, capacity: Welspun's US welded mills (350kt HFIW done March 2026; 300kt LSAW due late 2026); Borusan's 950kt of US capacity; U.S. Steel's Fairfield quench-and-temper line (Q2 2029) and a $75m premium thread line; Tenaris's own Koppel upgrade. None of these breaks 2027; all of them cap 2028.

What narrows the cone. The Hormuz-oil link is a self-hedge: a reopening returns about $85m a quarter of EBITDA while a lower oil price trims 2028 US activity; a prolonged closure keeps Brent high and defers Gulf revenue. Operators' capital discipline (2027 budgets about 1% above consensus despite $100+ oil, Morgan Stanley 14 October) means neither tail in US activity is extreme in 2027. Tenaris's 12% Middle East EBITDA exposure and 90% domestic sourcing of US sales limit the two logistics tails. Net cash of $3.56bn removes any financing tail.

What widens it beyond the operating line. The effective tax rate (15% in the first half of 2026 against 21% assumed; Argentine deferred-tax swings), the associates (Ternium's dividend cut in April 2026; Usiminas's $521m loss in 2025) and interest income on a cash pile that will not now grow add about ±$0.08 of EPS noise. The Brazilian legal tail (about $90m indemnity; a possible contracting ban) is a one-off.

Predictability, stated plainly. The chance that FY2027 EPS lands within 10% of my revised base is about 55%; within 10% of consensus median, about 40%, mostly because the median is low. The chance that the next two quarterly prints (November and February) beat consensus EBITDA is about 70%. The chance that FY2028 EPS lands within 10% of either my base or consensus is below 40%. A memo that states these three numbers has described the cone correctly.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C+634th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Helpful enough to monitor the case well, because the two drivers that matter most are published monthly by third parties and the company's own quarterly disclosure covers the rest. Tenaris discloses quarterly tube shipments in tonnes, from which average selling price per tonne follows; Tubes revenue by region (North America, South America, Europe, Middle East and Africa, Asia Pacific); segment EBITDA for Tubes and Other; operating working capital and its days; net cash on its own definition; and half-year sales and EBITDA guidance with its assumptions stated (Hormuz closed through 2026; fourth-quarter volume above 1mt; third quarter seasonally lower). What it does not disclose is the split of price between the US and international contracts, the tariff cost after mitigation except in call commentary ($110-120m a quarter, 7 May 2026), the value of Gulf deferrals (the $130m figure is Redburn's and Jefferies' reconstruction) and any working-capital or capital-return target. For a six-month monitoring window those gaps are tolerable, because the external series lead the company's numbers by one to two quarters.

The monitoring set, in order of lead time.

Company disclosure events in the window. 4 November 2026 (Q3; look for the fourth-quarter price commentary, the tariff run-rate, Gulf deferrals cumulatively, working-capital days, any reference to the final dividend); Vallourec's Q3 shortly before (freight-cost recovery, Oman routing, US price drop-through of $150-200/t); 18 February 2027 (FY2026; the first-half 2027 outlook against the second half of 2026; the final dividend; capex guidance; any comment on the buyback authorisation); 12 May 2027 (AGM and Q1 2027). Peer reads: Hunting (OCTG pricing commentary; Kuwait tender timing), SBO (US land), Saipem and Subsea 7 (offshore scarcity, Middle East logistics), Ternium (associate income; Pesquería start-up February 2027).

Where disclosure is weakest for this thesis. (1) International pricing: formula contracts are undisclosed, so a softening in Saudi or North Sea spot tenders (Morgan Stanley's "Saudi Slows") would show only in the regional revenue line a quarter later. (2) The Gulf: no cumulative deferral figure, so the first set's warning line (cumulative deferrals above $200m by year-end; Middle East revenue not up 15-20% sequentially in Q3) has to be read off the regional sales table. (3) Capital return: no policy statement since March 2026; the only datapoint will be the February 2027 final. (4) Welded against seamless: not split, so the HRC squeeze can be inferred only from the gross margin. (5) The share count consensus uses: a broker-by-broker matter, not a company one.

Compared with peers. Vallourec gives quarterly EBITDA guidance ranges and EBITDA per tonne, which is more granular on margin; Tenaris gives regional revenue, which is more granular on mix. Neither discloses US-international price splits. Tenaris's stated guidance assumptions (Hormuz closed) are unusual and useful: they make the upside identifiable as "not in guidance".

Conclusion. The case can be tracked monthly on its two main variables without waiting for the company, and the company's quarterly disclosure is adequate for the rest. The gaps sit in the parts of the thesis that matter least for the next six months (international pricing, geothermal) and in the one that matters most for valuation (capital return), which is dated to February.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C-605th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The trace from management's statements to EPS is clear at group level and adequate at the operating line, but it stops at the company's door: Tenaris names the building blocks (volume, price direction, the Hormuz assumption, seasonality, capex, the margin corridor) without quantifying them by region or product, so the quantification has to be done from outside. The external transmission, from the US index, the rig count, Hormuz and Section 232 to EPS, is unusually clear and quantifiable for a cyclical, because the index is public, the lag is known and the operating leverage is stable. The weak link in both chains is the same: the 55-70% of revenue priced off undisclosed international formulas, and the noisy below-EBIT items. The milestones the memo should track are therefore few and specific.

The internal trace: what management says, and what it implies.

The group arithmetic is straightforward: EBITDA less depreciation of about $610-630m gives EBIT; plus net financial items and associates of about $220-290m gives pre-tax profit; tax at 17.5-21%; minorities of about $65m; 1,009.6m shares. The Consumer can rebuild any quarter from four numbers (tonnes, price per tonne, margin, tax rate).

The external transmission, quantified.

  1. US index to EPS. 30-43% of sales reprice off PipeLogix with a one-to-two-quarter lag at a stable premium of about 34%. 10% on the index is $450-560m of revenue and $360-450m of EBITDA at full drop-through, $0.28-0.35 of EPS; in the first year 55-65% reaches EBITDA because scrap and billet costs follow with an 80% correlation and a lag of up to 12 months. This is the clearest transmission in the case and the memo's central sensitivity.
  2. Rig count and intensity to volume. Short-cycle land volume is about 82% correlated with the rig count (the Risks report); pipe per rig rises 3-4% a year; Rig Direct adds share. 1% of group volume is about $60m of EBITDA and $0.045 of EPS.
  3. Hormuz to revenue and cost. $120-130m a quarter of deferred premium revenue (30-40kt) at 55-65% drop-through including about $30m a quarter of freight; 70-90 days from navigability to invoice. Reopening also lowers Brent, which reaches US rigs with a lag of about 25 weeks (Morgan Stanley) and then only through the next budget round.
  4. Section 232 to both sides. Price support (imports -34-40%; the full-customs-value rule since April 2026) and a cost of $110-150m a quarter on imported billets and bars; mitigation saves perhaps a third because domestic bars carry protected prices. A relaxation removes price within one to two quarters and cost within two (140 days of inventory), so the net first-year effect is negative by $250-600m of EBITDA.
  5. Hot-rolled coil to welded margin. About $350/t of ERW margin compression, $35-40m a quarter, until the welded index catches up (welded +33% year to date is doing so); the Nucor Hickman renewal in December 2026 sets the 2027 base.
  6. Below EBIT. 100 basis points of dollar rates is about $0.03 of EPS; the tax rate swings with the Argentine peso (15% in the first half of 2026 against 21% assumed); associates about $95m a year but cyclical. These do not transmit from the value chain; they are noise around it.

Where the trace is unclear. International formula pricing (Middle East, offshore, Argentina) is reported only as regional revenue a quarter late; a softening there shows up as a mix effect that cannot be separated from volume. The company does not give the price-volume-mix bridge that Vallourec's EBITDA-per-tonne disclosure approximates. And the capital-return trace runs to total shareholder return, not to EPS, which matters because the model's return at the guideline multiple is dominated by the multiple, not by earnings (the summary attributes 38.8 points of the 67.6% to the multiple and 15.9 to earnings).

Milestones and measures for the memo, in one list. Q4 2026 tonnes above 1mt and revenue per tonne up 3-5% on Q2; Q4 EBITDA margin above 23.5%; the first-half 2027 outlook above the second half of 2026; a final dividend near $1.10-1.20 or near $0.60-0.75; the tariff run-rate below $110m a quarter; the Middle East revenue line recovering 15-20% sequentially once transits resume; the PipeLogix seamless index holding above $3,000/t through the first quarter of 2027; imports staying below about 120kt a month.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

A421st of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Sentiment risk is elevated. The stock has re-rated, it is the outlier in its cohort, and the market regime is risk-off. Several outside events could cut 15-25% from the share price without any change to Tenaris's earnings.

  1. Cohort mean-reversion. Tenaris is up 73.8% in 12 months against 8-15% for its tubular peers, which are down over three months. It trades at a 24-month-forward P/E of 13.26x (89th percentile of five years) against Vallourec's 8.1x on a similar margin. The Wisdom Library's "outlier-to-cohort relative-strength fade" and "extended trend = banked upside" both apply.
  2. An energy ceasefire or Hormuz reopening. The sector digest names an energy ceasefire hitting 2027 budgets as the main risk to the services theme. Oil below $85 removes the Fitness for Fighting energy rationale. Energy services have run 42-65% in 12 months, so a sector unwind is plausible.
  3. Market regime. Risk-off, defensives leading, and the Momentum tilt moved to Underweight in the week of 23 October 2026, so high-momentum cyclicals are where selling falls.
  4. Peer events. Vallourec's third-quarter print and Bernstein's "last stop in Hormuz" view. The Saipem and Subsea 7 decision by November 2026. Monthly US import data (the 81% month-on-month welded import rise in July). PipeLogix monthly prints. Any sign that distributors are restocking.
  5. US trade politics around the 3 November 2026 midterms.
  6. Capital-return disappointment repeated at the February 2027 results, if no buyback or special.
  7. Governance headlines. Free-float compression and Milan delisting talk (Bernstein); Techint share sales; Argentine political conflicts.
  8. Listing country. Italian spread stress (France spilling over into Italy) touches the Milan listing but not the revenue. It is a flows risk only.

Offsets: short interest is extremely low, options show only hedging, the sell-side is mostly Hold (14% Buy), so the stock is not crowded by the sell-side, and the net-cash balance sheet limits fundamental fear. The danger is valuation-and-momentum, not leverage.

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?B+723rd of 25full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

C649th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Tenaris rarely upgrades guidance explicitly. It guides conservatively, half a year at a time, and lets results do the upgrading. Upgrades come as beats followed by upward consensus revisions, not as raised targets. When outside shocks arrive, guidance is trimmed promptly and with stated assumptions.

Jefferies says management has "consistently set conservative operational targets that understate financial performance" under both Rocca and Podskubka, and that "strong quarterly results regularly trigger upward revisions in consensus full-year EBITDA". Redburn counts EPS beats against consensus in 18 of 21 quarters. Guidance excluded any Hormuz resolution, a pattern of building in bad news. This fits the Wisdom Library's "sandbagging precision management" archetype. The new CEO's first guidance (May 2026) was constructive and the August guidance was cautious, so his own style is not yet established. For the model: the guided path is a floor more often than a midpoint, and upgrades arrive through the reported quarters.

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

A751st of 25

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: The record is excellent over one, three and five years, including in adverse conditions.

  • Last year. Fourth quarter 2025: EBITDA margin 23.9% against 22.6% consensus. First quarter 2026: EBITDA $735m against $707m (+4%). Second quarter 2026: revenue $2,967m against $2,936m (+1%), EBITDA $649m against $646-647m (in line), net income $477m against $434-436m (+9-10%, helped by financial results), EPS $0.47 against $0.38-0.41. FY2025's 24.2% margin sat within the guided corridor.
  • Last three to five years. EPS beats in 18 of 21 quarters (Redburn). Margins were kept in the 20-25% corridor through the 2024-25 downturn. FY2023 revenue of $14.9bn came in above conservative multi-year guidance. The main miss was the reported second quarter of 2024 (the $171m Usiminas provision; adjusted EPS $0.47).
  • Comparable conditions. In the 2024 downturn, with US prices falling and rigs declining, the comparable condition for a future down-leg, Tenaris still beat consensus while brokers cut (Morgan Stanley Underweight in early 2024; Redburn's cuts in August 2024). Bearish downgrades were right only about 33% of the time over a decade.

For the model: lean the delivery expectation slightly above consensus in normal conditions. In a sharp external shock, expect guidance to be trimmed promptly rather than missed.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

B+624th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The weight should differ sharply by horizon, because Tenaris's track record is a record of near-term delivery against conservative guidance, not of predicting the cycle.

  • FY2026 and the first half of 2027: high weight, lean up. Revenue and EBITDA of about 1-2% above the FactSet median, and free cash flow well above consensus.
  • Full-year FY2027: medium weight, lean up on operations. EBITDA about 6% above the median and about 3% above the mean. EPS is only about 2% above consensus on a like-for-like share count, because below-EBIT items and tax lean the other way.
  • FY2028 to FY2030: low weight. Do not lean up. In 2029 the risk is that a cyclical down-leg is deeper than my modest base case, so lean down there.

The direction of the lean is therefore up near term and down further out. This is the opposite of a "beat-and-raise" story that compounds.

The track record, and what it does and does not prove.

  • Near-term delivery is excellent. EPS beat consensus in 18 of the last 21 quarters (Redburn). In the first quarter of 2026, EBITDA was $735m against $707m expected (+4%). In the second quarter it was $649m against $647m (in line), with net income $477m against $434m (+10%, helped by financial results) and revenue +1%. The EBITDA margin stayed inside the 20-25% corridor through the 2024-25 downturn, while sector margins typically compress by 400-600 basis points. Over a decade, bearish downgrades were right only about a third of the time.
  • Why it beats. Guidance is structurally conservative. It is set half a year at a time, excludes favourable unknowns (it assumes Hormuz stays closed through 2026) and gives only qualitative credit for price increases that are already visible in the index. Because realised prices lag the index by one to two quarters, management can see the next two quarters' price well, and it guides below what it sees. This mechanism is observable and repeatable for FY2026 and the first half of 2027, which is why those periods deserve high weight.
  • What the record does not prove. Tenaris has no record of forecasting beyond six months, and it publishes no multi-year targets other than the margin corridor. Its delivery in downturns was a delivery of resilience: margins held at about 24%. It was not a delivery of growth. The beats in 2024 came while consensus was being cut. So the beat habit says the floor is reliable; it says nothing about whether the 2027-28 price leg lasts.
  • The misses. Working capital (143 days against an aim of below 130). Legal provisions (the $171m CSN/Usiminas charge in the second quarter of 2024). The capital-return pivot (the market was modelling a $1.2bn buyback run-rate and got none). These are exactly the items where my forecasts carry most uncertainty: the free cash flow path and the share count.

How I have applied the lean in the inputs, so the Consumer can see what is judgement and what is evidence.

Line My figure Consensus median Lean Basis
FY2026 revenue / EBITDA 12,187 / 2,778 11,892 / 2,732 +2.5% / +1.7% Guidance (about 12.1bn / 2.77bn) plus the usual small beat; consensus median is pulled down by stale estimates (named brokers sit at 12.06-12.41bn)
FY2026 free cash flow 1,770 1,320 +34% First half already $899m; second half needs only $420m for consensus; I assume modest working-capital absorption

FY2029 EBITDA 2,958 2,968 (two brokers) 0% Normalisation; one-to-two-broker coverage means there is no real consensus

Weighting guidance for the third set. I suggest these widths of the plausible range around my figures, as a starting point for testing:

  • FY2026 EBITDA: ±2%. One quarter remains, and the Q4 volume and price are largely contracted or indexed.
  • FY2027 EBITDA: -20% to +10%. The asymmetry comes from the policy and oil downside against an upside that is mostly timing (Hormuz).
  • FY2028-29 EBITDA: ±25%.
