Group judgement

Financial forecasts

C+576th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

My base case makes Tenaris a quality cyclical harvesting cash in a protected but self-limiting upcycle.

  • The next 18 months are the most predictable and the most favourable part of the forecast. Revenue, EBITDA and especially free cash flow are likely to come in above consensus, because guidance excludes the Gulf, realised prices lag a visible index, and the company's beat habit is mechanical. In my base, FY2026 EBITDA is $2.78bn and FY2027 is $3.24bn, about 6% above the median. Free cash flow is about $450m above consensus in FY2026.
  • Beyond that, the drivers are external. They are Section 232, distributor restocking and the Hormuz-oil link. The fast reinforcing loops behind the 2027 recovery turn balancing during 2028. Three US capacity additions are dated, restocking ends by construction, and operators are not spending their $100+ oil.
  • So the four-year path is a plateau and a dip, not a compounding line. EPS rises from $1.83 to about $2.17 by FY2028, then falls to about $1.95 in FY2029, with no share-count support.
  • The probability-weighted outcome for FY2027 sits close to consensus (EBITDA about $3.19bn against a $3.16bn mean). My above-consensus base is the likeliest path, but the downside tails (tariff relaxation, oil below $65) are larger in size than the upside (an earlier Gulf reopening).
  • The single largest unpriced variable is capital return. On my numbers net cash grows to about $5bn by 2030. A buyback restart, which the free-float arithmetic allows, or a special dividend would be the only source of per-share compounding.

For the third set: the forecasts deserve high weight to mid-2027 and low weight thereafter. For the fourth set: near-term upgrades are likely, but the market should not extrapolate them.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

D4715th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: Below operating profit, the next four years will be less helpful to EPS than the last four. The share count, which fell 14% from FY2022 to FY2025 and added about 4-7% a year to EPS in 2024-25, is flat in the base case. Net financial income plus associates settles at about $220m to $250m a year after an unusually good 2026 ($290m). The tax rate normalises at about 20.5-21% after a low 2026 (17.5%). As a result, net income converts from EBIT at about 0.83-0.84 in 2027-28, against about 0.90 in 2026. EPS growth from FY2025 to FY2028 (+5.9% a year in my base) comes entirely from operating profit.

Two findings matter for the fourth set. First, part of the apparent gap between my EPS and published consensus is a share-count artefact in consensus: on the true 1,009.6m shares, consensus FY2027 EPS is $2.10, not $2.06. Second, consensus non-operating income looks rich for FY2027 by about $40m, but in FY2028 consensus offsets its high non-operating income ($345m) with a high tax-plus-minorities rate (26%). My below-EBIT conversion in FY2028 is therefore about the same as consensus.

Free cash flow before dividends will be lower than the FY2023-25 average (about $2.1bn a year against $2.3bn including the FY2023 release), but steadier. Dividends rise to about $1.4bn to $1.6bn a year, and cash accumulates because no buyback is assumed.

Buybacks: why the base case has none, and what a restart would do.

  • Evidence. The board terminated the buyback early in March 2026, citing volatility. It doubled the interim dividend to $0.59 in August with no new programme. The $1.2bn authorisation renewed in 2025 is largely unused.
  • The free-float arithmetic (P-02) shows the constraint limits pace, not possibility.
  • My probability of some buyback being announced by the end of 2027 is about 35-45%, most likely at the February 2027 results or the May 2027 meeting. That is below 50%, so it is not in the base case.
  • Effect if restarted. At $500m a year from mid-2027, EPS is about +0.5% in FY2027, +1.5% in FY2028 and +2.5% by FY2029. That is small, because cash forgone earns about 3.5% before tax against an earnings yield of about 7%.
  • Bernstein's expectation of $8.3bn to $11bn of distributions through 2028 implies a restart or specials. My base distributes about $4.2bn in 2026-28, all through dividends. The Consumer should treat that gap as the size of the capital-return question.

Dividends. The FY2025 final paid in May 2026 was $0.60 ($606m). The FY2026 interim is $0.59, paid in November 2026, so $1.19 is paid in calendar 2026. FactSet's consensus of $1.09 for FY2026 appears understated against the declared amounts. I assume the final dividend rises to about $0.75 (May 2027) and the interim to about $0.65 (November 2027), so $1.40 is paid in 2027, then $1.55 in 2028-29 and $1.60 in 2030. That is about 70-76% of free cash flow after leases. Consensus has $1.50 for FY2027. Above about $1.50 to $1.60, dividends alone would distribute all surplus cash; below it, cash builds. A special dividend is a plausible alternative to buybacks for a controller that prefers cash.

Free cash flow against history. Before dividends: FY2022 $887m (working capital absorbed $2.1bn), FY2023 $3,893m, FY2024 $2,304m, FY2025 $2,094m (FactSet fixed-asset capex basis). My path is $1,770m, $1,900m, $2,100m, $2,110m and $1,960m. Lower than FY2023-25 because EBITDA is lower and capex higher (about $640m-650m in 2027-28 for Pennsylvania, Sault Ste. Marie and Artrom, against $568m-636m). Steadier because working capital moves little: 40.0% of revenue at end-2026, 38.5% in 2027 as Hormuz stock ships, 38% thereafter. If days returned from 143 to 128-130, a further $400m-500m would be released, upside not in the base. Lease payments are about $55m-60m a year. Acquisitions are Artrom (about $100m, closing in the fourth quarter of 2026) plus about $25m a year of tuck-ins.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set said consensus non-operating income "looks rich" across the years. For FY2026 it does not: first-half actuals of $164m make $288m achievable. For FY2027 it is rich by about $40m. For FY2028 the richness is cancelled by consensus's higher tax and minority charge. The first set's warning is right in direction for FY2027 only.

What would make me change the base case.

  • Up: a Hormuz reopening before the February 2027 results; a fourth-quarter 2026 EBITDA margin above about 24%; the first-half 2027 guide above the second half of 2026; a buyback announcement.
  • Down: Section 232 exemptions or a trade deal after the 3 November midterms; US imports rising for three months; distributor months of supply above six; US rigs below 550; Brent below $85, which removes the energy-tilt rationale; a failed Nucor coil renewal; deferrals turning into cancellations (cumulative deferrals above $200m by year end, or Middle East revenue not up 15-20% sequentially in the third quarter, the first set's warning line).

Judgements I hold differently from the first set, in one place.

  1. Fixed cash costs are $2.0bn to $3.0bn and volume leverage is 1.7x to 2.0x, not $3bn and 2.0x.
  2. Free float limits the pace of buybacks, not their possibility. About $2.6bn or more of buybacks remain possible before the 75% threshold.
  3. Consensus non-operating income is rich only for FY2027. In FY2028 it is offset by a high tax and minorities assumption.
  4. FY2028 is not a coin-flip. Normalisation starting in 2028 and showing in 2029 is more likely, about 60:40.
  5. International prices are flat in nominal terms, not flat in real terms.
  6. Consensus FY2027-28 EPS is understated by about 2-3% purely because of the share count used.

C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D6212th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: There is meaningful scope, about $0.4bn to $0.8bn of EBITDA above FY2026 levels by FY2027-28 in a favourable case. It would come from a few big areas, all congruent with strategy, and peer precedent (Vallourec's margin restoration) shows margin recovery is achievable.

The big areas, roughly in order of size:

  1. US price drop-through (index +16-20% reaching the income statement with a lag): the largest, worth several hundred million dollars of EBITDA if it holds. This underlies the Redburn and Morgan Stanley FY2027 EBITDA of about $3.5bn to $3.6bn against consensus of $3.05bn.
  2. Hormuz normalisation. Recovery of about $130m a quarter of deferred premium revenue and lower freight, worth up to about $80m to $90m of EBITDA a quarter by the second-quarter 2026 arithmetic, plus a one-off catch-up.
  3. Tariff mitigation (C-77).
  4. Offshore mix (Suriname, Guyana, Brazil, the Black Sea): higher-margin premium and coated products.
  5. Cash outputs. A working-capital release (about $0.4bn to $0.5bn) and restarted buybacks (EPS +3-5% a year at the 2024-25 pace).

The smaller areas are digital services, Artrom, new energy, automation and power costs. Peer precedent: Vallourec lifted EBITDA margins from low single digits to above 20% for 13 quarters by restructuring, but that was a self-help turnaround. Tenaris's improvement is a cyclical and logistics recovery and depends more on outside conditions. Congruence: all are inside the current strategy (Rig Direct, regionalisation, offshore). The second set should weigh item 1 by the durability of tariffs and item 2 by Hormuz timing, as both are outside management's control.

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

C629th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Over the last twelve months the core growth engine has not decelerated. It has stopped falling and is starting to re-accelerate, although revenue growth is still well below Tenaris's long-run normal rate. The input forces (US price, offshore spending) are accelerating, not slowing. The one input that slowed, the US rig count, has turned.

  • Revenue. Trailing twelve months to June 2026: $12,040m, against $11,768m for the prior twelve months, +2.3%. By quarter, year on year: +2.1% (third quarter 2025), +5.3%, +6.1%, then -3.9% (second quarter 2026, Hormuz deferral). The long-run normal rate is about 6-7% a year (revenue grew from $2.8bn in 2003 to $12.0bn in 2025, about 6.8% a year), so current growth is below normal. That is because the business is at the trough of a pricing cycle, not because of structural decay.
  • Input forces. The US seamless index is up 16% this year. Offshore capex steps up in 2027. US footage and pipe intensity are growing. The US rig count fell about 7% year on year before turning up about 10% this year. North American sales in the first half of 2026 rose about 12% ($3,050m against $2,725m). The slowing input is South American onshore (Colombia, Ecuador; South American sales down to $1,205m in the first half of 2026 on lower pipeline invoicing). The Middle East is deferred.

The Risks report's "false friend" claim, that spot price rises mask decaying volume, is contradicted by the North American sales growth and the fourth-quarter volume guidance. It is partly right for welded and for South America.

C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

B606th of 25

Judgement: Consensus models a gentle mid-cycle recovery. EBITDA margins return to about the 2019-25 average (about 23-24%), revenue grows modestly, and EPS rises about 5% a year with no buyback help. This is credible for FY2026, cautious for FY2027 given current price and offshore forces, and plausible but uncertain for FY2028, where the dispersion widens. Two line items look optimistic (non-operating income, the FY2027 dividend) and one looks conservative (FY2026 free cash flow).

Credibility given the change forces: the US seamless index is up 16-20%, which affects 30-43% of sales with a lag. Offshore spending steps up in 2027. Hormuz is excluded from guidance. So FY2027 consensus revenue of +6% leaves room for upside. The supply-side and policy fragility (C-31, C-81) justify FY2028 being flat to modestly up. The gap between consensus and the bull cases (Redburn and Morgan Stanley at about $3.5bn to $3.6bn of FY2027 EBITDA, against $3.05bn) is about price persistence, not about cost structure.

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C567th of 22

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: The net impact is a narrower, mid-cycle version of the last six years: average EBITDA of about $3.06bn a year in 2026-30 at an average margin of 23.4%. That compares with $2.53bn and 23.3% over 2019-25 (FactSet operating basis), which includes the 2020 trough, and with $3.60bn and 28% over the 2022-25 boom-and-fade. The path is much less volatile, and it carries no share-count tailwind. EPS rises from $1.83 (FY2025) to about $2.15-2.17 in FY2027-28, about 6% a year, slightly ahead of consensus's 5%. It then dips to about $1.95 in FY2029 and recovers to about $2.05 in FY2030. EPS stays below the FY2022-23 peaks ($2.23 and $3.32) throughout. Free cash flow before dividends averages about $1.97bn, below the FY2023-25 average and far above the FY2019-22 average. The balance sheet ends the period with about $5bn of net cash unless capital-return policy changes. Net, the forecasts are mildly positive for the investment case near term (2026-27 upside to consensus, strong cash) and neutral to negative further out (no compounding, cyclical fade, rising idle cash).

History against forecast.

Line FY2019-25 range FY2022-25 average FY2026-30 forecast range FY2026-30 average
Revenue 5,147 to 14,869 12,784 12,187 to 13,554 13,104

What has changed structurally against the last six years, and how each nets out.

  1. Pricing regime. In 2022-23 the price spike came from a demand shock plus the exit of Russian supply. In 2025-27 it comes from tariffs plus restocking. The new regime is smaller and policy-fragile. Net effect: lower peak margins (24.5% against 32%).
  2. Cost base. It is semi-fixed and ratchets up in upturns. North American regionalisation adds $50m-100m of fixed cost but removes part of a $560m-600m tariff cost. Net effect: slightly positive by 2028 (+0.5 to 1.0 margin point), larger decremental margins in the next downturn.
  3. Relative position. Margin parity with Vallourec and share loss in Saudi Arabia and Argentine line pipe offset Rig Direct gains in the US and offshore. Net effect: volume growth of 2-4% a year, roughly the market plus pipe intensity.
  4. Gulf logistics. The 2026 drag of about $390m of revenue and about $250m of EBITDA (deferrals plus freight) reverses in 2027-28 in the base case. Net effect: positive for 2027-28 growth rates, neutral for levels.
  5. Below EBIT. No buybacks; more interest income; normal tax. Net effect: EPS grows more slowly than net income would have under the 2024-25 policy, by about 4-7 points a year.
  6. Cash. Steadier free cash flow, a rising dividend and rising idle cash. Net effect: return on equity falls to about 11%.

Skew and cone, for the third set. The next two to three quarters are skewed upwards: guidance excludes Hormuz and gives limited credit for lagged price. From FY2028 the skew turns downwards: the price leg is policy and restocking dependent, three US capacity additions are dated, and operators' budgets do not respond to high oil. The probability-weighted FY2027 EBITDA (P-10) is about $3.19bn, close to the consensus mean of $3.16bn. My above-consensus base case is therefore the more likely single path, but not a large expected-value gap. The tail scenarios (tariff relaxation, oil below $65) are why consensus sits where it does.

Forecasts and upgrades

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

A611st of 12

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Current guidance, consensus and the narrative reflect the direction of our assessment and not its near-term size or its medium-term shape. Guidance is conservative by construction (half a year at a time, Hormuz assumed closed, only qualitative credit for a price index already up 26% this year) and the company's upgrade mechanism is the next half-year outlook rather than a raised number; the next two events (4 November and 18 February) are about 70-80% likely to deliver a met-and-stepped-up outcome, and the February final dividend is about 60% likely to confirm that total distributions have been maintained through a doubled ordinary dividend rather than halved. Consensus is stale in its median (half the brokers last updated before the September and October price prints, several on a share count 2-3% too high), so FY2027 EBITDA and EPS are more likely to rise 7-10% than to fall over the next two events, with positive breadth and a ratings mix (14% Buy, Hold mean) that has room to follow. The narrative knows every fact in our case and few of the joins: the end-date of the restocking rent, the two-sided Hormuz-oil effect, the size of the dividend and the late-cycle meaning of October's record monthly gain are each in one or two houses' work or in none. All of that is favourable for an investment case over the next six to nine months. The gap closes and then reverses after mid-2027: the published bull cases carry the price leg into 2028 at $3,277-3,410/t where our path fades it, the FY2028 consensus is at best right and at risk from the top, and the first set's observation stands that the share price has already re-rated to the top decile of its five-year range on a multiple half of which is sector beta. The favourable gap is therefore real, dated and front-loaded; the memo should present it as an 18-month case whose exit is the second half of 2027, when guidance, estimates and narrative are all more likely to turn than to extend.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

B673rd of 12

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: Over the next 18 months there are seven earnings events. At the first two (4 November 2026 and 18 February 2027) guidance is more likely to be met and then set higher than to be reduced: I put the probability that the second-half 2026 guidance is met or beaten at about 80%, and the probability that the first-half 2027 outlook given in February is set above the second half of 2026 at about 70%, in line at 20%, below at 10%. The reason is mechanical. Tenaris guides half a year at a time from what it can already see in its order book, and what it can see is an index up 26% year to date with the largest monthly rise in 18 years in October, lean distributor inventories, imports down, a rig count at 603 and a Gulf assumption (closed) that can only be beaten. From the August 2027 outlook onwards the odds even out and then tilt down: the second-half 2027 outlook is about 45% in line, 35% below, 20% above, and the February 2028 outlook for the first half of 2028 is more likely to be below the second half of 2027 than above it, because by then the restocking will have ended, imports and new mills will have responded, and any post-reopening oil fall will be in operators' 2028 budgets. Tenaris does not raise guidance in the formal sense; it reiterates, beats and lets the next half-year guide step up. The memo should therefore read "raised" as "the next half guided above the last", which is the company's only form of upgrade. On the secondary question, the 20-25% margin corridor will not be raised even if 2027 prints at or above 25%, because management treats it as a through-cycle band, and no new mid-term target is likely; the one piece of medium-term "guidance" that is likely to be raised is the dividend, with the February 2027 final the decisive event.

Event by event.

Why the near-term skew is strong. Three things coincide. First, the lag: realised prices follow the index by one to two quarters, so the October move is largely a first-half 2027 event; management will see it in its Rig Direct order book before February. Second, the guidance convention: the August guide gave only qualitative credit for price and assumed Hormuz closed; Jefferies calls it "deliberately conservative", and the record is 18 beats in 21 quarters, with the fourth quarter of 2025 at 23.9% against 22.6% expected and the first quarter of 2026 at $735m against $707m. Third, the peer read-across: Vallourec guided its third quarter down (EBITDA $170-210m) on Middle East costs but said US land price rises of $150-200/t add $20-25m sequentially and that nothing has been cancelled; Bernstein calls Vallourec's third quarter "the last stop" of the Hormuz drag. The risk to the near-term skew is cost, not demand: HRC at $1,375-1,385/t, Nucor's repeated spot increases, the Hickman renewal and the fact that about 140 days of inventory carries today's input costs into the first half of 2027.

Why the odds turn in the second half of 2027. The same evidence that makes 2027 strong dates its end. Seamless imports rose 48% month on month in August at a US price far above world parity; Jefferies' 2018 note shows imports return when the arbitrage pays the tariff; Welspun's LSAW mill is due late 2026 and Borusan runs 950kt of US capacity; U.S. Steel's Fairfield line is dated 2029 but its $75m thread line is sooner. The IMF path for Hormuz (gradual from March 2027, normal by October) delivers Tenaris's Gulf catch-up in the second and third quarters of 2027, after which the Middle East line is flat. And Deutsche Bank's $74 Brent on a settlement would meet the operators' 2028 budget round in December 2027. Management's own habit then works in reverse: it trims promptly with stated assumptions, as it did in August 2026.

The secondary question. The 20-25% corridor has been in place since the 2024 capital markets day and was met in 2024 and 2025 and will be met in 2026 (about 22.8%). A 2027 print at or above 25% would not move it; management would call it the top of the cycle. The probability of a raised corridor is about 10%; of a new capital markets day with new targets under the new CEO, about 30%, with content likely to be continuity (Rig Direct, regionalisation, offshore, low-carbon products). The informal ambition "offshore momentum through 2028" is likely to be met on the evidence of subsea backlogs (about $16bn across contractors; Subsea 7 $13.6bn), though Bernstein's note that subsea capex peaks in 2028 caps it. The capital-return "guidance" is the one medium-term item with a good chance of being raised: a final dividend of $1.00-1.20 in February 2027, which I put at 60%, would double the annual payout to about $1.8bn and would be read by the market as a raised distribution policy; a final of $0.60-0.75 (40%) would confirm the first and second sets' lower path and disappoint.

For the memo (topic #4). Guidance is conservative, the next two events skew to met-and-stepped-up, the company's upgrade mechanism is the next half-year guide rather than a raised number, and the shape after mid-2027 turns against the stock. The favourable gap between our assessment and guidance is therefore concentrated in the next two events.

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

A641st of 12

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: FY2027 consensus is more likely to be upgraded than cut over the next two events, by about 7-10% on EBITDA and EPS by May 2027, with positive net breadth, because the median is stale and the October price data have not yet been modelled by half the brokers FactSet shows. FY2026 consensus moves up modestly (1-3%) on the fourth quarter. From the August 2027 results the direction reverses for FY2028: the brokers who have extrapolated the price leg (Redburn's $3,283/t steady through 2028; Morgan Stanley's $3,410/t) will trim, and the FY2028 median, which already embeds flat Tubes revenue, will be held or cut by 5-10%. Net breadth therefore runs positive to about May 2027, flattens over the summer and turns negative in the second half of 2027. For 18 months to three years out (FY2029), there is no consensus to speak of (two brokers on EBITDA, one on EPS); what exists shows a 6-8% fall and is more likely to be held or cut than raised.

Where consensus is now, and why it lags. FactSet median (25 October): FY2026 revenue $11,892m, EBITDA $2,732m, EPS $1.86; FY2027 $12,620m, $3,053m, $2.06 (mean $3,158m and $2.12); FY2028 $13,227m, $3,233m, $2.11 (mean $2.21). Twelve brokers on FY2026-27, ten on FY2028. The dispersion for FY2027 EBITDA runs from Oddo ($2,865m, August) and Zephirin ($2,915m) through Banca Akros, Equita, Intermonte, Intesa and TD Cowen ($3,016-3,118m, mostly dated August) to Jefferies ($3,207m, 29 September), Goldman Sachs ($3,375m), a restricted broker ($3,393m, 12 October), Morgan Stanley ($3,556m) and Redburn ($3,630m, 16% above consensus, EPS $2.60). The low half of the range was last updated before the September and October index prints (+16% year to date at 1 October, +26% at 26 October) and before Jefferies' reading of inventories below average. The median is therefore a stale-estimate artefact more than a view: the brokers who have updated sit $3.2-3.6bn. The same is true of EPS: the median $2.06 is pulled down by brokers using 1,027-1,036m shares (consensus EPS is about 2-3% understated on the true 1,009.6m count) and by non-updated models.

The revision path so far. FactSet mean FY2027 EPS is up 1.3% over one month, 1.7% over three, 9.2% over six and 17.7% over twelve, from a series low of $1.52. FY2026 is flat to -1% over one to three months (Hormuz, receivables). FY2028 is -1.2% over three months and +6.0% over six. The dashboard's revision-momentum score is 13 and its sell-side momentum rating B; buy share 14%, up 8 points over three months; mean rating Hold; mean target $32.64 a share against $29.05. Momentum is positive but has flattened over the summer; the October price data arrived after most of the series.

Why the FY2027 upgrade is probable rather than possible. Four reasons. (1) The arithmetic of the lag: an index already 9% above what even the base case assumed, with 30-43% of sales repricing within one to two quarters, is worth $150-250m of 2027 EBITDA before cost offsets; the better-updated brokers already show it (Goldman, Morgan Stanley, Redburn). (2) The guidance mechanism: a first-half 2027 outlook above the second half of 2026 forces models that carry "flat on H2" to move. (3) The share-count artefact: as brokers roll models to the 1,009.6m count, EPS rises 2-3% with no change in earnings. (4) Peer confirmation: Vallourec's US price drop-through and Bernstein's "last stop" view mean the tubular analysts will be upgrading both names together. Against these: the cost side (HRC, scrap, Nucor) will make some brokers (Redburn's "Caveat COGS" style) hold margins down; and the two restricted or thin estimates (EPS $1.69-1.81) may not update at all.

Why FY2028 and beyond are more likely to be cut than raised after mid-2027. The FY2028 median already assumes flat Tubes revenue ($11,787m to $11,796m) and a 24.4% margin, which is a plateau; the upside names (Morgan Stanley $3,507m, the restricted broker $3,798m, Goldman $3,487m) assume the price leg persists, and the evidence on imports, inventories and capacity says it fades. My revised FY2028 EBITDA of about $3.24bn is on the median; the risk to the median is from the top. For FY2029 the two brokers show EBITDA of $2,968m (median) and EPS of $1.98; my $2.85bn and $1.85 are below them; the memo should not call the FY2029 figures "consensus".

Breadth and the ratings mix. The 14% buy share and Hold mean are unusual for a stock up 74% in a year, and they are the clearest sign that the sell-side has not caught up with the price: the ratings are valuation-constrained (targets $25-39 a share against $29) rather than earnings-constrained. Upgrades of estimates will come faster than upgrades of ratings. Expect the buy share to rise into the first quarter of 2027 as targets follow estimates, and expect it to be the first thing to reverse once the price fade shows.

For the memo (topics #7 and #8). Momentum in sell-side estimates is positive and about to accelerate for FY2027 (a 7-10% upgrade cycle over two events, with positive breadth), is neutral for FY2028 and will turn negative for FY2028 in the second half of 2027. The dividend consensus ($1.09 and $1.50) is the single line most likely to be revised sharply upward in February. Vallourec's revisions will move with Tenaris's; the offshore names' (Saipem, Subsea 7, TGS) will move with oil.

11d

Current multiple

D-0748
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D486th of 12

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: The share price moves about 40% with its dashboard peers and about 60% on its own, and the proportion has swung widely: the peer basket explained 48-51% of Tenaris's daily variance from April to September 2025 (the tariff and oil-price period), only 21-26% from March to August 2026 (when the Hormuz deferrals, the buyback termination and the CEO succession made the story company-specific), and 40% again by October 2026 as the energy-services complex and Tenaris re-coupled. The multiple is correspondingly mixed: Tenaris trades like an offshore energy-services stock on valuation (24-month-forward P/E 13.26x against Saipem 12.6x, Subsea 7 12.3x, GTT 15.4x) and nothing like its tubular peers (Vallourec 8.1x, Hunting 10.1x, SBO 7.5x), so the level of the multiple is set by the sector's scarcity re-rating while its premium to the tubular cohort is company-specific. Roughly: half the multiple is industry-driven and half company-driven. For predictability this is a middling result. It means delivery is rewarded (60% idiosyncratic), but a sector de-rating on a lower oil price would take the stock down with a beta of about 1.1 regardless of delivery, and the company-specific half rests on two narratives (tariff winner; cash-rich dividend payer) that have dated tests in November and February.

The co-movement data (daily returns, 126-day windows, equal-weighted basket of 19 dashboard peers).

Window end Correlation Beta R-squared
Nov 2024 to Mar 2025 0.46-0.55 0.68-0.83 21-31%
Apr to Sep 2025 0.68-0.72 0.98-1.04 47-51%
Oct 2025 to Feb 2026 0.54-0.61 1.03-1.11 29-37%
Mar to Aug 2026 0.46-0.51 0.79-0.92 21-26%
Sep to Oct 2026 0.57-0.63 1.00-1.09 33-40%

Pairwise, the highest correlations are with Vallourec (0.59; R-squared 35%), Saipem (0.55), SBO (0.52), Hunting (0.49), Subsea 7 (0.48) and Technip Energies (0.46). The onshore and tubular names (Vallourec, SBO, Hunting) and the offshore majors (Saipem, Subsea 7) each explain 20-35% of Tenaris's variance; the engineering and shipping names (Elecnor, Tecnicas Reunidas, Rosetti Marino, Nekkar) explain almost none. Tenaris is a hybrid of the two energy-services groups, which is exactly its revenue mix (US land plus offshore).

Returns tell the same story from the other side. Over six months Tenaris fell 1.7% while the basket fell 8.6%: idiosyncratic outperformance during the Hormuz drag, which is the market rewarding the margin floor and the dividend. Over twelve months Tenaris rose 73.8%; its tubular peers rose 5-15% (Vallourec +14.8%, Hunting +15.0%, SBO +4.6%) while the offshore scarcity names rose 56-102% (Saipem +102%, TGS +83%, Subsea 7 +80%, Aker Solutions +67%, DOF +66%, SBM +62%, Odfjell Drilling +56%). The stock's run therefore belongs to the offshore scarcity cohort, not the tubular one. The first set's "outlier to cohort" warning is right if the cohort is tubulars and wrong if it is energy services; the market has chosen the second cohort because of Tenaris's net cash, its 20% offshore mix and its position as the tariff winner.

The multiple's own position. 24-month-forward P/E 13.26x, the 89th percentile of five years (median 9.46x; range 5.98x-16.03x) and the 80th of two years; the dashboard's current P/E 13.0x is at the 55th percentile of ten years, and the model's two-year-forward P/E is 12.9x against a ten-year median of 12.2x. On EV/EBITDA, about 8.0-8.4x FY2027 consensus (9.3x on Redburn's figures in May). Vallourec is at 5.2x 2026 EV/EBITDA against Tenaris's 8.4x (Bernstein) at similar margins; the gap is explained by net cash (about 12-15% of market capitalisation), the dividend, Rig Direct, liquidity and the US tariff position, and by Vallourec's ArcelorMittal overhang. That premium is company-driven and durable while those features hold; it is also where a capital-return disappointment or a tariff reversal would bite first.

What this means for the forecast's predictability and for the memo's return calculation. First, because 60% of the price variance is idiosyncratic and the company delivers, the share price should respond to the November and February prints; this supports the fourth set's view that near-term upgrades will be rewarded. Second, the 40% that is sector-driven is tied to the oil price through the offshore cohort, and the Hormuz-oil link makes the most likely fundamental positive for Tenaris (a reopening) a negative for that cohort's multiple; the memo should expect the stock to lag its own earnings in that event. Third, a caution on the model's guideline fair multiple of 23.24x "reached at the 18-month column": that figure is the ten-year 90th-percentile band ('mania'); the stock has not traded above 16.03x in five years, its ten-year median is 12.2x, and its energy-services peers sit at 8-15x. The return of +67.6% to December 2027 in the summary is 38.8 points of multiple expansion and 15.9 of earnings, so the model's return depends on a re-rating that neither the co-movement data nor the peer set supports. The memo analyst sets the fair multiple; from the predictability standpoint, a company-driven half of the multiple that is already at the top decile of five years is a source of downside variance, not of predictability.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the eleven ratings page uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

B+582nd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: In OCTG, share-price outperformance has come from being early in a pricing upcycle, and underperformance from holding into the pricing peak. Tenaris is now mid-upcycle on price with its share price already re-rated, so it fits the late-early or mid stage of the pattern rather than the early stage.

The patterns:

  1. Share prices follow the US price index and EPS revisions, which lead the income statement by one to two quarters. Weekly Baker Hughes rig counts and monthly US import data are the best leading indicators; the Risks report finds 82% correlation between rig counts and short-cycle land volume. PipeLogix lags actual pricing by one to two quarters. Tubular stocks peak when distributor inventories rebuild and imports return, often while reported earnings are still rising.
  2. The quality cyclical outperforms in downturns and lags in sharp upturns. Tenaris's net cash and 20% margin floor protect it on the way down: the 2024 trough margin was about 24% against a 400-600 basis point compression typical in the sector. More geared peers (Vallourec, SBO) outperform in the early upturn. Consensus FY2027 EPS growth is +56% for Vallourec and +143% for SBO, against +11% for Tenaris.
  3. Supply exit plus customer capital discipline creates a pricing window (Wisdom Library: customers' capital discipline is the asset owners' scarcity). The window lasts as long as a new mill takes to build and a policy to change.
  4. Historically the bearish sell-side downgrades have rarely been right. The Risks report calculates about 33% accuracy for bearish downgrades over a decade (Morgan Stanley's early-2024 Underweight; Redburn's August 2024 cuts).

Where Tenaris fits today. The stock is up 73.8% over 12 months, 42.5% over nine months and 6.6% over three. It trades at a 24-month-forward P/E of 13.26x, the 89th percentile of five years. Its tubular peers have risen only 8-15% (the Sector digest: "the tubular makers other than Tenaris"), and Vallourec trades at 8.1x current P/E against Tenaris's 13.0x despite similar margins. The Wisdom Library warns of "extended trend = banked upside" and "outlier-to-cohort relative-strength fade". Tenaris's run is the outlier in a cohort that has not moved, which is a fragility signal unless fundamentals keep diverging. Fundamentals have diverged partly: Tenaris's FY2027 EPS is up 17.7% over 12 months, and it gains more from tariffs because of its US scale. They have not diverged enough to justify a roughly 60% P/E premium to Vallourec without Tenaris's cash, diversification and Rig Direct.

For the model and the later sets: assume that the share price's next leg needs earnings delivery (fourth-quarter 2026 drop-through, 2027 margins) rather than further re-rating.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

Brokers' published bear cases cluster in the same range.

The fair trough is about €15-17 because the realistic trough EBITDA in a US-led down-leg is $2.0bn to $2.3bn (the 20% margin floor on about $10.5bn to $11.5bn of revenue), and because trough-year multiples have historically sat around 6-7x EV/EBITDA. The net cash, about 15% of today's equity value, cushions the fall relative to peers.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

C5810th of 25

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative ahead has a clear, dated near-term staircase: the third-quarter print and fourth-quarter guide (4 November), the February 2027 results with first-half 2027 guidance and the capital-return decision, and a Hormuz reopening whenever it comes. Behind it sits a 2027 earnings recovery driven by US price drop-through and offshore growth. Most steps are well founded but already expected. The share price has re-rated about 74% in 12 months to the 89th percentile of its five-year multiple, so the staircase must deliver earnings above consensus, or a return of buybacks, to move the stock further. The two-sided dated risks are US trade policy around the November 2026 midterms and an oil price that may fall if Hormuz reopens. For the second set: the narrative supports a model whose upside case is "Hormuz reopens with tariffs intact" and whose downside case is "a tariff reversal with sub-$65 WTI".

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–70

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: Congruence is high. Across about 70 answers the two sets tell one story, and I find no contradiction that changes a conclusion. The story is: a quality cyclical whose next 18 months are carried by an externally created price rent (Section 232 plus distributor restocking), a dated logistics recovery (Hormuz) and an offshore ramp; whose floor comes from Rig Direct lock-in, formula contracts and net cash; and which has no growth compounder behind it, so the four-year path is a plateau and a dip rather than a line. The scores cluster between 50 and 66 at every level (external forces 60, industry structure 65, competitive advantage 66, economic strength 60, management 60, guidance 58, consensus 60, forecasts 57), which is the signature of "moderate and coherent" rather than of answers pulling in different directions. The second set's base case sits 1-6% above consensus, which is what the first set's "lean slightly above" asked for. Five points jar, and I resolve each; two more are resolved by evidence that has arrived since. One structural tension remains, and it is a feature of the case rather than an error.

The five points that jar, and their resolution.

  1. Pricing power against industry rent. C-17 and C-45 called Tenaris's price rises "real" and its pricing power "demonstrable and used"; P-04 called most of the 2025-27 US price gain "an industry rent from Section 232 and restocking, not Tenaris's own pricing power". Both are right about different things. Tenaris's own power shows in the stable premium to the index (about 34%), in international contracts that hold through cycles and in the roughly 20% margin floor. The industry's rent sets the level of the index. The model treats the level as fading from 2028 and the premium as stable. That is congruent, and it is the right split for the memo: the company's quality protects the floor; policy sets the ceiling.
  2. Fixed costs of $3bn against $2-3bn. C-75 derived about $3bn of fixed cash costs and 2.0x volume leverage; P-01 found Bernstein's $2.0bn (excluding depreciation) and used a range, with leverage of 1.7-2.0x and a 45% incremental margin. A disagreement in degree, resolved by the second set; no conclusion changes.
  3. FY2028 as a coin-flip against 60:40 normalisation. The second set's reading of the loops (restocking ends by construction, three dated US capacity additions, operators not spending high oil) resolves this in favour of normalisation starting in 2028. The October price data do not change the direction; they raise the peak from which normalisation starts, which is why I steepen 2028-29.
  4. The form of capital return. C-19 said a restart of buybacks or a special distribution was "likely in 2027"; P-02 and P-08 put a buyback below 50% and carried none; and both sets carried dividends ($1.2-1.3bn in 2026; $1.40 a share paid in 2027) that imply total distributions roughly halved from 2025's $2.26bn. That last point jars with the evidence the AlphaSense reports surface: Jefferies and Redburn read the doubled interim as a doubled total (about $1.80 a share), the CEO explained the choice as liquidity and simplicity, and the sister company Ternium has the same policy for the same reason. The resolution is that the controller's preferred form is a larger ordinary dividend, which satisfies the free-float constraint, the family's cash needs (Pesquería, Vaca Muerta) and the market's desire for cash return at once. I change the input. With it, the first set's "likely in 2027" and the second set's "no buyback" are both true.
  5. The Hormuz-oil link. C-07 and C-09 said to run reopening and a lower oil price jointly; P-10's base takes the Gulf return from the third quarter of 2027 without any change to US activity. This is defensible for 2027 and not for 2028: operators set their 2027 budgets between December 2026 and February 2027 at $100+ Brent, before any March 2027 reopening, so a post-reopening fall in oil (Deutsche Bank's $74) reaches US rigs in the 2028 budget round. The second set's 2029 dip and my steeper 2028-29 carry that effect; the memo should say so explicitly rather than leave the asymmetry implicit.

Two points resolved by new evidence. The first set flagged a conflict between distributor indices weakening and Morgan Stanley's "inventories below average" (C-33); the latest data (Jefferies, 26 October; Morgan Stanley, 15 July) settle it in favour of lean inventories and falling imports. The first set called working capital a deteriorating standard (C-14); the quarterly history (145 days at end-2024) shows 143 is within the recent range.

The structural tension that remains. Management's delivery record scores high (C-61 72; C-69 75; beats in 18 of 21 quarters), while the forecasts score modestly (P-06 62; P-10 57). P-06 reconciles the two by horizon: the beat habit is a statement about the floor (conservative half-year guidance, prices visible one to two quarters ahead), not about the cycle. This is the single most important reconciliation for the memo, because it tells the Consumer where to trust the forecasts (to mid-2027) and where not to (2028 onwards), and it is the logic behind the fourth set's answers.

Why congruence matters for predictability. Congruent answers mean the forecast error is concentrated in a few known variables rather than spread across unresolved disagreements. Here it is concentrated in the US index, Hormuz timing and trade policy (D-03), all of which are observable monthly. That is the useful kind of uncertainty.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

A581st of 12

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: The sell-side narrative is comprehensive on the facts and incomplete on the joins. Every covering house discusses the US price surge, the two-sided tariff, the $130m Hormuz deferral, Vallourec's catch-up and the capital-return pivot; most discuss working capital and tariff mitigation. What almost nobody articulates is the four connections that make our assessment different: that the price gain is an industry rent that ends with restocking, and when; that a Hormuz reopening lowers the oil price that sets 2028 US budgets; that the doubled interim implies a doubled annual dividend (two of five houses do say this; the FactSet dividend consensus does not); and that the share count in several models is stale. The medium-term dynamics (the fixed-cost ratchet and 70% decremental in a price downturn; the new US and Middle East capacity dated 2027-29; the sustainability of financial income once the dividend absorbs cash; the tax-rate volatility) are discussed by one house each or by none. Investor commentary outside the sell-side could not be found by AlphaSense (T-I2 returned nothing from Twitter or similar); the proxies we have, a 14% buy share, a Hold mean rating, very low short interest and options showing only hedging, describe a stock owned for its quality and yield rather than debated. For the next 18 months the market knows the direction of our view (near-term beats) but not its size; for 18 months to three years out the market's bulls extrapolate the price leg where we fade it, and its bears (Oddo, Zephirin) do not believe the 2027 step-up at all.

Coverage. T-I2 identifies five houses in AlphaSense (Jefferies, Morgan Stanley, Bernstein, Rothschild & Co Redburn, iM Securities) and covers all five from their own documents. FactSet shows thirteen contributing brokers, so the AlphaSense set is about 40% of the covering universe by count but includes the four houses that write the sector's reference research. Views: Jefferies Buy (€32; its FY2027 EBITDA $3,207m), Bernstein Outperform (€29; reported EPS $1.96 and $1.93, which assumes a flat 2027), Morgan Stanley Equal-weight (upgraded from Underweight on 15 July 2026; $62 per ADS base, $30 bear; FY2027 about 10% above consensus), Redburn Neutral (€26; FY2027 EBITDA $3,630m and EPS $2.60, the highest, yet Neutral because "the market has already fully discounted" the recovery and the dividend pivot caps the multiple), iM Securities constructive. Barclays Overweight ($38.91), Goldman Hold, Intermonte Hold, Oddo Hold, Intesa Hold, Equita Hold.

Sector narrative that frames Tenaris. Energy-services investors are focused on offshore scarcity (Saipem-Subsea 7, "Saipem 7", 40% of the deepwater fleet; subsea backlogs about $16bn), on the Hormuz-driven oil price and on the US midterms. In that frame Tenaris is "the tariff winner with net cash and a yield", which is why its multiple sits with the offshore names (D-07). The risk-off regime (Momentum tilt Underweight in the week of 23 October) and Italian sovereign spreads are discussed at the market level, not in Tenaris research. Bernstein's 26 October read-across from Halliburton, Oceaneering and SLB ("the stars are gradually aligning" for a synchronised onshore and offshore upcycle) is the most bullish current framing and will be the one the market hears into the November print.

Investor commentary. AlphaSense holds none for Tenaris from Twitter or investor forums; T-I2 says so and found no recent broker coverage of SBO either. The indirect evidence: a 14% buy share and Hold mean rating against a 74% twelve-month gain; a relative-strength percentile of 88 with the price 8.4% above a rising 200-day average; very low short interest and hedging-only options (first set); three months of flat revision momentum before October. This is a stock that has been bought by generalist energy-services and yield investors on the offshore and tariff themes, is under-owned by the tubular specialists who rate it Hold on valuation, and is not a battleground. That configuration means a beat is rewarded (little is priced for the near-term upgrade cycle beyond what the better brokers show), and it also means the stock has no committed bulls to defend it when the price leg fades.

What the market knows and does not, by horizon.

  • Next 18 months. Known: the tariff-driven price rise, the Hormuz deferral and its reversal, the dividend pivot, Vallourec at parity, working capital. Partially known (two houses): the dividend at about $1.80; FY2027 EBITDA above $3.3bn. Not known: the size of the near-term beat implied by the October index against a median still carrying $2,500-2,850/t; the share-count artefact; the two-sided Hormuz-oil effect on 2028 budgets.
  • 18 months to three years. The bulls (Redburn, Morgan Stanley, Goldman) hold the price leg into 2028 at $3,277-3,410/t; the bears (Oddo, Zephirin) never let it arrive. Our view, a plateau in 2028 and a fade in 2029 from a higher 2027 peak, with a 70% decremental, is in nobody's published model. The capital-return question (dividend-only, cash no longer accumulating, no buyback) is understood by Redburn and Bernstein in different ways and not by the FactSet consensus.

For the memo (topics #7, #8 and #10). The gap between our assessment and the narrative is favourable for the next six months (a near-term upgrade cycle that only the better-updated houses have in their numbers and that the ratings mix has not caught up with), roughly neutral at six to 18 months (the market will have the 2027 numbers by then and will be debating the exit), and unfavourable for 18 to 36 months in the sense that the published bull cases are above our path. On the lifecycle question the narrative is in the "thesis broadening" phase (from tariff winner to offshore and dividend story) while our forecasts say the fundamental phase is "reinforcing into a 2027 peak"; the two will diverge in the second half of 2027.

Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 25 October 2026, 14:07 to 14:30 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Tenaris (TEN-IT): answers to the first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years). Money is in US dollars unless stated; Tenaris reports in dollars. "Company basis" means Tenaris's own reported figures; "FactSet basis" means the financials block; "brokers' basis" means FactSet consensus.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Reading notes: ten things to settle before building the model

These are reconciliations I made while reading the files. Each one changes a number the model will use.

  1. Both Earnings Reviews cover the same period, the second quarter of 2026 (three months to 30 June 2026, reported 5 August 2026), which is the latest period FactSet shows as reported. The file labelled "latest" (06, dated 2026-09-11) was written first. The file labelled "earlier, 2026 quarter 4" (07) says the fourth-quarter results are not out, reviews the same second quarter, and cites sources up to 23 October 2026, so it is the more recent of the two. Section 4 ("Financial delivery") of file 06 is about TS Tech, a Japanese car-seat maker. That is a research error and should be ignored. The second-quarter figures below come from file 07's results table and the financials block. Third-quarter results are due on 4 November 2026.
  2. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line has dividends taken off. Despite its label, the block's FY2019 to FY2025 free cash flow equals operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex less cash dividends paid. For FY2025 that is 2,662 - 568 - 900 = 1,194, against the 1,193 shown. FY2024 is 2,940 - 636 - 758 = 1,546 and FY2023 is 4,464 - 571 - 637 = 3,256. So the peer table's historical free-cash-flow-to-net-income ratios for TEN-IT (0.8x, 0.8x, 0.6x) are after dividends. The consensus years are not, so the two cannot be compared. Free cash flow before dividends (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex) was 3,893 in FY2023, 2,304 in FY2024 and 2,094 in FY2025. That is 99%, 113% and 108% of reported net income.
  3. Net cash has two definitions. The company reports net cash of $3,322m at end-2025 and $3,564m at end-June 2026. Those figures include non-current investments, mostly bonds, of about $1.2bn. FactSet's net cash (cash and short-term investments less total debt) is $2,430m and $2,336m on the same dates. FactSet's enterprise value of 27,434 counts only $2,783m of cash.
  4. The share count needs resolving. FactSet shows 1,011.8m basic shares outstanding at end-2025, down from 1,180.5m in FY2022 after buybacks. Consensus EPS divides net income by roughly 1,010m to 1,035m shares. FactSet's capital-structure snapshot instead uses 1,072.0m diluted shares, which gives the $31,141m market cap and the 27,434 enterprise value. On about 1,010m shares the market cap is about $29.4bn, enterprise value is about $25.6bn, and FY2027 EV/EBITDA is about 8.4x rather than 9.0x. Check this against the share register before modelling. The sources give 62,355,174 treasury shares cancelled in May 2026; one passage says 90.7m.
  5. Per-share units. The Milan ordinary share is at €25.93, about $29.05 at the implied rate of roughly 1.12 dollars per euro. One NYSE ADS equals two shares. The broker targets of $42 to $80, Morgan Stanley's $60 target and its $30 bear case are therefore per ADS; halve them for a per-share figure. FactSet's mean target of $32.64 is per share, about €29.1 and roughly 12% above the price.
  6. Dividend yield. The 2026 interim dividend was doubled to $0.59 per share, from about $0.29-0.30 for 2025 (the earlier figure is inferred from "doubled" and from Jefferies' late-2025 expectation of $0.30). Consensus dividend per share is $1.09 for FY2026 (counted by payment period), a yield of about 3.8%. Redburn's "6.8% annualised yield" and Bernstein's "11% distribution yield" do not fit these figures. The 11% almost certainly includes buybacks at the 2024-25 rate. Neither should be used without checking.
  7. The "$414m of dividends to Techint Holdings" in the first half of 2026 is the controlling holder's pro-rata share of the $606m dividend, about 64-68%. It is not a related-party leakage, and the Risks digest frames it misleadingly.
  8. Industry figures conflict between credible sources, so model the ranges. Tenaris's global OCTG share is 31% (Rystad, via Morgan Stanley), 27% (Redburn) or 35% of a $35bn tubes market (Bernstein). Premium connections: 37%. Premium seamless: Tenaris 40%, Vallourec 26%, Nippon Steel 18%. Capacity: 7.8mt (Bernstein) or 8.7mt (iM). The Section 232 tariff cost is $140m to $150m a quarter (Redburn, Bernstein), about $560m to $600m a year or about 20% of FY2025 EBITDA. Another citation gives $70m a quarter. The likely explanation is gross cost against cost after mitigation, or different dates, so the second set should model the gross cost and then each mitigation explicitly. Rig Direct covers 90% of Tenaris's own US sales, not 90% of the US market; the Vallourec digest gets this wrong.
  9. The sell-side revision file says it is for FY2027, but its consensus levels (EPS 1.89, EBITDA 2,819, sales 11,398) look like FY2026 or the next twelve months. Use its direction of revisions, not its levels. The block's own weekly EPS series by fiscal year is the cleaner read.
  10. The rating mix is mostly Hold. FactSet shows 14% Buy and a mean rating of Hold among the brokers it can see. A Refinitiv figure of 58% Overweight from August 2026 is out of date.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Steer for the second set: the load-bearing inputs the model should carry

This list condenses the answers above into the inputs that matter; it adds no new judgement.

  1. Revenue by pricing mechanism and region. US index-linked sales (about 30-43%, a one-to-two-quarter lag, the seamless index +16-20%); international contract sales (formula-based, flat real prices); offshore (growing, visible to 2028); Argentina (Vaca Muerta growth, exposed to liberalisation and import competition); Colombia and Ecuador weak.
  2. Hormuz as a timing variable linked to oil. About $130m a quarter of deferred revenue at about 65% EBITDA drop-through including logistics. Recovered over one to two quarters after reopening. Run it jointly with a lower oil price scenario.
  3. Section 232 as two lines. The US price premium (keep it while tariffs last), and the gross tariff cost of $140m to $150m a quarter, declining as domestic steel sourcing ramps. The policy-reversal scenario removes price faster than cost.
  4. Operating leverage. Fixed cash costs about $3bn and rising slightly with North American regionalisation; about 49% variable contribution; historical incremental and decremental EBITDA margins of about 40%, or about 70% when the change is all price.
  5. Margins. A trough of about 22-23% EBITDA in the second half of 2026, about 24-25% in FY2027 in the base case, a 20% floor in a downturn, and a 25% top of the corridor.
  6. Working capital at about 38-40% of revenue (143 days now; 128 days is about $0.4bn to $0.5bn of release).
  7. Below EBIT. Financial income on about $3.5bn of cash (rate-sensitive), weak associates (Ternium, Usiminas), a tax rate of 20-22%, and Argentine deferred-tax noise. Consensus non-operating income looks rich.
  8. Share count flat at about 1,005m to 1,012m (verify against the register), with no buybacks in the base case and a dated 2027 restart scenario. Dividends of about $1.2bn to $1.3bn a year.
  9. Capex about $550m to $600m, about equal to depreciation of about $610m. M&A limited to Artrom.
  10. Valuation anchors for the later sets. A 24-month-forward P/E of 13.26x (89th percentile of five years; ten-year median 12.2x); EV/EBITDA about 8.0-8.4x FY2027; a fair trough of about €15-17.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Tenaris S.A. (TEN-IT; NYSE ADS: TS), the global leader in seamless and welded steel pipe and related services for the oil and gas industry. Its segments are Tubes (OCTG casing and tubing, premium connections, line pipe and coated line pipe, sold largely through its Rig Direct supply-and-service model; about 95% of sales per company disclosure) and Other (sucker rods, coiled tubing, Argentine oilfield services and industrial pipe).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Vallourec (VK-FR: the closest premium seamless peer, with a fully restructured cost base after German mill closures and the "New Vallourec" plan); Nippon Steel and its subsidiary U.S. Steel (integrated steelmakers with US tubular mills; U.S. Steel idled tubular capacity in 2020); TMK (Russian, the number two OCTG producer globally; private and under sanctions); JFE Steel (Japanese premium seamless producer and Tenaris's 13Cr alloy supplier); Hunting PLC (HTG-GB: asset-light premium threader whose Titan unit shows extreme operating leverage); Schoeller-Bleckmann (SBO-AT: downhole equipment maker with steep revenue swings); Tubacex (TUB-ES: seamless stainless pipe, with cost programmes in Europe); Welspun Corp (welded and line pipe, expanding US capacity). Inputs: What is the split of Tenaris's cost of sales and selling, general and administrative expense between fixed and variable costs, and how did EBITDA move against revenue in each of 2015-16, 2019-20, 2021-23 and 2024-25? Bernstein estimates variable contribution at about 49% of the average selling price; is that still valid after 2025? How did the 2020 restructuring (plant idling and the fixed-cost savings programme announced at $220m a year) deliver against target and timing, and which cost lines came back in the 2021-23 upturn? What did the $200m annual cost-reduction programme target and deliver, over what period, and on which cost lines? How much fixed cost does the regionalisation of North American production add (extra shifts at Bay City; the Koppel finishing and heat-treatment restart; the CAD 306m Sault Ste. Marie modernisation), and at what US rig count or mill utilisation does fixed-cost absorption at these mills turn negative? What is Section 232's cost to Tenaris gross and after mitigation, per quarter, from mid-2025 to now? Redburn has cited $140m to $150m a quarter, and another citation gives $70m. How quickly is it falling as Tenaris moves to domestic steel bars (the Nucor Steel Memphis framework from January 2026) and its own steel? How long is the lag before steel and tariff cost changes reach cost of goods sold under Tenaris's inventory accounting, given operating working capital of 143 days at June 2026 (company)? Redburn's March 2026 note "Caveat COGS" addressed this. How much of second-quarter 2026 selling, general and administrative expense ($484m) is re-routing and freight from the Strait of Hormuz closure, and how fast does it reverse? How cuttable are labour costs, given unionisation, Argentine restrictions on lay-offs (the 2026 SIAT mediation) and wage indexation in Argentina and Brazil? For peers: what did Vallourec's closures and cost programme promise against what they delivered, and how long did they take? What did Hunting's EMEA restructuring deliver, and what explains Titan's EBITDA margin swing from 0.6% to 13.1%? Focus: How strongly Tenaris's EBITDA now responds to a change in volume and in US price, now that North American fixed costs have been added. How much of the cost base (tariff cost, logistics, labour, plant fixed costs) can realistically be removed or reduced by 2027-28, and on what timetable.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Vallourec (VK-FR: premium seamless rival with 100% locally made US steel, selling through distributors); Nippon Steel and U.S. Steel (integrated US competitor after the merger); JFE Steel (Japanese premium seamless); TMK (Russian volume player shut out of Western markets); SeAH Steel (Korean welded and seamless exporter to the US); Welspun Corp (Indian welded and line pipe; won an Argentine pipeline tender against Techint); the Saudi national champion formed by merging JESCO and AMTJ under the Public Investment Fund (local-content competitor for Aramco); Baosteel and TPCO (Chinese seamless exporters to the Middle East); Hunting PLC (premium connection pricing outside the integrated mills). Operating conditions: (1) a US onshore upcycle under Section 232 protection, with distributor restocking (2025-26); (2) a US onshore downturn with a falling rig count (as in 2015-16, 2019-20 and 2024); (3) removal or relaxation of US steel tariffs or anti-dumping orders, leading to an import surge; (4) steel input inflation (hot-rolled coil, scrap, tariffed billets and bars) with pass-through lags; (5) long-term national oil company contracts in the Middle East and Latin America during a logistics disruption (the Strait of Hormuz closure since March 2026); (6) a deepwater offshore upcycle that needs premium connections; (7) competitive tenders against low-cost Asian and Indian suppliers in liberalising markets (Argentina). Inputs: What share of Tenaris's revenue reprices off PipeLogix or other US price indices, and with what lag? Redburn has cited about 43% of global sales, and independent observers 30% to 40%. How has Tenaris's realised price compared with the US seamless index through cycles? Bernstein cites realised pricing 34% above the benchmark index; does that premium widen or compress in downturns? Which indices, lags, floors and caps govern pass-through in the major national oil company and international oil company contracts: ADNOC's $1.9bn contract extended by two years; Aramco; Petrobras; ExxonMobil's ten-year Stabroek supply contract? What happened to US OCTG prices and import volumes after earlier tariff changes: the 2018 Section 232 introduction, country quotas and exemptions, and the rise to 50% in June 2025 and to full customs value in April 2026? How has the US seamless-to-welded price spread behaved when imports rose? Where and how much price did Tenaris give back in 2015-16, 2019-20 and 2024? Vallourec reported a US seamless trough of about $2,110 a tonne in August 2024. What are US distributor inventories (months of supply) and distributor price indices now, and how much of 2026 demand is restocking rather than end use? Under Rig Direct, what is the record on renewals, customer losses and price renegotiation? Did Middle East customers accept freight and war-risk surcharges in 2026, given that Vallourec said it was negotiating recovery of these costs? How has Tenaris's price premium in Argentina changed since import barriers were lowered (the pipeline tender lost to Welspun)? What do customers, competitors and the sell-side now say about seamless OCTG pricing into 2027-28, and about Jefferies' and Morgan Stanley's published price paths? Focus: Whether Tenaris's 2025-26 US price gains reflect the company's own pricing power or an industry rent created by tariffs and restocking. How much realised price and margin Tenaris would keep in a US downturn, or after a removal of tariffs.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Several interacting loops (Rig Direct lock-in, localisation driven by tariffs, the distributor inventory cycle, capacity discipline, working-capital absorption, and the link between the Strait of Hormuz and the oil price) decide whether Tenaris's 2027-28 earnings path extends or normalises, and the model must choose between those paths. Peers: Vallourec (VK-FR: shares the same US price and capacity loops); Nippon Steel and U.S. Steel (potential capacity response in the US); Welspun Corp (adding US capacity in response to protected prices); the Saudi PIF national champion (local capacity response in the Middle East); Hunting PLC (independent threader exposed to the same inventory cycle); Subsea 7 and Saipem (offshore capacity scarcity, a parallel case of customers' capital discipline creating supplier pricing power). Inputs: (a) The Rig Direct installed base increasing switching costs, share and scale: is the US share gain continuing, and is the offshore extension (Suriname for TotalEnergies; Guyana for ExxonMobil) repeating it? (b) Tariffs reduce imports, raise domestic prices, prompt domestic investment by Tenaris, Welspun and others, and so add capacity that eventually relieves prices: how fast is new US OCTG capacity arriving? (c) US price rises trigger distributor restocking, which supports further price rises, then overstocking and price falls: where is the inventory cycle now? (d) A high oil price caused by the Hormuz closure raises operator budgets and activity; reopening could lower oil and activity: how did operators' 2027 budgets respond in previous oil swings? (e) Capacity exits (Vallourec Germany, U.S. Steel) create under-supply (Bernstein's estimate of a global seamless deficit, the first since 2013), higher prices and an incentive for new capacity in India, Saudi Arabia and Southeast Asia: how much is under construction? (f) Upcycles absorb working capital, which lowers free cash flow and may delay buybacks and the EPS support they gave: how did Tenaris's cash conversion behave in 2022? (g) Vallourec's margin catch-up: does it reinforce industry discipline or start a contest for share? Focus: Which of these loops dominate over 2027-28, and which leading indicators (imports, distributor months of supply, rig additions, new mill announcements) would show the US price loop turning from reinforcing to balancing.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Tenaris's EPS depends on a small number of separable inputs on which brokers diverge widely (FactSet FY2027 EPS estimates range from $1.81 to $2.50 per share, October 2026), and the buyback pause has removed share-count reduction as a source of EPS growth. Peers: Vallourec (VK-FR: same price, volume and tariff drivers); Hunting PLC (HTG-GB: OCTG and offshore volumes); Schoeller-Bleckmann (SBO-AT: US land activity sensitivity); Saipem (SPM-IT: Middle East logistics cost recovery after the Hormuz closure); Ternium (sister company and associate whose earnings flow into Tenaris's results). Inputs: OCTG and line pipe volumes by region for 2026-29 (US, Canada, Mexico, Argentina and Vaca Muerta, the Middle East, offshore Brazil, Guyana, Suriname and the Black Sea); realised price by region and product (seamless, welded, premium connections, coated line pipe); the Section 232 cost and how it is mitigated over time; the timing of shipment and invoicing of the deferred Middle East volumes once the Strait of Hormuz reopens (company guidance: within 70 to 90 days), and the associated logistics costs; hot-rolled coil and scrap costs, and the terms of the Nucor coil supply contract that expires in late 2026; financial income on net cash, which the company reported at $3,564m at end-June 2026, and its sensitivity to interest rates; associate income from Ternium and Usiminas, and any charges from the CSN indemnity litigation or the Confab claims in Brazil; the effective tax rate, including Argentine deferred-tax effects of peso devaluation; minorities; the share count after the May 2026 treasury share cancellation; the likelihood and size of a buyback restart, given the free-float constraint Bernstein has flagged for the Milan listing and the end of Techint's accelerated share disposal agreement in May 2026; dividend policy after the 2026 interim was doubled to $0.59 a share; capex; working capital days (143 at June 2026, against 128 a year earlier, per company disclosure). Focus: Which inputs explain the gap between consensus FY2027-28 EPS and the bull cases (Redburn's published path of EPS rising from $1.96 to $2.70 by 2028), and whether, when and in what form surplus cash will be returned to shareholders from 2027.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 25 October 2026, 23:16 to 23:29 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Tenaris (TEN-IT): answers to the second set of questions, the operating and financial model for FY2026 to FY2030

Written 2026-10-25 for the Investment Analyst answering the third set (predictability of the forecasts) and the fourth set (how far guidance, consensus and the market narrative reflect them). Money is in US dollars, millions unless stated. Tenaris reports in dollars.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

How to read the numbers in these answers

  1. Basis. My EBITDA figures are on the company's own reported basis (FY2025 $2,899m, 24.2% margin; first half of 2026 $1,384m, 22.8%). FactSet's operating EBITDA, which the model's history uses, runs slightly higher (FY2025 $2,911m; first half of 2026 $1,408m). The brokers' adjusted basis, which consensus uses, runs slightly lower (FY2025 $2,881m, 24.0%). Rule of thumb: brokers' basis is about 0.6% below company basis; FactSet operating is 0.4% to 1.7% above it. Reported EPS and the brokers' EPS were identical in FY2025 ($1.83).
  2. Share count. 1,009.6m shares are in issue since the cancellation of 62,355,174 treasury shares on 12 May 2026, with none left in treasury. I use 1,010m for FY2026 and 1,009.6m thereafter. Consensus EPS implies about 1,011m shares for FY2026, 1,027m for FY2027 and 1,036m for FY2028 (net income divided by EPS), so some brokers still use a stale or diluted count. On the true count, consensus EPS is about $1.86, $2.10 and $2.17, not $1.86, $2.06 and $2.11. That 2-3% is a modelling artefact, not an earnings difference, and the Consumer should not read it as upside.
  3. Free cash flow in my figures is operating cash flow less capital spending including intangibles, before lease payments, acquisitions and dividends. That matches the FactSet consensus definition. FactSet's historical "free cash flow" line deducts dividends and must not be compared with it (first set, reading note 2).
  4. Dividends per share are by payment period, as FactSet records them: a May final for the prior year plus a November interim.
  5. Middle East / Strait of Hormuz. My base case assumes the Strait reopens gradually in mid-2027, with Gulf revenue restored from the third quarter of 2027 and a partial catch-up of deferred shipments. That is a judgement, not a forecast I hold with confidence. It is the single largest timing assumption in the model, and P-10 shows what changes if it is wrong.

The shape in one sentence: a price-led recovery from a 2026 trough margin of 22.8% to 24.5% in 2027, a plateau in 2028 as price fades and volume and the Gulf carry the line, a cyclical dip in 2029 as the US protection-and-restocking price leg unwinds, and a mid-cycle 2030; earnings per share grow about 6% a year from FY2025 to FY2028 with no help from the share count, and cash accumulates to about $5bn because the base case returns cash only through dividends.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Notes on the inputs above.

  • The EBITDA margin is on the company's own reported basis (FY2025 24.2%; first half of 2026 22.8%).
  • "Net financial items" includes the share of results of associates (Ternium 11.46%, Usiminas, Techgen), because the form has no separate line for them. The financial result alone is about +150, +125, +135, +145 and +155. Associates are about +140 in FY2026 (first half +81), then about +95 a year.
  • Minorities are not a form line. I assume -70, -65, -65, -60 and -60.
  • Dividend per share is by payment period. FY2026 is the May 2026 final of $0.60 plus the November 2026 interim of $0.59. Later years assume a final of $0.75, $0.85, $0.85 and $0.90 and an interim of $0.65, $0.70, $0.70 and $0.70.
  • The -90 of buybacks in FY2026 was made in the first quarter, before the board terminated the programme. No buybacks are assumed afterwards.
  • Acquisitions in FY2026 include Artrom (€86m, about $100m), closing in the fourth quarter of 2026.
  • Shares in issue are 1,009.6m since the treasury cancellation of 12 May 2026; use about 1,010m as the FY2026 average.

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

Tenaris reports two segments, Tubes (about 95% of sales) and Other (sucker rods, coiled tubing, Argentine oilfield services, industrial pipe). Figures are my estimates on the company basis.
